# Arbitrary-Distance Quantum Error Correction with Gauss's Law for $\mathbb Z_2$ Lattice Gauge Theory

**arXiv ID**: 2608.00165v1
**Authors**: Neel S. Modi, Lento Nagano, Masazumi Honda, Nobuyuki Yoshioka, Christian W. Bauer
**Published**: 2026-07-31
**Categories**: hep-lat, hep-th, quant-ph
**Comments**: 46 pages, 13 figures
**HTML URL**: https://arxiv.org/html/2608.00165v1

## Abstract

It has previously been shown by Rajput, Roggero, and Wiebe that $\mathbb Z_2$ Gauss's law constraints can be used to build efficient quantum error-correcting codes (QECCs) that are robust against arbitrary single-qubit errors. In this work, we generalize the construction to be robust against arbitrary $t$-qubit errors, where $t$ is any positive integer. This includes a derivation of the optimal Gauss's law code within the considered family by minimizing the number of physical qubits required for a given code distance. Finally, we compare our codes against other efficient QECCs on metrics such as the number of physical qubits, the locality of the encoded Hamiltonian, and the logical error rate in the code capacity setting. Compared to using a domain-agnostic code for every lattice degree of freedom, we find that the Gauss's law code primarily excels at reducing the locality of the encoded Hamiltonian. Moreover, the physical qubit overhead is also reduced for $t \le 3$ (distance $d \le 7$).

## Full Text

Arbitrary-Distance Quantum Error Correction with Gauss’s Law for ℤ₂ Lattice Gauge Theory

## Abstract

It has previously been shown[67]thatℤ2\mathbb{Z}_{2}Gauss’s law constraints can be used to build efficient quantum error-correcting codes (QECCs) that are robust against arbitrary single-qubit errors. In this work, we generalize the construction to be robust against arbitrarytt-qubit errors, wherettis any positive integer. This includes a derivation of the optimal Gauss’s law code within the considered family by minimizing the number of physical qubits required for a given code distance. Finally, we compare our codes against other efficient QECCs on metrics such as the number of physical qubits, the locality of the encoded Hamiltonian, and the logical error rate in the code capacity setting.
Compared to using a domain-agnostic code for every lattice degree of freedom, we find that the Gauss’s law code primarily excels at reducing the locality of the encoded Hamiltonian. Moreover, the physical qubit overhead is also reduced fort≤3t\leq 3(distanced≤7d\leq 7).

## IIntroduction

Gauge theory describes fundamental interactions between elementary particles.
Studying the dynamics of a given gauge theory is relevant to understanding how non-perturbative phenomena such as confinement and string breaking emerge.
Lattice gauge theory (LGT) regulates the continuum gauge theory described by quantum field theory by putting it on the discretized lattice space(time), and allows us to investigate non-perturbative physics numerically[87,51,50].
While the conventional Monte Carlo approach has provided illuminating results, it suffers from the sign problem[83]when dealing with chemical potentials, topological terms, and real-time dynamics.
To overcome this problem, we have recently seen a growth of interest in Hamiltonian simulations of LGT using quantum computing (reviews of the many different approaches, both analogue and digital, can be found in Refs.[86,89,21,2,6,93,46,8]).

In the Hamiltonian setting, one typically works in Weyl (A0=0A_{0}=0) gauge.
In this gauge, Gauss’s law is not enforced through an equation of motion, but is instead enforced through constraints on the Hilbert space.
We can impose these constraints by completely or partly solving them at the kinematical level and/or fixing the gauge redundancy, and rewriting the Hamiltonian in terms of the remaining degrees of freedom[45,91,68,23,20,33,27,7,37].
This approach automatically guarantees gauge-invariant dynamics and can reduce the number of qubits. However, it may introduce longer-range or more complicated interactions in some formulations, which may cause increasing gate costs[39,57,91,20,33,34].
Forℤ2\mathbb{Z}_{2}gauge theories coupled to matter, this strategy can be used to eliminate the fermionic matter entirely and recast the model as a pure spin system[90,91,11,10].
The alternative is to retain the local, gauge-redundant Hamiltonian and either preserve Gauss’s law at the algorithmic level[55,58,79,3,70], suppress gauge-violating errors during the evolution[92,56,82,38,22], or mitigate their effects in measured observables through symmetry verification and postselection[78,4,9].
In this approach, one requires additional qubits to represent the enlarged Hilbert space, and one needs to protect the gauge symmetry from algorithmic or hardware errors.
In particular, taming the hardware error in a scalable way is also necessary in general to have reliable results in large-scale simulations.

Quantum error correction (QEC) is a general collection of techniques to protect quantum data (in the form oflogicalstates) by equipping it with redundancies (typically by usingphysicalstates in the larger Hilbert space)[71,76,13,49,61,32,81].
These techniques have recently moved from principle to practice: a superconducting surface-code memory has been operated below the error-correction threshold, with the logical error rate suppressed as the code distance is increased[1], which makes the scaling of code distance a concrete rather than an asymptotic concern.

In the stabilizer formalism[31], the space of logical states can be specified by the generators (stabilizers) of an Abelian group (stabilizer group)𝒮\mathcal{S}, via the requirementg​|ψ⟩=|ψ⟩g\ket{\psi}=\ket{\psi}for allg∈𝒮g\in\mathcal{S}.
Rajput, Roggero, and Wiebe constructed prototypical examples of LGT-specific stabilizer codes[67]by regarding Gauss’s law constraints inℤ2\mathbb{Z}_{2}LGT as stabilizers and incorporating them with repetition code stabilizers.
We refer to this as the RRW construction or RRW code.
It not only detects gauge-violating errors but can also be used to correct them using standard QEC protocols.
On top of that, it was shown that the RRW code uses fewer physical qubits compared with naively encoding every lattice variable independently with a domain-agnostic code (⟦5,1,3⟧\llbracket 5,1,3\rrbracketcode)[67].
Furthermore, Ref.[73]proposed how to perform fault-tolerant real-time evolution in the RRW code.
Ref.[62]performed memory and dynamics simulations to compare the performance of the RRW code and generic quantum error-correcting codes (QECCs) against bit-flip noise.
Other important results include extensions toℤN\mathbb{Z}_{N}LGT[74,84]and the lowest-level truncation ofSU​(2)\mathrm{SU}(2)LGT[88].
(See also[30,47,52,24,19,36,85,16,53,69,15]for other studies utilizing or formulating Gauss’s law for QEC.)
While these developments have enabled a better understanding of the relationship between Gauss’s law and QEC, the RRW construction inℤ2\mathbb{Z}_{2}LGT[67,73]focused on code distanced=3d=3, meaning it can only correct up to weight-one errors.
For quantum error correction to be useful, it is important to be able to suppress higher-weight errors, as well as to obtain the error threshold under which we can control the logical error rate.Figure 1:Summary of this paper. We depict the 1D example for simplicity, but we also give 2D results in the main text. (a) 1D lattice forℤ2\mathbb{Z}_{2}LGT. The square symbol is a link variable (gauge field) and the circle is a site variable (matter field). Each Gauss’s law constraint is a parity check between a site and two adjacent links, as highlighted in orange. (b) construction of the classical and quantum Gauss’s law code. Classical Gauss’s law duplicates sites and links with Gauss’s law to buildZZ-type stabilizers, and is concatenated withXX-type classical codes to yield a quantum Gauss’s law code with prescribed code distance. (c) Generic (domain-agnostic) QEC encoding used as a baseline for comparison. We encode each lattice site and link by using the⟦n,1,d⟧\llbracket n,1,d\rrbracketstabilizer code with minimalnn.Table 1:Summary of our resultsDescriptionLocationconstruction of classical Gauss’s law (GL) code with arbitraryddDef.III.1alternative construction of classical GL codeLem.III.2construction of quantum GL codeDef.III.4GL code with optimal encoding ratek/nk/n(1D)Thm.III.5GL code with optimal encoding ratek/nk/n(2D)Thm.III.6qubits/locality of GL and minimal-length code (1D)Tab.3, Tab.4qubits/locality of GL and minimal-length code (2D)Tab.5, Tab.6code capacity memory simulation of GL and minimal length code (1D)Figs.13and12

Our main results are summarized inTable1andFig.1.
We construct an explicit family of Gauss’s law codes with anarbitrarycode distance forℤ2\mathbb{Z}_{2}LGT coupled to matter, thereby extending the previous RRW construction (d=3d=3) (see Def.III.1and Def.III.4).
As shown inFig.1(b), the construction first duplicates each lattice site and/or link, and then builds aZZ-type stabilizer by combining Gauss’s law with duplications.
The total number of physical qubits thus depends on the number of duplications.
We obtain the explicit construction (i.e., duplication pattern) which minimizes the number of physical qubits for a given number of logical qubitskkand code distancedd(see Thm.III.5for the 1D case and Thm.III.6for the 2D case).
In other words, our codes have an optimal encoding ratek/nk/namong the family of Gauss’s law codes.
To assess the performance of Gauss’s law codes, the number of physical qubits and the number of qubits involved in each Hamiltonian term are evaluated for concrete examples ofℤ2\mathbb{Z}_{2}LGTs, which are then compared with baseline (domain-agnostic) QECCs (seeFig.1(c)).
The comparison results are given inTables3and4for the 1D case, andTables5and6for the 2D case.
As a first step toward threshold studies (seeFigs.13and12), we also confirm that we can suppress the logical error rate by increasing the code distance under code capacity setting.

This paper is organized as follows.
SectionIIexplains preliminaries needed for this work, including QEC (SectionII.1) and Hamiltonian LGTs withℤ2\mathbb{Z}_{2}gauge symmetry (SectionII.2).
Readers familiar with these topics can skip this section.
Useful notations forℤ2\mathbb{Z}_{2}LGTs are summarized inTable2.
Next, our theoretical results are given inSectionIII.
After reviewing the RRW results (d=3d=3)[67]inSectionIII.1, the arbitrary distance Gauss’s law code is constructed inSectionIII.2.
Furthermore, the optimal Gauss’s law code constructions for 1D and 2D cases are given inIII.3.SectionIVshows the comparison of code properties with baseline codes (minimal-length codes).
We compare the number of physical qubits and Hamiltonian locality inSectionIV.1, and the code capacity memory simulation is given inSectionIV.2.
Finally, we summarize our results and provide an outlook inSectionV.
Additional details related to our results are provided in the appendices.

## IIPreliminary

## II.1Primer on Error-Correcting Codes

Error correction refers to a broad class of techniques that are used to protect data from being corrupted by noisy environments.
Included in these techniques are: (i)error-correcting codesthat encode logical information with redundancies; (ii)recovery operationsthat aim to purify data; and (iii) strategies forscalingto arbitrarily-high robustness (c.f. fault tolerance and thresholds).
It was discovered in the 1990s that many techniques for protecting classical data are neatly generalizable to the quantum setting[14,75,31].
The similarities are powerful enough that it is useful for our purposes to separately review both the classical and quantum theory of error correction.
Indeed, many of the constructions introduced in this paper are formulated purely in the classical language, with the quantization being a mathematical formality.

This section is intended as a pedagogical introduction for the reader who is less familiar with these subjects.
We have chosen to restrict the discussion solely to error-correcting codes, as this is the key concept needed to understand the substance of this paper.
The only other technicality strictly needed to make sense of our results is the notion ofsyndrome decoding, which is discussed inAppendixB.
For the reader who is interested in learning more about error correction in the quantum setting, we highly recommend the famous notes by Gottesman[32]and Preskill[65].

## II.1.1Classical Linear Codes

Error-correcting codes were developed in the classical setting to facilitate the storing and transmission of data in the presence of noisy environments.
The idea is to convert akk-bit messagemminto a longernn-bitcodewordc​(m)c(m), prior to exposure to noise.
That way, when noise occurs, it happens directly on the codewordc​(m)c(m).
If the mathematical structure ofc​(m)c(m)is chosen cleverly, and the amount of noise experienced is reasonably small, then it can be possible to recover the exact original messagemm, in spite of the errors incurred.

Note that it is the bits of the codeword—not the message—that are directly subject to noise.
Therefore, many meaningful properties of an error-correcting code depend entirely on the set of codewords, remaining agnostic as to which codeword represents which underlying message.
For this reason, a classical code is typically defined purely by a selection ofnn-bit codewordsCC.
In the setting of binary codes, we haveC⊆{0,1}nC\subseteq\{0,1\}^{n}.

A useful class of error-correcting codes is provided by the (binary) linear codes.
This class represents the setting in which the set of codewordsCCis a linear subspace of the binary vector space𝔽2n\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}.
When we use the term “classical code” in this paper, we will always refer to binary linear codes.

## Definition II.1(Classical Code).

Letn≥1n\geq 1be an integer.
Then annn-bitclassical codeis a linear subspaceC⊆𝔽2nC\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}. Elementsc∈Cc\in Care calledcodewords.

## Example II.2(Classical Code).

Letn=5n=5, and defineC⊆𝔽25C\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{5}byC={00000,10100,01011,11111}.C=\{00000,10100,01011,11111\}.(II.1)

This is a linear subspace, becauseCCcan also be viewed as the span of two basis vectors,C=span⁡{10100,01011}.C=\operatorname{span}\{10100,01011\}.(II.2)

Therefore,CCis a55-bit classical code.

The basic function of a classical code is to be able to detect errors that may occur on codewords.
This is achieved by checking whether some received bit string is a codeword or not.
However, some errors will not be detectable.
For instance, if the bits flipped by an error are exactly those needed to transform the codeword into an entirely different codeword, then the received bit string will be recognized as a codeword, and the error will go undetected.
DefinitionII.3summarizes the notion of undetectable errors.

## Definition II.3(Undetectable Error).

LetC⊆𝔽2nC\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}be a classical code, and letc∈Cc\in Cbe a codeword.
A vectore∈𝔽2ne\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}is called anundetectable error when it acts onccif the following conditions are both satisfied:
- (i)

e≠0e\neq 0; and
- (ii)

c+e∈Cc+e\in C.

A basic fact about undetectable errors is that they arealwaysundetectable, irrespective of which codeword they act on.
In fact, any nonzero codeword is an undetectable error, and vice versa.
The nonzero requirement simply ensures that we’re genuinely talking about an “error,” meaning a strictchangein the codeword.
This is summarized by LemmaII.4, but it follows trivially from linearity.

## Lemma II.4.

LetC⊆𝔽2nC\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}be a classical code, and letc∈Cc\in Cbe arbitrary.
Theneeis an undetectable error when it acts onccif and only ifeeis a nonzero codeword.

Thus, we can simply write “eeis an undetectable error,” without referencing on which codeword it acts.

## Example II.5(Undetectable Error).

Continue from exampleII.2.
Take, for example, the codewordc=10100c=10100.
An example of an undetectable error on this codeword is obtained by flipping every single bit, which transformsccintoc′=01011c^{\prime}=01011.
This error is captured by the vectore=11111e=11111, so thatc+e=c′c+e=c^{\prime}.
And, as indicated by LemmaII.4,eeis indeed a codeword.

Moreover,eeis an undetectable error when it acts on any codeword.
For instance, flipping all the bits of0000000000byeeproduces1111111111, which is again a codeword.

Having discussed what exactly an undetectable error means, we can now discuss the basic properties of a classical codeCC.
The most common properties usually considered are:
- •

The total number of bits used by the code, also called the number ofphysical bits(denotednn, as before).
- •

The number oflogical bitsof information that can be stored in the code (denotedkk).
- •

The minimum number of bits that are flipped by any undetectable error, also called thedistanceof the code (denoteddd).

The number of logical bitskkcan be thought of as the maximum length of a messagemmthat can be faithfully encoded byCC.
Mathematically, this is simply given byk≡dimC.k\equiv\dim C.(II.3)

The code distanceddis a measure of how difficult it is to produce an error that is invisible to the codeCC.
Not all undetectable errors are equal in this regard; a common way to distinguish them is through theirweight.
The weight of an erroreeis denotedwt(e)\operatorname*{wt}(e), and it simply counts the number of11s in the binary representation ofee.
Therefore, the distance ofCCcan be mathematically expressed asd=minc∈C∖{0}​wt(c).d=\min_{c\in C\setminus\{0\}}\operatorname*{wt}(c).(II.4)

When a codeCChasnnphysical bits,kklogical bits, and code distancedd, it is common to write the descriptorC=[n,k,d]C=[n,k,d]as a shorthand.
Importantly, specifyingnn,kk, andddalone is not enough to fully specify the codeCC.
Nevertheless, the descriptor notation provides a quick and easy way to build a mental model of the performance and efficiency of the code, without getting into details.
The performance roughly corresponds to the distancedd, and the efficiency roughly corresponds to the ratior=k/nr=k/n, also called theencoding rate.

## Example II.6(Properties of Classical Code).

Continue from ExampleII.2.
The number of physical bits is clearlyn=5n=5.
The number of logical bits isk=2k=2, since Eq. (II.2) indicates that there are two basis vectors.
The distance isd=2d=2, because although we discussed the undetectable errore=11111e=11111in ExampleII.5, this is not the undetectable error with smallest weight—that would be1010010100, with weight22.
Based on the above observations, this code can be written with the shorthandC=[5,2,2]C=[5,2,2].

In order to detect (and eventually correct) errors in a classical codeCC, it is necessary to be able to quickly determine whether a givennn-bit stringvvis a codeword or not.
This knowledge could be obtained from looking at the full list of codewords, but that is not practical for larger codes.
A more efficient approach is to provide a set of linear constraints on the physical bits such thatCCis exactly the linear subspace that satisfies all of those constraints.
If all linear constraints are satisfied, thenv∈Cv\in C; if even a single linear constraint is not satisfied, thenv∉Cv\notin C.
In this setting, the linear constraints are calledparity checks(or simply “checks” for short).

IfC⊆𝔽2nC\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}iskk-dimensional, then one needs exactly(n−k)(n-k)independent parity checks.
One can always arrange for these checks to be homogeneous, so that they take the formH​c=0,Hc=0,(II.5)

whereHHis an(n−k)×n(n-k)\times nbinary matrix, called theparity-check matrix, such that the codeCCcoincides exactly with the set of vectorsc∈𝔽2nc\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}that satisfy Eq. (II.5).
Conversely, any(n−k)×n(n-k)\times nbinary parity-check matrixHHcan itself be used to define the codeCC.
Note that the same codeCCmay admit several parity-check matrices, which are thus considered equivalent.

## Definition II.7(Parity-Check Matrix).

LetC=[n,k,d]C=[n,k,d]be a classical code.
ThenH∈𝔽2(n−k)×nH\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{(n-k)\times n}is called a(full-rank)111It is possible that a parity check matrixH∈𝔽2m×nH\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{m\times n}is not full-rank. In this case,k=n−rank​(H)k=n-\text{rank}(H).parity-check matrixforCCifH​c=0⇔c∈C.Hc=0\Leftrightarrow c\in C.(II.6)

Conversely, any full-rankH∈𝔽2(n−k)×nH\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{(n-k)\times n}defines a classical codeCCbyC≡{c∈𝔽2n∣H​c=0}.C\equiv\{c\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}\mid Hc=0\}.(II.7)

## Example II.8(Parity-Check Matrix).

TakeC=[5,2,2]C=[5,2,2]from exampleII.6, and write out the bit string for each codeword asc=c1​c2​c3​c4​c5c=c_{1}c_{2}c_{3}c_{4}c_{5}.
The linear constraintsc1\displaystyle c_{1}=c3,\displaystyle=c_{3},(II.8)c2\displaystyle c_{2}=c4=c5,\displaystyle=c_{4}=c_{5},(II.9)

are satisfied for every codeword.
Moreover, every55-bit string that satisfies these constraints is also a codeword.
There are33independent constraints in (II.8), and they can be made homogeneous as follows:c1+c3\displaystyle c_{1}+c_{3}=0,\displaystyle=0,(II.10)c2+c4\displaystyle c_{2}+c_{4}=0,\displaystyle=0,(II.11)c4+c5\displaystyle c_{4}+c_{5}=0,\displaystyle=0,(II.12)

where addition is performed in𝔽2\mathbb{F}_{2}.
The matrix form of (II.10) is preciselyH​c=0Hc=0, whereH=(101000101000011)H=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&1&0&0\\
0&1&0&1&0\\
0&0&0&1&1\end{pmatrix}(II.13)

is the parity-check matrix.
Note that this matrix is not unique; for instance, we could have replaced the checkc4+c5=0c_{4}+c_{5}=0withc2+c5=0c_{2}+c_{5}=0, which would change the third row ofHH.

To test whetherv=10111v=10111is a codeword, simply computeH​v=010Hv=010.
Since this is nonzero,vvcannot be a codeword.
The quantityH​vHvis also sometimes denotedσ​(v)\sigma(v)and is called the(error) syndromeofvv.
In addition to providing insight on whether an error has occurred on a received word, the syndrome is often useful for deducing (ordecoding) which specific error occurred, thereby enabling recovery of the original encoded data.

Notably, this “syndrome decoding” process is largely a separate undertaking from the code construction itself, and we do not discuss it in the main text to avoid distracting from the central story of this paper.
We leave the task of defining error syndromes and decoding strategies toAppendixB, which contains all technical details required to mathematically specify our code capacity simulations.

## II.1.2Quantum Codes in the Stabilizer Formalism

Analogously to the classical case, quantum codes aim to encode akk-qubit message state|ψm⟩\ket{\psi_{m}}into annn-qubit code state|ψc⟩\ket{\psi_{c}}.
It is then only the code state|ψc⟩\ket{\psi_{c}}that is exposed to noise, meaning that the ability to protect against errors is primarily a function of the allowed set of code states.

We will focus on the special case where the set of code states forms a sub-Hilbert space of all possiblenn-qubit states.
This is called thecode space,ℋC⊆ℂ2n\mathcal{H}_{C}\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{2^{n}}.
The sub-Hilbert space property is the quantum analogue of the linear subspace property for classical codes.

## Definition II.9(Quantum Code).

Letn≥1n\geq 1be an integer.
Then annn-qubitquantum codeis a sub-Hilbert spaceℋC⊆ℂ2n\mathcal{H}_{C}\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{2^{n}}.
Elements ofℋC\mathcal{H}_{C}are called code states.

## Example II.10(Quantum Code).

Letn=5n=5, and consider the setℋC\mathcal{H}_{C}of all quantum states of the formα1​|00000⟩+α2​|10100⟩+α3​|01011⟩+α4​|11111⟩,\alpha_{1}\ket{00000}+\alpha_{2}\ket{10100}+\alpha_{3}\ket{01011}+\alpha_{4}\ket{11111},(II.14)

where eachαj\alpha_{j}is a complex number.
This is a sub-Hilbert space ofℂ25\mathbb{C}^{2^{5}}, and thereforeℋC\mathcal{H}_{C}qualifies as a55-qubit quantum code.222The fact that normalized quantum states in the code space satisfy∑j|αj|2=1\sum_{j}|\alpha_{j}|^{2}=1is independent from the definition of the code space itself.In fact, this is the quantum code whose code states are superpositions of codewords from the classical code defined in ExampleII.2.
Not all quantum codes have the simple interpretation of representing superpositions of classical codewords, but using an example with this property helps make the intuition behind the definitions more clear.

Having discussed the notion of quantum codes in general, we now turn to the special case ofstabilizer codes, which are central to the modern theory of QECCs.

## Definition II.11(Stabilizer Code).

Let𝒫n={±1,±i}⋅{I,X,Y,Z}⊗n\mathcal{P}_{n}=\{\pm 1,\pm i\}\cdot\{I,X,Y,Z\}^{\otimes n}be thenn-qubit Pauli group.
Suppose a subgroup𝒮\mathcal{S}of the Pauli group𝒫n\mathcal{P}_{n}satisfies the following properties:
- (i)

𝒮\mathcal{S}is Abelian;
- (ii)

The negative identity−I∈𝒫n-I\in\mathcal{P}_{n}isnotan element of𝒮\mathcal{S}.

Then quantum codeℋC⊆ℂ2n\mathcal{H}_{C}\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{2^{n}}defined byℋC={|ψ⟩∈ℂ2n∣S​|ψ⟩=|ψ⟩,∀S∈𝒮}.\mathcal{H}_{C}=\{\ket{\psi}\in\mathbb{C}^{2^{n}}\mid S\ket{\psi}=\ket{\psi},\quad\forall S\in\mathcal{S}\}.(II.15)

is called thestabilizer codewith stabilizer𝒮\mathcal{S}.
We also say thatℋC\mathcal{H}_{C}is thecode spaceof𝒮\mathcal{S}.

Even though the physical Hilbert spaceℂ2n\mathbb{C}^{2^{n}}has dimension which grows exponentially innn, the code space can be defined efficiently by using stabilizer codes.
In particular, DefinitionII.11reduces the problem to providing an Abelian subgroup𝒮\mathcal{S}of𝒫n\mathcal{P}_{n}.
Such a subgroup𝒮\mathcal{S}can always be defined by a set of at mostnngenerators.
Each stabilizer generator can be viewed as cutting the Hilbert space dimension in half (see LemmaII.16), implying that these are high-dimensional linear constraints.

## Example II.12(Stabilizer Code).

The quantum codeℋC\mathcal{H}_{C}from exampleII.10is actually a stabilizer code.
To understand this, consider the subgroup𝒮⊆𝒫5\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{5}generated by the operatorsS1\displaystyle S_{1}=Z I Z I I,\displaystyle=\texttt{Z I Z I I},(II.16)S2\displaystyle S_{2}=I Z I Z I,\displaystyle=\texttt{I Z I Z I},(II.17)S3\displaystyle S_{3}=I I I Z Z.\displaystyle=\texttt{I I I Z Z}.(II.18)

This clearly defines an Abelian group that does not contain−I-I.
For this example, we have selectedS1S_{1},S2S_{2}, andS3S_{3}asZZ-type operators that mimic the rowsh1h_{1},h2h_{2}, andh3h_{3}of the parity-check matrix (II.13) from exampleII.8:h1\displaystyle h_{1}=10100,\displaystyle=10100,(II.19)h2\displaystyle h_{2}=01010,\displaystyle=01010,(II.20)h3\displaystyle h_{3}=00011.\displaystyle=00011.(II.21)

By doing so, we are guaranteed thatSi​|x⟩=(−1)hi⋅x​|x⟩,S_{i}\ket{x}=(-1)^{h_{i}\cdot x}\ket{x},(II.22)

for any55-bit stringx∈𝔽25x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{5}in the computational basis.
Then the conditionsSi​|x⟩=|x⟩S_{i}\ket{x}=\ket{x}are precisely equivalent to the linear constraintshi⋅x=0h_{i}\cdot x=0, which are exactly the parity checks for the classical codeCCfrom ExampleII.2.
SinceℋC\mathcal{H}_{C}consists of superpositions of the classical codewords, as noted in ExampleII.10, this immediately implies by linearity thatℋC\mathcal{H}_{C}is the code space for𝒮\mathcal{S}, making it a stabilizer code.

A highly useful concept in the setting of stabilizer codes is that oflogical Pauli operators, which does not have a direct classical analogue.
In fact, this is precisely the state-independent quantum notion of an “undetectable” error, as clarified by LemmaII.14.
The reader who is less familiar with quantum error correction may consider proving it as an exercise.

## Definition II.13(Logical Pauli Operators).

LetℋC\mathcal{H}_{C}be a stabilizer code with stabilizer𝒮\mathcal{S}.
Then annn-qubit Pauli operatorE∈𝒫nE\in\mathcal{P}_{n}is called alogical Pauli operatorif the following conditions are satisfied:
- (i)

EEcommutes with every element of the stabilizer group𝒮\mathcal{S}.
- (ii)

EEisnotcontained in the stabilizer group𝒮\mathcal{S}.
- (iii)

EEisnota global phase operator,II,−I-I,i​IiI, or−i​I-iI.

As a shorthand, we write thatEEis a logical Pauli operator if and only ifE∈𝒩​(𝒮)∖(𝒮​ℐ),E\in\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{S})\setminus\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{I}\right),(II.23)

where𝒩​(𝒮)≡{P∈𝒫n∣P​S​P†∈𝒮,∀S∈𝒮}\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{S})\equiv\{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}\mid PSP^{\dagger}\in\mathcal{S},\quad\forall S\in\mathcal{S}\}(II.24)

is thenormalizerof𝒮\mathcal{S}in𝒫n\mathcal{P}_{n}, andℐ≡{I,i​I,−I,−i​I}⊆𝒫n\mathcal{I}\equiv\{I,iI,-I,-iI\}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{n}(II.25)

is the set of global phase operations; and𝒮​ℐ={ia​S∣a∈{0,1,2,3},S∈𝒮}.\mathcal{S}\mathcal{I}=\{i^{a}S\mid a\in\{0,1,2,3\},S\in\mathcal{S}\}\,.(II.26)

## Lemma II.14.

LetℋC\mathcal{H}_{C}be a stabilizer code with stabilizer𝒮\mathcal{S}, and supposeE∈𝒫nE\in\mathcal{P}_{n}is a Pauli operator.
ThenEEisundetectable(meaning thatE​|ψ⟩∝|ψ⟩E\ket{\psi}\propto\ket{\psi}for some|ψ⟩∈ℋC\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{H}_{C}) if and only ifEEis a logical Pauli operator.
Moreover, this is equivalent to the condition thatEEcommutes with the entire stabilizer𝒮\mathcal{S}, while not being related by global phase to any element of𝒮\mathcal{S}.

## Example II.15(Logical Pauli Operators).

TakeℋC\mathcal{H}_{C}to be the quantum code from ExampleII.10, whose stabilizer𝒮\mathcal{S}was determined in ExampleII.12.
An example of a logical operator onℋC\mathcal{H}_{C}is given byE=X X X X XE=\texttt{X X X X X}.
A quick check shows thatEEcommutes with all stabilizer generators,S1S_{1},S2S_{2}, andS3S_{3}.
In fact, this operation has precisely the same interpretation as the undetectable error discussed in ExampleII.5for the classical code—both operations apply a bit-flip to all55bits.
To make mathematical sense of why the detectability of classical bit-flip errors is the same as that forXX-type errors in the quantum setting, note the relationSi​E=(−1)hi⋅e​E​Si,S_{i}E=(-1)^{h_{i}\cdot e}ES_{i},(II.27)

which holds for anyXX-type Pauli errorEE, provided thate∈𝔽25e\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{5}is chosen to be the corresponding classical bit-flip error.

At least for bit-flip errors, this clarifies why commutation with the stabilizer group is related to the undetectability of the error.
As seen from LemmaII.14, it is in fact the general rule that a Pauli operator will be undetectable oneverycode state if and only if it commutes with the entire stabilizer.
Analogously to the classical case, this motivates the notion ofstabilizer syndromefor Pauli operators.
IfE∈𝒫nE\in\mathcal{P}_{n}is any Pauli operator, then its syndrome is defined to be the answers to each boolean question of the form, “DoesEEcommute with the stabilizer generatorSi∈𝒮S_{i}\in\mathcal{S}?”
As in the classical case, the syndrome clearly helps to determine the detectability of an error, but it can also be used to posit informed guesses on how an error should be corrected, by using syndrome decoders.
We delegate the discussion of technicalities related to syndrome decoding toAppendixB.

Stabilizer codes admit properties that are analogous to the properties of classical codes.
The most basic properties are the following:
- •

The total number of qubits used by the code, also called the number ofphysical qubits(denotednn).
- •

The number oflogical qubitsof information that can be stored in the code (denotedkk).
- •

The minimum number of qubits acted upon by any logical Pauli operator, also called thedistanceof the code (denoteddd).

The number of logical qubitskkcan be thought of as the maximum number of qubits in a message state|ψm⟩\ket{\psi_{m}}that can be faithfully encoded by the code.
Mathematically, it is given byk=log2⁡(dimℋC).k=\log_{2}(\dim\mathcal{H}_{C}).(II.28)

In practical terms, however, one needs to know howkkcan be determined from the generators of a stabilizer group𝒮\mathcal{S}, since that will be the actual information available when dealing with a code.
For this, we refer to LemmaII.16, which immediately implies thatk=n−rk=n-r, whererris the number of stabilizer generators.

## Lemma II.16.

Let𝒮\mathcal{S}be annn-qubit stabilizer group with code spaceℋC\mathcal{H}_{C}.
Ifr≥1r\geq 1is an integer, and{S1,S2,…,Sr}⊆𝒮\{S_{1},S_{2},\dots,S_{r}\}\subseteq\mathcal{S}is a complete set of independent generators for𝒮\mathcal{S}, thendimℋC=2n−r\dim\mathcal{H}_{C}=2^{n-r}.

As in the classical case, the code distanceddis a measure of how difficult it is to produce an error that is invisible to the code—but this time, the errors can be Pauli operators.
In this setting, theweightof an errorEEis denotedwt(E)\operatorname*{wt}(E), and it counts the number of physical qubits on whichEEacts as non-identity.
This is the analogue of the classical weight function that counts the number of nonzero bits.
The distance of the stabilizer code is then the minimum weight of any logical Pauli operator.
In other words, this can mathematically be expressed asd=minE∈𝒩​(𝒮)∖(𝒮​ℐ)​wt(E).d=\min_{E\in\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{S})\setminus(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{I})}\operatorname*{wt}(E).(II.29)

As in the classical case, it is common to summarize the properties of a stabilizer code by using the descriptor⟦n,k,d⟧\llbracket n,k,d\rrbracket, where the double-bracket notation indicates that this is a stabilizer code, rather than a classical code.

## Example II.17(Properties of Stabilizer Code).

TakeℋC\mathcal{H}_{C}and𝒮\mathcal{S}from ExamplesII.10andII.12, which hasn=5n=5physical qubits. There arer=3r=3stabilizer generators, so the number of logical qubits can also be computed ask=n−r=2k=n-r=2.
This agrees with the fact thatdimℋC=4\dim\mathcal{H}_{C}=4, corresponding to two logical qubits.

For the code distance, we need the minimum-weight Pauli errorEEthat is undetectable by the code.
The logical operatorX X X X Xfrom ExampleII.15has weight55, but it is not the smallest weight error.
As an example, consider the operatorE=Z I I I IE=\texttt{Z I I I I}.
Clearly, this operator commutes with the entire stabilizer𝒮\mathcal{S}, and it’s not difficult to see thatEEis not in𝒮\mathcal{S}and is clearly not a global phase.
Therefore,EEis a logical operator.
LemmaII.14suggests that there must exist a code state|ψ⟩\ket{\psi}which is mapped to a physically different code state byEE.
Such an example is not hard to find; for instance,E​(|00000⟩+|10100⟩)=|00000⟩−|10100⟩.E\left(\ket{00000}+\ket{10100}\right)=\ket{00000}-\ket{10100}.(II.30)

The weight of this operator iswt(E)=1\operatorname*{wt}(E)=1, which clearly cannot be reduced without literally making it proportional to the identity element of the Pauli group (and thus not an error).
Therefore, the code distance isd=1d=1.

Putting it all together, the codeℋC\mathcal{H}_{C}is a⟦5,2,1⟧\llbracket 5,2,1\rrbracketstabilizer code.
Note that this descriptor does not exactly match the descriptor[5,2,2][5,2,2]for the classical codeCCthat is imitated byℋC\mathcal{H}_{C}.
It matches on the number of physical bits and logical bits, but not on the code distance.

The number of physical qubits matches the number of physical bits because each qubit of the quantum code is imitating a corresponding bit of the classical code.
The number of logical qubits matches the number of logical bits because LemmaII.16shows that each stabilizer generator reduces the Hilbert space dimension by a factor of two.
But the code distance does not match, because the minimum-weight Pauli error was aZZ-error, which has no classical analogue—the classical code was only detecting bit-flip errors, which areXX-errors in this setting.

The failure of the quantum code distance to match the classical code distance in ExampleII.6highlights an important point: quantum codes need to take into account the possibility of bit-flip errorsandphase-flip errors.
With some ingenuity, this is achievable in the stabilizer framework, as discussed in any standard text on the subject, e.g.[61,64].
For the purposes of this paper, we will deal with this point by introducing a construction known as theconcatenationof two codes.

## II.1.3Concatenation of Codes

There are many ways to construct stabilizer codes that can simultaneously handle bit-flip and phase-flip errors.
Any standard reference will discuss this point, which includes the construction of CSS codes, perfect codes, and concatenation[48].
For the purposes of this work, we will need the concatenation construction.
In fact, we will only need a very special case of concatenation, formulated so that it produces a quantum code from a pair of classical codes.

As alluded to by ExampleII.12, it is possible to produce a stabilizer code—entirely fromZZ-type operators—that mimics a classical parity-check matrix.
But the problem with doing so, as discussed in ExampleII.6, is that the code distance drops to11, because although bit-flip errors can be detected exactly as in the classical code, the quantum code remains indifferent to phase-flip errors.
Conversely, if we were to construct a stabilizer code entirely fromXX-type operators, it would be able to detect phase-flip errors, but it would become oblivious to bit-flip errors.

Concatenation solves this problem by instead using two parity-check matrices,H(1)H^{(1)}andH(2)H^{(2)}, for classical codes[nout,kout,dout][n_{\mathrm{out}},k_{\mathrm{out}},d_{\mathrm{out}}]and[nin,kin,din][n_{\mathrm{in}},k_{\mathrm{in}},d_{\mathrm{in}}], respectively.
ConvertingH(1)H^{(1)}intoZZ-type stabilizer generators, and consideringnoutn_{\mathrm{out}}blocks ofninn_{\mathrm{in}}qubits, it becomes possible to detectXX-type errors that are incurred over multiple blocks of the code.
However,ZZ-type errors remain undetected.
By convertingH(2)H^{(2)}intoXX-type stabilizer generators, consideringnoutn_{\mathrm{out}}blocks ofninn_{\mathrm{in}}qubitsallows to detectZZ-type errors.

To define the construction precisely, the simplest method is to write down the stabilizer generators.
We will focus on the case wherekin=1k_{\mathrm{in}}=1, since that is all we will need in this paper.
Our optimal code constructions inSectionIIIare largely unaffected if other concatenation strategies are used.

We first introduce a precursor definition (mostly for notation):

## Definition II.18.

Lethhbe annn-bit string, and letOObe ann′n^{\prime}-qubit operator.
Then defineSO​(h)S^{O}(h)to be then×n′n\times n^{\prime}-qubit operatorSO​(h)≡⨂i=1nO​(i),S^{O}(h)\equiv\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}O(i),(II.31)

whereO​(i)O(i)is then′n^{\prime}-qubit operatorO​(i)≡{I⊗n′,hi=0,O,hi=1.O(i)\equiv\begin{cases}I^{\otimes n^{\prime}},&h_{i}=0,\\
O,&h_{i}=1.\end{cases}(II.32)

## Definition II.19(Concatenation).

LetCout=[nout,kout,dout]C_{\mathrm{out}}=[n_{\mathrm{out}},k_{\mathrm{out}},d_{\mathrm{out}}]andCin=[nin,1,din]C_{\mathrm{in}}=[n_{\mathrm{in}},1,d_{\mathrm{in}}]be two classical codes, with parity-check matricesH(out)H^{(\mathrm{out})}andH(in)H^{(\mathrm{in})}, respectively.
Writehi(out)h^{(\mathrm{out})}_{i}andhi(in)h^{(\mathrm{in})}_{i}for theithi^{\mathrm{th}}rows ofH(out)H^{(\mathrm{out})}andH(in)H^{(\mathrm{in})}, respectively.
For1≤i≤nout1\leq i\leq n_{\mathrm{out}}and1≤j≤nin−11\leq j\leq n_{\mathrm{in}}-1(remember thatkin=1k_{\mathrm{in}}=1), define thenout×ninn_{\mathrm{out}}\times n_{\mathrm{in}}-qubitinner blockoperatorsSi,j≡I⊗(i−1)​nin⊗SX​(hj(in))⊗I⊗(nout−i)​nin,S_{i,j}\equiv I^{\otimes(i-1)n_{\mathrm{in}}}\otimes S^{X}\left(h^{(\mathrm{in})}_{j}\right)\otimes I^{\otimes(n_{\mathrm{out}}-i)n_{\mathrm{in}}},(II.33)

whereXXdenotes the single-qubit Pauli-XXoperator.
Now letZ¯\overline{Z}denote anyZZ-type Pauli operator that is a logical error forCinC_{\mathrm{in}}.
For1≤i≤nout−kout1\leq i\leq n_{\mathrm{out}}-k_{\mathrm{out}}, define thenout×ninn_{\mathrm{out}}\times n_{\mathrm{in}}-qubitouter blockoperatorsSi≡SZ¯​(hi(out)).S_{i}\equiv S^{\overline{Z}}\left(h^{(\mathrm{out})}_{i}\right).(II.34)

Then theconcatenationofCoutC_{\mathrm{out}}andCinC_{\mathrm{in}}is the stabilizer code generated by all inner block and outer block operators defined above.
We say thatCoutC_{\mathrm{out}}is theouter code, andCinC_{\mathrm{in}}is theinner codefor this construction.
The concatenation ofCoutC_{\mathrm{out}}andCinC_{\mathrm{in}}is denotedCout∘CinC_{\mathrm{out}}\circ C_{\mathrm{in}}.

Note that this definition depends on the choice of parity-check matrices used to define the classical codesCinC_{\mathrm{in}}andCoutC_{\mathrm{out}}, and we mentioned earlier that a given code does not necessarily have a unique parity check matrix. However, the concatenation is independent of the choice of parity check matrices:

## Lemma II.20(Independent of Parity-Check Matrices).

LetCout=[nout,kout,dout]C_{\mathrm{out}}=[n_{\mathrm{out}},k_{\mathrm{out}},d_{\mathrm{out}}]andCin=[nin,1,din]C_{\mathrm{in}}=[n_{\mathrm{in}},1,d_{\mathrm{in}}]be two classical codes.
Then the concatenationC≡Cout∘CinC\equiv C_{\mathrm{out}}\circ C_{\mathrm{in}}is independent of the parity-check matrices chosen forCoutC_{\mathrm{out}}andCinC_{\mathrm{in}}.

LemmaII.20serves as the final step to conclude our definition, ensuring that concatenated codes are well-defined even when parity-check matrices are not explicitly provided.
We can now proceed to ExampleII.21, which shows explicitly how to build the stabilizer generators for a concatenated code.

## Example II.21(Concatenation).

Take the outer codeCoutC_{\mathrm{out}}to be defined by the parity-check matrixH(out)=(101000101000011),H^{(\mathrm{out})}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&1&0&0\\
0&1&0&1&0\\
0&0&0&1&1\end{pmatrix},(II.35)

as used in previous examples.
Clearly,Cout=[5,2,2]C_{\mathrm{out}}=[5,2,2]as we have discussed in previous examples.
Take the inner codeCinC_{\mathrm{in}}to be defined by the parity-check matrixH(in)=(110011).H^{(\mathrm{in})}=\begin{pmatrix}1&1&0\\
0&1&1\end{pmatrix}.(II.36)

It can be shown thatCin=[3,1,3]C_{\mathrm{in}}=[3,1,3], which encodes a single logical bit, and therefore can be used in the concatenation construction from DefinitionII.19.

First, we construct operatorsSX​(hj(in))S^{X}\left(h^{(\mathrm{in})}_{j}\right)from the rows of (II.36) asSX​(h1(in))\displaystyle S^{X}\left(h^{(\mathrm{in})}_{1}\right)=X X I,\displaystyle=\texttt{X X I},(II.37)SX​(h2(in))\displaystyle S^{X}\left(h^{(\mathrm{in})}_{2}\right)=I X X,\displaystyle=\texttt{I X X},(II.38)

which leads to the inner block generatorsS1,1\displaystyle S_{1,1}=X X I​∣I I I∣​I I I​∣I I I∣​I I I,\displaystyle=\texttt{X X I}\mid\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{I I I},(II.39)S1,2\displaystyle S_{1,2}=I X X​∣I I I∣​I I I​∣I I I∣​I I I,\displaystyle=\texttt{I X X}\mid\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{I I I},(II.40)S2,1\displaystyle S_{2,1}=I I I​∣X X I∣​I I I​∣I I I∣​I I I,\displaystyle=\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{X X I}\mid\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{I I I},(II.41)S2,2\displaystyle S_{2,2}=I I I​∣I X X∣​I I I​∣I I I∣​I I I,\displaystyle=\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{I X X}\mid\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{I I I},(II.42)S3,1\displaystyle S_{3,1}=I I I​∣I I I∣​X X I​∣I I I∣​I I I,\displaystyle=\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{X X I}\mid\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{I I I},(II.43)S3,2\displaystyle S_{3,2}=I I I​∣I I I∣​I X X​∣I I I∣​I I I,\displaystyle=\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{I X X}\mid\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{I I I},(II.44)S4,1\displaystyle S_{4,1}=I I I​∣I I I∣​I I I​∣X X I∣​I I I,\displaystyle=\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{X X I}\mid\texttt{I I I},(II.45)S4,2\displaystyle S_{4,2}=I I I​∣I I I∣​I I I​∣I X X∣​I I I,\displaystyle=\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{I X X}\mid\texttt{I I I},(II.46)S5,1\displaystyle S_{5,1}=I I I​∣I I I∣​I I I​∣I I I∣​X X I,\displaystyle=\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{X X I},(II.47)S5,2\displaystyle S_{5,2}=I I I​∣I I I∣​I I I​∣I I I∣​I X X,\displaystyle=\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{I X X},(II.48)

where the divider lines∣\midare shown to make it easier to distinguish the different blocks of qubits.

Next, for the outer block generators, we need to choose aZZ-type Pauli operatorZ¯\overline{Z}that serves as a logical error for the inner codeCinC_{\mathrm{in}}, whereCinC_{\mathrm{in}}has been modified to work in theXX-basis via the operators Eq. (II.37).
An example of a logicalZZ-type error is provided byZ¯=Z Z Z\overline{Z}=\texttt{Z Z Z}, which clearly commutes with bothSX​(h1(in))S^{X}\left(h^{(\mathrm{in})}_{1}\right)andSX​(h2(in))S^{X}\left(h^{(\mathrm{in})}_{2}\right), but is not contained in the group generated by those operators.

By using the notation from definitionII.18, the operatorsSZ¯S^{\overline{Z}}are given bySZ¯​(h1(out))\displaystyle S^{\overline{Z}}\left(h^{(\mathrm{out})}_{1}\right)=Z¯​∣I¯∣​Z¯​∣I¯∣​I¯,\displaystyle=\overline{\texttt{Z}}\mid\overline{\texttt{I}}\mid\overline{\texttt{Z}}\mid\overline{\texttt{I}}\mid\overline{\texttt{I}},(II.49)SZ¯​(h2(out))\displaystyle S^{\overline{Z}}\left(h^{(\mathrm{out})}_{2}\right)=I¯​∣Z¯∣​I¯​∣Z¯∣​I¯,\displaystyle=\overline{\texttt{I}}\mid\overline{\texttt{Z}}\mid\overline{\texttt{I}}\mid\overline{\texttt{Z}}\mid\overline{\texttt{I}},(II.50)SZ¯​(h3(out))\displaystyle S^{\overline{Z}}\left(h^{(\mathrm{out})}_{3}\right)=I¯​∣I¯∣​I¯​∣Z¯∣​Z¯,\displaystyle=\overline{\texttt{I}}\mid\overline{\texttt{I}}\mid\overline{\texttt{I}}\mid\overline{\texttt{Z}}\mid\overline{\texttt{Z}},(II.51)

where we defineI¯\overline{\texttt{I}}as a shorthand for the33-qubit identity,I I I.
Plugging inZ¯\overline{Z}, we finally obtain the outer block generatorsS1\displaystyle S_{1}=Z Z Z​∣I I I∣​Z Z Z​∣I I I∣​I I I,\displaystyle=\texttt{Z Z Z}\mid\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{Z Z Z}\mid\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{I I I},(II.52)S2\displaystyle S_{2}=I I I​∣Z Z Z∣​I I I​∣Z Z Z∣​I I I,\displaystyle=\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{Z Z Z}\mid\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{Z Z Z}\mid\texttt{I I I},(II.53)S3\displaystyle S_{3}=I I I​∣I I I∣​I I I​∣Z Z Z∣​Z Z Z.\displaystyle=\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{Z Z Z}\mid\texttt{Z Z Z}.(II.54)

The concatenated code is now fully defined by taking the inner block generatorsSi,jS_{i,j}together with the outer block generatorsSiS_{i}.
If any22-qubitZZ-type error occurs—whether supported on a single block, or separated between multiple blocks—then itmustanti-commute with one of theXX-type generatorsSi,jS_{i,j}, and therefore it will be detected by the concatenated code.
Similarly, if any single-qubitXX-type error occurs, itmustanti-commute with one of theZZ-type generatorsSiS_{i}, and therefore it will also be detected by the concatenated code.
It is not hard to see that these properties follow from the classical abilities of the inner codeCin=[3,1,3]C_{\mathrm{in}}=[3,1,3]and outer codeCout=[5,2,2]C_{\mathrm{out}}=[5,2,2]to detect errors of weights22and11, respectively.
Extending this observation to the smallestundetectable(i.e., logical) errors, we obtain lemmaII.22.

## Lemma II.22.

LetCout=[nout,kout,dout]C_{\mathrm{out}}=[n_{\mathrm{out}},k_{\mathrm{out}},d_{\mathrm{out}}]andCin=[nin,1,din]C_{\mathrm{in}}=[n_{\mathrm{in}},1,d_{\mathrm{in}}]be two classical codes.
WritedXd_{X}anddZd_{Z}for the minimum weights ofXX-type andZZ-type Pauli operators that are logical errors for the concatenated codeCout∘CinC_{\mathrm{out}}\circ C_{\mathrm{in}}.
ThendX=doutd_{X}=d_{\mathrm{out}}anddZ=dind_{Z}=d_{\mathrm{in}}.

On the surface, LemmaII.22might not seem immediately helpful to understand the actual distance of a concatenated code.
After all, distance refers to the smallest weight ofanylogical Pauli error, which can even include mixed errors that are made from all three Pauli matrices,XX,YY, andZZ.
This is where LemmaII.23comes into play.
In particular, it clarifies that knowledge ofdXd_{X}anddZd_{Z}immediately implies knowledge of the full code distance.

## Lemma II.23(Properties of Concatenated Code).

LetCout=[nout,kout,dout]C_{\mathrm{out}}=[n_{\mathrm{out}},k_{\mathrm{out}},d_{\mathrm{out}}]andCin=[nin,1,din]C_{\mathrm{in}}=[n_{\mathrm{in}},1,d_{\mathrm{in}}]be two classical codes.
Then the concatenationCout∘CinC_{\mathrm{out}}\circ C_{\mathrm{in}}is a stabilizer code with descriptorCout∘Cin=⟦nout×nin,kout,min⁡(dout,din)⟧.C_{\mathrm{out}}\circ C_{\mathrm{in}}=\llbracket n_{\mathrm{out}}\times n_{\mathrm{in}},k_{\mathrm{out}},\min(d_{\mathrm{out}},d_{\mathrm{in}})\rrbracket.(II.55)

## Example II.24(Properties of Concatenated Code).

Continue from ExampleII.21.
It’s immediately clear that the number of physical qubits in the concatenation isn=nout×nin=15n=n_{\mathrm{out}}\times n_{\mathrm{in}}=15.

To understand the number of logical qubits, note that we have1010inner block generators and33outer block generators, for a total ofr=13r=13stabilizer generators.
Then the number of logical qubits is given byk=n−r=2k=n-r=2, which indeed does match the number of logical bitskout=2k_{\mathrm{out}}=2of the classical codeCoutC_{\mathrm{out}}.

Finally, regarding the code distance, LemmaII.23suggests that the distance should be22.
This is equivalent to saying that any single-qubit Pauli error should be detectable, and there is a22-qubit Pauli error that is logical.
Note that this should be true when considering theentirePauli group—not justXX-type orZZ-type errors.
Indeed, by glancing at the generators from ExampleII.21, it’s easy to see that any single-qubitXX,YY, orZZerror anticommutes with at leastoneof the generators.
Additionally, there are plenty of22-qubit Pauli errors that are invisible to the code; an example is given byE=X I I​∣I I I∣​I X I​∣I I I∣​I I I,E=\texttt{X I I}\mid\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{I X I}\mid\texttt{I I I}\mid\texttt{I I I},(II.56)

which clearly commutes with all inner and outer block generators.
Therefore, the code distance isd=2d=2.

Putting it all together, the descriptor of the concatenated code isCout∘Cin=⟦15,2,2⟧,C_{\mathrm{out}}\circ C_{\mathrm{in}}=\llbracket 15,2,2\rrbracket,(II.57)

as suggested by LemmaII.23.

A useful benefit to keep in mind when thinking about concatenated codes is that, while there is a fixed code distanced=min⁡(dX,dZ)d=\min(d_{X},d_{Z}), it is possible in practice to detect many errors with weights higher thandd.
For instance, if low-weight errors occur on every single block of the code, then the inner generators will still detect them.
The exact code distance becomes the dominant measure of performance only when we deal with very specific error channels (e.g., local stochastic Pauli noise) and consider the limit of very low per-qubit error rates.
This point will be revisited inSectionIV.

## II.2ℤ2\mathbb{Z}_{2}Lattice Gauge Theory with and without Gauss’s law constraints

This work focuses on constructing error-correcting codes forℤ2\mathbb{Z}_{2}lattice gauge theories (LGTs) coupled to matter.
While there are several inequivalent LGTs that can be defined with the gauge groupℤ2\mathbb{Z}_{2}, many of them share exactly the same Hilbert space at the kinematical level, once a lattice geometry has been fixed.
Therefore, the construction of error-correcting codes needs essentially zero input from the dynamics of these theories.

That being said, the dynamics can indeed impact the relative difficulty when it comes to actually implementing a full quantum simulation protocol for a given LGT.
For instance, it is completely possible that a codeC1C_{1}serves better than another codeC2C_{2}when simulating HamiltonianHH; while at the same time,C2C_{2}serves better thanC1C_{1}for simulating a different HamiltonianH′H^{\prime}.
The difference can even depend on the specific simulation strategy chosen for each Hamiltonian, or indeed the low-level hardware specifications.

In this work, we do not perform a detailed comparison of circuits for quantum simulation; instead, we consider thelocality of the encoded Hamiltonianas a simple proxy for the difficulty of simulation.
Comparisons using this proxy are performed inSectionIV, but they require us to introduce explicit Hamiltonians in this section.
Since the code constructions depend only on the kinematics and not the dynamics, we have organized this section based on the kinematical Hilbert space, and we discuss the prototypical Hamiltonian choice for each Hilbert space therein.
All notation introduced in this section is summarized inTable2.

Before discussing the Hilbert spaces we need, it is worth outlining the general setup for all LGTs considered in this work.
Unless otherwise specified, we work on a periodic, hypercubic latticeΛ≡([0,N1)×[0,N2)×⋯×[0,ND))∩ℤD,\Lambda\equiv\left([0,N_{1})\times[0,N_{2})\times\cdots\times[0,N_{D})\right)\cap\mathbb{Z}^{D},(II.58)

inDDspatial dimensions.
Some LGTs may use staggered fermions for their matter content, which requires that the number of lattice sitesNμN_{\mu}along theμth\mu^{\mathrm{th}}axis is aneven integerforμ∈{1,2,…,D}\mu\in\{1,2,\dots,D\}.
The set of lattice sites will be denoted𝒮≅Λ\mathcal{S}\cong\Lambda, the set of links will be denotedℒ\mathcal{L}, and the set of plaquettes will be denoted𝒫\mathcal{P}.
For any plaquetteP∈𝒫P\in\mathcal{P}, we write∂P\partial Pto denote the set of linksL∈ℒL\in\mathcal{L}comprising its boundary.

There are two distinguished kinematical descriptions for theℤ2\mathbb{Z}_{2}LGT that we will consider.
We will often refer to these as the “standard” description and the “gauge-fixed” description.
In the standard description, gauge fields are supported on links, matter is supported on lattice sites, and Gauss’s law is enforced by hand (but not kinematically).
In the gauge-fixed description, the Hilbert space is reduced by solving Gauss’s law at the kinematical level, thereby removing all matter content and leaving the gauge fields as the only dynamical degrees of freedom.
This kinematical reduction allows any Hamiltonian in the standard description to be uniquely projected down to a Hamiltonian in the gauge-fixed formulation, with identical dynamics.

## II.2.1Standard Description

Lattice sites are labeled byS𝐧S_{\mathbf{n}}, where𝐧=(n1,n2,…,nD)∈Λ\mathbf{n}=(n_{1},n_{2},\dots,n_{D})\in\Lambdais a lattice coordinate.
Links are labeled byL𝐧,μL_{\mathbf{n},\mu}, where𝐧∈Λ\mathbf{n}\in\Lambdaandμ∈{1,2,…,D}\mu\in\{1,2,\dots,D\}denotes an available spatial direction.
By abuse of notation, we useS𝐧S_{\mathbf{n}}to simultaneously denote the siteS𝐧∈𝒮S_{\mathbf{n}}\in\mathcal{S}, as well as the corresponding site variable operator, and itsℤ2\mathbb{Z}_{2}-eigenvalue (represented as a bitS𝐧∈𝔽2S_{\mathbf{n}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}) in any given state within its own eigenbasis.
Similarly, we useL𝐧,μL_{\mathbf{n},\mu}to simultaneously denote the linkL𝐧,μ∈ℒL_{\mathbf{n},\mu}\in\mathcal{L}, the corresponding link variable operator, and itsℤ2\mathbb{Z}_{2}-eigenvalue (represented as a bitL𝐧,μ∈𝔽2L_{\mathbf{n},\mu}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}) in any given state within its own eigenbasis.
Graphically, this labeling of lattice data is shown for the22D spatial lattice inFig.2.L(1,1),1L_{(1,1),1}L(1,1),2L_{(1,1),2}S(1,1)S_{(1,1)}L(2,1),1L_{(2,1),1}L(2,1),2L_{(2,1),2}S(2,1)S_{(2,1)}L(3,1),1L_{(3,1),1}L(3,1),2L_{(3,1),2}S(3,1)S_{(3,1)}L(4,1),1L_{(4,1),1}L(4,1),2L_{(4,1),2}S(4,1)S_{(4,1)}L(1,2),1L_{(1,2),1}L(1,2),2L_{(1,2),2}S(1,2)S_{(1,2)}L(2,2),1L_{(2,2),1}L(2,2),2L_{(2,2),2}S(2,2)S_{(2,2)}L(3,2),1L_{(3,2),1}L(3,2),2L_{(3,2),2}S(3,2)S_{(3,2)}L(4,2),1L_{(4,2),1}L(4,2),2L_{(4,2),2}S(4,2)S_{(4,2)}L(1,3),1L_{(1,3),1}L(1,3),2L_{(1,3),2}S(1,3)S_{(1,3)}L(2,3),1L_{(2,3),1}L(2,3),2L_{(2,3),2}S(2,3)S_{(2,3)}L(3,3),1L_{(3,3),1}L(3,3),2L_{(3,3),2}S(3,3)S_{(3,3)}L(4,3),1L_{(4,3),1}L(4,3),2L_{(4,3),2}S(4,3)S_{(4,3)}L(1,4),1L_{(1,4),1}L(1,4),2L_{(1,4),2}S(1,4)S_{(1,4)}L(2,4),1L_{(2,4),1}L(2,4),2L_{(2,4),2}S(2,4)S_{(2,4)}L(3,4),1L_{(3,4),1}L(3,4),2L_{(3,4),2}S(3,4)S_{(3,4)}L(4,4),1L_{(4,4),1}L(4,4),2L_{(4,4),2}S(4,4)S_{(4,4)}Figure 2:Example4×44\times 4lattice in two spatial dimensions, with periodic boundary conditions. The unique set of sites and links are shown, with an example indexing. The Hilbert spaceℋ\mathcal{H}for this lattice is spanned by every possible assignment of bits to the site and link variables, disregarding Gauss’s law constraints.

The Hilbert spaceℋ\mathcal{H}used in the standard description is then spanned by the simultaneous eigenbasis of allS𝐧S_{\mathbf{n}}andL𝐧,μL_{\mathbf{n},\mu}operators.
In other words,ℋ\mathcal{H}consists of a single qubit on every lattice site and link, representing theℤ2\mathbb{Z}_{2}-charge orℤ2\mathbb{Z}_{2}-flux on that site or link, respectively.

Gauge invariant states in this description can be defined via the Gauss law operatorsG𝐧≡ZS𝐧×∏L∈∂S𝐧∗ZL,𝐧∈Λ,G_{\mathbf{n}}\equiv Z_{S_{\mathbf{n}}}\times\prod_{L\in\partial S^{*}_{\mathbf{n}}}Z_{L},\quad\mathbf{n}\in\Lambda,(II.59)

where we use the notation∏L∈∂S𝐧∗ZL≡(∏μ=1DZL𝐧,μ)×(∏μ=1DZL𝐧−μ^,μ),\prod_{L\in\partial S_{\mathbf{n}}^{*}}Z_{L}\equiv\left(\prod_{\mu=1}^{D}Z_{L_{\mathbf{n},\mu}}\right)\times\left(\prod_{\mu=1}^{D}Z_{L_{\mathbf{n}-\hat{\mu},\mu}}\right),(II.60)

to denote the product over all linksLLthat are incident to the lattice site𝐧\mathbf{n}, andμ^\hat{\mu}denotes the unit lattice vector along theμ\mu-direction.
When we subscript a Pauli operator with a lattice variable, we mean that the Pauli operator acts solely on the qubit corresponding to that lattice variable.

The gauge-invariant subspaceℋG⊆ℋ\mathcal{H}_{G}\subseteq\mathcal{H}is then the simultaneous+1+1-eigenspace of all Gauss’s law operatorsG𝐧G_{\mathbf{n}};ℋG:={|ψ⟩∈ℋ∣G𝐧​|ψ⟩=|ψ⟩,∀𝐧∈Λ}.\mathcal{H}_{G}:=\{\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{H}\mid G_{\mathbf{n}}\ket{\psi}=\ket{\psi},\,\forall\mathbf{n}\in\Lambda\}\,.(II.61)

Indeed, states in this subspace remain invariant under arbitrary gauge transformations.

There are several inequivalentℤ2\mathbb{Z}_{2}LGTs that can be defined in the standard description, thereby carrying the same kinematical Hilbert spaceℋ\mathcal{H}, the same Gauss’s law operatorsG𝐧G_{\mathbf{n}}, and the same resulting gauge-invariant subspaceℋG\mathcal{H}_{G}.
These include theories with hard-core bosonic matter[41,42,40,5], and truncations of theU​(1)\mathrm{U}(1)gauge field coupled to staggered fermions[51,80].
A prototypical Hamiltonian forℤ2\mathbb{Z}_{2}LGT coupled to
hard-core bosonic matter[41]takes the electric basis formH=\displaystyle H=−g24​∑L∈ℒZL−m2​∑S∈𝒮ZS−λ​∑P∈𝒫∏L∈∂PXL\displaystyle\,-\frac{g^{2}}{4}\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}}Z_{L}-\frac{m}{2}\sum_{S\in\mathcal{S}}Z_{S}-\lambda\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\prod_{L\in\partial P}X_{L}−κ2​∑𝐧∈Λ∑μ=1DXS𝐧​XL𝐧,μ​XS𝐧+μ^,\displaystyle\,-\frac{\kappa}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\Lambda}\sum_{\mu=1}^{D}X_{S_{\mathbf{n}}}X_{L_{\mathbf{n},\mu}}X_{S_{\mathbf{n}+\hat{\mu}}}\,,(II.62)

where the plaquette term with coupling constantλ\lambdashould be dropped in the case of spatial dimensionD=1D=1.
If one wishes to couple to staggered fermions instead, an alternativeℤ2\mathbb{Z}_{2}LGT can be defined by a Hamiltonian of the formHfer=\displaystyle H_{\rm fer}=−g24​∑L∈ℒZL+m​∑𝐧∈ΛϵS𝐧​χS𝐧†​χS𝐧−λ​∑P∈𝒫∏L∈∂PXL\displaystyle\,-\frac{g^{2}}{4}\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}}Z_{L}+m\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\Lambda}\epsilon_{S_{\mathbf{n}}}\chi^{\dagger}_{S_{\mathbf{n}}}\chi_{S_{\mathbf{n}}}-\lambda\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\prod_{L\in\partial P}X_{L}+iκ2∑𝐧∈Λ∑μ=1D(χS𝐧†ηS𝐧μXL𝐧,μχS𝐧+μ^−h.c.),\displaystyle\,+i\frac{\kappa}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\Lambda}\sum_{\mu=1}^{D}\left(\chi^{\dagger}_{S_{\mathbf{n}}}\eta^{\mu}_{S_{\mathbf{n}}}X_{L_{\mathbf{n},\mu}}\chi_{S_{\mathbf{n}+\hat{\mu}}}-{\rm h.c.}\right)\,,(II.63)

whereχS\chi_{S}andχS†\chi_{S}^{\dagger}are fermion operators at siteSSsatisfying the standard canonical anti-commutation relations, andϵS𝐧\epsilon_{S_{\mathbf{n}}}andηS𝐧μ\eta^{\mu}_{S_{\mathbf{n}}}are site-dependent signatures defined asϵS𝐧=(−1)∑ν=1Dnν,ηS𝐧μ=(−1)∑ν=1μ−1nν.\displaystyle\epsilon_{S_{\mathbf{n}}}=(-1)^{\sum_{\nu=1}^{D}n_{\nu}},\quad\eta^{\mu}_{S_{\mathbf{n}}}=(-1)^{\sum_{\nu=1}^{\mu-1}n_{\nu}}.(II.64)

It is known that the fermionic Fock space can be mapped to qubits, where the fermion operators are expressed as spin operators via appropriate transformations.
The most well-known such transformation is the Jordan-Wigner transformation[44]which maps the Hamiltonian (II.2.1) into a local spin Hamiltonian forD=1D=1and a non-local spin Hamiltonian forD≥2D\geq 2.
For the11D application, see for instance[60], which can be adapted to our convention as the HamiltonianHfer=\displaystyle H_{\rm fer}=−g24​∑n=1NZLn−m2​∑n=1N(−1)n​ZSn\displaystyle\,-\frac{g^{2}}{4}\sum_{n=1}^{N}Z_{L_{n}}-\frac{m}{2}\sum_{n=1}^{N}(-1)^{n}Z_{S_{n}}−κ4​∑n=1N(XSn​XLn​XSn+1+YSn​XLn​YSn+1),\displaystyle\,-\frac{\kappa}{4}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\left(X_{S_{n}}X_{L_{n}}X_{S_{n+1}}+Y_{S_{n}}X_{L_{n}}Y_{S_{n+1}}\right)\,,(II.65)

where we used the fact that𝐧→n\mathbf{n}\to nis a single integer in11D.
The Hamiltonians (II.2.1) and (II.2.1) commute with all Gauss’s law operatorsG𝐧G_{\mathbf{n}}, implying that Gauss’s law is satisfied during the dynamics, even though there are states inℋ\mathcal{H}that are not gauge invariant, and therefore do not satisfy Gauss’s law.

For simplicity, in22D and higher dimensions, this paper exclusively uses (II.2.1) as ourℤ2\mathbb{Z}_{2}LGT formulation.444In11D, we may occasionally use (II.2.1) for illustrative purposes.Since our constructions are entirely kinematical and therefore apply automatically to allℤ2\mathbb{Z}_{2}LGTs on the same lattice, they are identical for all equivalent lattice Hilbert spaces, provided that the Gauss’s law conventions are chosen consistently with (II.59).

## II.2.2Gauge-Fixed Description

An alternative kinematical description is obtained by solving the Gauss’s law constraints explicitly.
This leads to a Hilbert space spanned entirely by the link variablesL𝐧,μ∈ℒL_{\mathbf{n},\mu}\in\mathcal{L}.
In this formulation, the kinematical Hilbert space is isomorphic to the gauge-invariant Hilbert spaceℋG\mathcal{H}_{G}(seeEq.II.61), and simply consists of one qubit per link, representing the electric flux.

Any Hamiltonian in the standard description can be converted to the gauge-fixed description by demanding that each Gauss’s law operatorG𝐧G_{\mathbf{n}}acts as identity on the gauge-fixed Hilbert space, and replacing all site operators appropriately.
For example, for the hard-core boson theory (II.2.1), we can substituteXS\displaystyle X_{S}→IS,\displaystyle\to I_{S},(II.66)ZS\displaystyle Z_{S}→∏L∈∂S∗ZL,\displaystyle\to\prod_{L\in\partial S^{*}}Z_{L},(II.67)

into (II.2.1) to obtain the gauge-fixed HamiltonianHgf=\displaystyle H_{\text{gf}}=−g24​∑L∈ℒZL−m2​∑𝐧∈Λ∏L∈∂S∗ZL\displaystyle\,-\frac{g^{2}}{4}\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}}Z_{L}-\frac{m}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\Lambda}\prod_{L\in\partial S^{*}}Z_{L}−λ​∑P∈𝒫∏L∈∂PXL−κ2​∑L∈ℒXL,\displaystyle\,-\lambda\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\prod_{L\in\partial P}X_{L}-\frac{\kappa}{2}\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}}X_{L}\,,(II.68)

where we reuse the product notation given asEq.II.60.
As before, if we are dealing with the case of one spatial dimension, then the plaquette term is discarded.

While the gauge-fixed formulation (II.2.2) requires fewer qubits than the standard formulation, the locality of the Hamiltonian can be substantially higher.
For this reason, the Hamiltonian of the standard formulation (II.2.1) may be a more feasible target for implementation on existing hardware, particularly in circumstances where the ability to perform multi-qubit gates is more of a bottleneck than the total number of available qubits.Table 2:List of notations for cubic lattice andℤ2\mathbb{Z}_{2}LGT in general dimensionSymbolNameΛ\Lambda(Eq.II.58)latticeDDspace dimension𝐧∈Λ\mathbf{n}\in\LambdacoordinatesS𝐧∈𝒮S_{\mathbf{n}}\in\mathcal{S}sitesL𝐧,μ∈ℒL_{\mathbf{n,\mu}}\in\mathcal{L}linksP∈𝒫P\in\mathcal{P}plaquettesG𝐧G_{\mathbf{n}}(Eq.II.59)Gauss’s lawHH(SectionII.2.1)hard-core boson HamiltonianHferH_{\text{fer}}(SectionII.2.1)staggered fermion HamiltonianHgfH_{\text{gf}}(SectionII.2.2)gauge-fixed bosonic Hamiltonianℋ\mathcal{H}full Hilbert spaceℋG\mathcal{H}_{G}(Eq.II.61)gauge-invariant Hilbert space

## IIIArbitrary-Distance Gauss’s Law Codes

## III.1Motivation: The RRW Code

In our standard formulation of theℤ2\mathbb{Z}_{2}LGT defined inSectionII.2, a violation of Gauss’s law manifests itself as a bit-flip error.
For instance, consider the11D lattice, where sitesSnS_{n}can be indexed by a single integer1≤n≤N1\leq n\leq N, and linksLnL_{n}no longer need to specify a directionμ\muin space.
A piece of the11D lattice is shown inFig.3.

The Gauss’s law operator at siteSnS_{n}takes the formGn≡ZLn−1​ZSn​ZLn,G_{n}\equiv Z_{L_{n-1}}Z_{S_{n}}Z_{L_{n}},(III.1)

which is diagonalized in the computational basis, and acts as identity on any gauge-invariant state.
Therefore, at the level of dynamics constrained by gauge invariance, this permits the locally-defined states|0⟩Ln−1​|0⟩Sn​|0⟩Ln,\displaystyle\ket{0}_{L_{n-1}}\ket{0}_{S_{n}}\ket{0}_{L_{n}},(III.2)|1⟩Ln−1​|1⟩Sn​|0⟩Ln,\displaystyle\ket{1}_{L_{n-1}}\ket{1}_{S_{n}}\ket{0}_{L_{n}},(III.3)|0⟩Ln−1​|1⟩Sn​|1⟩Ln,\displaystyle\ket{0}_{L_{n-1}}\ket{1}_{S_{n}}\ket{1}_{L_{n}},(III.4)|1⟩Ln−1​|0⟩Sn​|1⟩Ln,\displaystyle\ket{1}_{L_{n-1}}\ket{0}_{S_{n}}\ket{1}_{L_{n}},(III.5)

but forbids the locally-defined states|1⟩Ln−1​|0⟩Sn​|0⟩Ln,\displaystyle\ket{1}_{L_{n-1}}\ket{0}_{S_{n}}\ket{0}_{L_{n}},(III.6)|0⟩Ln−1​|1⟩Sn​|0⟩Ln,\displaystyle\ket{0}_{L_{n-1}}\ket{1}_{S_{n}}\ket{0}_{L_{n}},(III.7)|0⟩Ln−1​|0⟩Sn​|1⟩Ln,\displaystyle\ket{0}_{L_{n-1}}\ket{0}_{S_{n}}\ket{1}_{L_{n}},(III.8)|1⟩Ln−1​|1⟩Sn​|1⟩Ln,\displaystyle\ket{1}_{L_{n-1}}\ket{1}_{S_{n}}\ket{1}_{L_{n}},(III.9)

where every state in the latter group can be obtained via single bit-flip from some state(s) in the former group.Ln−1L_{n-1}SnS_{n}LnL_{n}Figure 3:Illustration of variables participating in a single Gauss’s law check for a lattice in11spatial dimension.

This is aclassicalrestriction, based solely on the values of the individual bits, with no regard to the phase information.
In other words, it merely restricts which computational basis states can be reached during gauge-invariant dynamics, without making reference to any superpositions of states.
To clarify this point, convert any computational basis state into a “lattice bit string” by writing the eigenvalues of all link and site variables in order from left to right:b≡S1​L1​S2​L2​⋯​Ln−1​Sn​Ln​⋯​SN​LN∈𝔽22​N.b\equiv S_{1}L_{1}S_{2}L_{2}\cdots L_{n-1}S_{n}L_{n}\cdots S_{N}L_{N}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2N}.(III.10)

The computational basis state|b⟩\ket{b}is always an eigenstate of the Gauss’s law operatorGnG_{n}, and it satisfies the Gauss’s law constraint if and only ifGn​|b⟩=|b⟩⇔Ln−1+Sn+Ln=0,G_{n}\ket{b}=\ket{b}\iff L_{n-1}+S_{n}+L_{n}=0,(III.11)

where addition is performed in𝔽2\mathbb{F}_{2}.

Since the Gauss law operator commutes with the Hamiltonian, any computational basis state that contributes during exact real-time evolution governed by the Hamiltonian (II.2.1) must necessarily satisfy theNNlinear constraints given by (III.11).
As long as the initial state is gauge invariant, the dynamics is constrained guaranteed to only explore the gauge-invariant “code” space.
For a periodic lattice containingNNsites in11D, this code can either be viewed as a classical[2​N,N,3][2N,N,3]code, or a⟦2​N,N,1⟧\llbracket 2N,N,1\rrbracketquantum code.

To understand this more clearly, note that a periodic11D lattice withNNsites contains2​N2Ndegrees of freedom (DOFs), considering both sites and links.
Depending on whether we think in classical or quantum terms, these DOFs are either bits or qubits.
Gauss’s law then provides a single linear constraint per vertex (forNNtotal constraints), which are parity checks at the classical level, or stabilizer generators at the quantum level.
This means there are exactly2​N−N=N2N-N=Nlogical DOFs left over when all constraints are applied.
Of course, we know exactly what thoseNNlogical DOFs physically represent: they are simply theNNlink variables, whose configuration uniquely determines the fermion content at all lattice sites.

The classical code distance is the minimum number of bit-flips needed to modify one codeword into a different codeword.
This is33, because in addition to flipping a prescribed linkLL, one must also flip the lattice sites at both endpoints ofLLto preserve Gauss’s law.
As discussed inSectionII.1.2, viewed as a quantum code the code distance is only 1, since only bit-flip (XX) errors can be detected, while phase-flip (ZZ) errors can not.

By concatenating this classical bit-flip code with a separate[3,1,3][3,1,3]code used to handle phase-flips, we obtain a full[2​N,N,3]∘[3,1,3]=⟦6​N,N,3⟧[2N,N,3]\circ[3,1,3]=\llbracket 6N,N,3\rrbracket(III.12)

stabilizer code—this is precisely theRRW code, originally introduced in[67].

To study what errors can and can not be detected, we consider bit-flip and phase-flip errors that can occur on the lattice.
In order for an error to be undetectable, itmusteither:
- •

Flip all33phases within an inner block; or
- •

Flip a bit on a sequence of consecutive blocks that constitutes a flux string (pair of matter excitations and link excitation between them) on the lattice.

Both of these possibilities are illustrated inFig.4, and it is easy to see that the corresponding Pauli errors will commute with the stabilizer generators of the concatenated code.
For additional intuition,Fig.4also shows an example of a higher-weight error that can not only be detected, but even corrected based on typical QEC protocols from the literature (e.g.[31,49]).
This point will be discussed in more detail inSectionIV.⋯\cdots⋯\cdots⋯\cdots⋯\cdots⋯\cdots⋯\cdotsZZZSn−1S_{n-1}SnS_{n}Sn+1S_{n+1}Sn+2S_{n+2}Ln−1L_{n-1}LnL_{n}Ln+1L_{n+1}⋯\cdots⋯\cdots⋯\cdots⋯\cdots⋯\cdots⋯\cdotsXXXXXSn−1S_{n-1}SnS_{n}Sn+1S_{n+1}Sn+2S_{n+2}Ln−1L_{n-1}LnL_{n}Ln+1L_{n+1}⋯\cdots⋯\cdots⋯\cdots⋯\cdots⋯\cdots⋯\cdotsZYZZZZZXSn−1S_{n-1}SnS_{n}Sn+1S_{n+1}Sn+2S_{n+2}Ln−1L_{n-1}LnL_{n}Ln+1L_{n+1}Figure 4:Different types of Pauli errors in the RRW code, shown on top of the physical qubits representing a portion of the11D lattice. Each link and site is represented with33physical qubits functioning as a[3,1,3][3,1,3]phase-flip code. Top: logical (undetectable)ZZ-type error on linkLnL_{n}. Middle: logical (undetectable)XX-type error that creates a flux string originating at siteSn−1S_{n-1}, spanning33linksLn−1L_{n-1},LnL_{n}, andLn+1L_{n+1}, and terminating at siteSn+2S_{n+2}. Bottom: higher-weightdetectable and correctableerror using all Pauli matrices,XX,YY, andZZ.

Note that for errors to be undetectable, several Pauli errors have to happen in close proximity.
Errors with sufficiently low density of affected qubits are easily handled by this code.
This is in fact a feature of many quantum error correcting codes, which are built out of local blocks of physical qubits comprising a set of logical qubits.
For errors to be undetectable, several errors have to occur in the same block.
One difference of the Gauss’ law codes discussed here is that there are no separated blocks that can be defined, since Gauss’ law always acts on neighbors on both “sides”.
This will be discussed more later.

As this construction will be extended to higher distances, it will be interesting to investigate how this locality continues to hold.

## III.2Extension to Arbitrary Distance

Now we come to our extension of the RRW code to higher distances.
The construction proceeds in two steps.
First, we construct the classical Gauss’s law code, capable of reaching arbitrary distance, that handles bit-flip errors in an analogous way to the original RRW construction.
Finally, to build a fully capable quantum code, we concatenate the classical Gauss’s law code with an arbitrarily chosen secondary code that can handle phase-flip errors.

The majority of the effort goes into extending the classical Gauss’s law code.
For this, we have to understand more deeply why the RRW code defined inSectionIII.1has distance33.
The standard formulation of theℤ2\mathbb{Z}_{2}LGT makes reference to site variablesS𝐧S_{\mathbf{n}}and link variablesL𝐧,μL_{\mathbf{n},\mu}.
At the classical level, a configuration of the lattice assigns a single bit to each variable, and each Gauss’s law constraint takes the form of the linear constraintS𝐧+∑μ=1D(L𝐧,μ+L𝐧−μ^,μ)=0,𝐧∈Λ.S_{\mathbf{n}}+\sum_{\mu=1}^{D}\left(L_{\mathbf{n},\mu}+L_{\mathbf{n}-\hat{\mu},\mu}\right)=0,\quad\mathbf{n}\in\Lambda.(III.13)

The set of configurations on the lattice that satisfy these constraints are geometrically represented by disjoint collections of open strings and closed strings.
An open string consists of a pair of matter excitations joined by a continuous, non-intersecting path of excited electric flux along the links of the lattice. Closed strings are similar, but they close into a loop, and therefore do not possess matter excitations.
These two possibilities are shown inFig.5.111101101111011011011011011011011011Figure 5:Examples of flux strings that correspond to classical lattice configurations that satisfy Gauss’s law. The bits for each link and site along the string paths are shown. Blue: open string, contains matter excitations at the endpoints. Green: closed string, contains no matter excitations.

As used by RRW, the key observation is that classical lattice configurations satisfying (III.13) can be interpreted as the codewords defining a classical code within the larger
space of all possible assignments of bits to the lattice variables.
The set of all possible assignments of bits is the binary vector space𝔽2(D+1)​N\mathbb{F}_{2}^{(D+1)N}, since there areD​NDNlinks andNNsites, and therefore(D+1)​N(D+1)Ntotal bits that need to be assigned in order to define a classical lattice configuration.
The set of codewordsCCis isomorphic to the linear subspaceC≅𝔽2D​N⊆𝔽2(D+1)​NC\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}^{DN}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{(D+1)N}.
The reduction in dimension byNNis precisely the result of theNNlinear constraints of the form (III.13).
The distance of this code is automatically33, because the smallest gauge invariant excitation that can be created is a pair of matter excitations on adjacent sites, separated by a single link of flux.555Technically, if the lattice has a periodic dimension spanned by just22links, there can also be a non-contractible flux string that winds around in this very short direction. To keep our discussion simple, we will neglect this possibility by assuming that the number of lattice sites in each direction is large enough.

An extension to higher distances can be motivated from this physical observation.
Namely, we duplicate the data on each site and link, so that the number of bit-flips needed to excite a flux string is increased.
For instance, if a single copy is made of every site and link variable, then it now requires66bit-flips to excite the smallest flux string, instead of the33bits that were needed in the previous case.
This example is shown inFig.6.111111111111Figure 6:Excitation of the smallest flux string, if there is an identical copy of every link and site variable on the lattice. This doubles the distance of the code from33to66.

The duplication increases the number of bits that we need to keep track of in a classical configuration.
A useful notation is to writeS𝐧(i)S^{(i)}_{\mathbf{n}}for theithi^{\mathrm{th}}copy of the site variableS𝐧S_{\mathbf{n}}, andL𝐧,μ(j)L^{(j)}_{\mathbf{n},\mu}for thejthj^{\mathrm{th}}copy of the link variableL𝐧,μL_{\mathbf{n},\mu}.
In the most general case, the number of repetitions of each site and link variable can be chosen independently across the lattice.
To describe this general case, we will find it useful to introduce integersp𝐧∈ℕp_{\mathbf{n}}\in\mathbb{N}andq𝐧,μ∈ℕq_{\mathbf{n},\mu}\in\mathbb{N}that specify the number of times the site variableS𝐧S_{\mathbf{n}}and link variableL𝐧,μL_{\mathbf{n},\mu}have been repeated, respectively.

To understand the resulting code, we need to understand the Gauss’s law constraints in terms of the duplicated variables.
For instance, seeFig.7for an example vertex where everyp𝐧=2p_{\mathbf{n}}=2and everyq𝐧,μ=2q_{\mathbf{n},\mu}=2.S𝐧(1)S^{(1)}_{\mathbf{n}}S𝐧(2)S^{(2)}_{\mathbf{n}}L𝐧,x(1)L^{(1)}_{\mathbf{n},x}L𝐧,x(2)L^{(2)}_{\mathbf{n},x}L𝐧−x^,x(1)L^{(1)}_{\mathbf{n}-\hat{x},x}L𝐧−x^,x(2)L^{(2)}_{\mathbf{n}-\hat{x},x}L𝐧,y(1)L^{(1)}_{\mathbf{n},y}L𝐧,y(2)L^{(2)}_{\mathbf{n},y}L𝐧−y^,y(1)L^{(1)}_{\mathbf{n}-\hat{y},y}L𝐧−y^,y(2)L^{(2)}_{\mathbf{n}-\hat{y},y}Figure 7:Example vertex showing labeling of duplicate lattice variables. Several copies of Gauss’s law constraints are satisfied between all the duplicated variables.

It turns out that there are two mathematically equivalent ways to express the linear constraints enforced at a vertex𝐧∈Λ\mathbf{n}\in\Lambdaby Gauss’s law, under the assumption that the classical lattice data has simply been copied into each respective duplicated variable.
The first way to express this is to imagine selecting a representative value for the lattice variable from each link and site, and then demanding that a Gauss’s law constraint holds between them.
Enforcing this for every possible choice of representatives gives a constraint of the formS𝐧(a)+∑μ=1D(L𝐧,μ(bμ)+L𝐧−μ^,μ(cμ))=0,S^{(a)}_{\mathbf{n}}+\sum_{\mu=1}^{D}\left(L^{(b_{\mu})}_{\mathbf{n},\mu}+L^{(c_{\mu})}_{\mathbf{n}-\hat{\mu},\mu}\right)=0,(III.14)

where1≤a≤p𝐧1\leq a\leq p_{\mathbf{n}}is the index that selects any of the possible representatives ofS𝐧S_{\mathbf{n}}, and the sequences of indices1≤bμ≤q𝐧,μ1\leq b_{\mu}\leq q_{\mathbf{n},\mu}and1≤cμ≤q𝐧−μ^,μ1\leq c_{\mu}\leq q_{\mathbf{n}-\hat{\mu},\mu}select any of the possible representatives of the surrounding link variables.
The number of constraints that can be written down of the form (III.14) is given by the combinatorial factor#​constraints=p𝐧×∏μ=1Dq𝐧,μ​q𝐧−μ^,μ,\#\text{ constraints}=p_{\mathbf{n}}\times\prod_{\mu=1}^{D}q_{\mathbf{n},\mu}q_{\mathbf{n}-\hat{\mu},\mu},(III.15)

but it turns out that the number ofindependentlinear constraints within this collection is only#​indep. constraints=−2​D+p𝐧+∑μ=1D(q𝐧,μ+q𝐧−μ^,μ).\#\text{ indep. constraints}=-2D+p_{\mathbf{n}}+\sum_{\mu=1}^{D}\left(q_{\mathbf{n},\mu}+q_{\mathbf{n}-\hat{\mu},\mu}\right).(III.16)

To understand why (III.16) holds true, we come to the second way in which the linear constraints can be expressed at any vertex𝐧∈Λ\mathbf{n}\in\Lambda.
Namely, in homogeneous form,S𝐧(1)+∑μ=1D(L𝐧,μ(1)+L𝐧−μ^,μ(1))\displaystyle S^{(1)}_{\mathbf{n}}+\sum_{\mu=1}^{D}\left(L^{(1)}_{\mathbf{n},\mu}+L^{(1)}_{\mathbf{n}-\hat{\mu},\mu}\right)=0,\displaystyle=0,(III.17)S𝐧(a)+S𝐧(a′)\displaystyle S^{(a)}_{\mathbf{n}}+S^{(a^{\prime})}_{\mathbf{n}}=0,\displaystyle=0,(III.18)L𝐧,μ(b)+L𝐧,μ(b′)\displaystyle L^{(b)}_{\mathbf{n},\mu}+L^{(b^{\prime})}_{\mathbf{n},\mu}=0,\displaystyle=0,(III.19)L𝐧−μ^,μ(c)+L𝐧−μ^,μ(c′)\displaystyle L^{(c)}_{\mathbf{n}-\hat{\mu},\mu}+L^{(c^{\prime})}_{\mathbf{n}-\hat{\mu},\mu}=0,\displaystyle=0,(III.20)

where it’s important to keep in mind that addition is still defined over𝔽2\mathbb{F}_{2}.
The linear constraints (III.17) illustrate that there is really only a single Gauss’s law constraint, applied to one set of representative variables (for which we have arbitrarily chosen the index11).
The rest of the constraints are simply the statement that every representative of a lattice variable equals every other representative.
But this is basically just a classical repetition code on the duplicate variables.
SettingMMbinary variables equal to each other requiresM−1M-1constraints.
Therefore, the number of independent constraints is obtained by summing#\displaystyle\#indep. constraints=p𝐧−1+∑μ=1D(q𝐧,μ−1)\displaystyle\qquad=p_{\mathbf{n}}-1+\sum_{\mu=1}^{D}\left(q_{\mathbf{n},\mu}-1\right)+∑μ=1D(q𝐧−μ^,μ−1)+1,\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum_{\mu=1}^{D}\left(q_{\mathbf{n}-\hat{\mu},\mu}-1\right)+1\,,=−2​D+p𝐧+∑μ=1D(q𝐧,μ+q𝐧−μ^,μ)\displaystyle\qquad=\,-2D+p_{\mathbf{n}}+\sum_{\mu=1}^{D}\left(q_{\mathbf{n},\mu}+q_{\mathbf{n}-\hat{\mu},\mu}\right)(III.21)

where the final+1+1comes from including the single representative constraint for Gauss’s law.
Indeed, this exactly simplifies to (III.16), for any lattice site𝐧\mathbf{n}.

Knowing the number of independent constraints at any vertex𝐧∈Λ\mathbf{n}\in\Lambdaputs us in the perfect position to count the number of logical bits encoded by this code.
