This is an important calculation to perform as a consistency check to make sure that our duplication procedure did not inadvertently add any logical DOFs that were not already present in the originalℤ2\mathbb{Z}_{2}LGT.
To count the number of logical DOFs, we need to knowrr, the number of rows in the full-rank parity-check matrixHHfor this code.
Naïvely, we could sum the number of independent linear constraints per site according to (III.16), over all sites𝐧∈Λ\mathbf{n}\in\Lambda,rnaïve\displaystyle r_{\text{na\"{i}ve}}=∑𝐧∈Λ(−2​D+p𝐧+∑μ=1D(q𝐧,μ+q𝐧−μ^,μ))\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\Lambda}\left(-2D+p_{\mathbf{n}}+\sum_{\mu=1}^{D}\left(q_{\mathbf{n},\mu}+q_{\mathbf{n}-\hat{\mu},\mu}\right)\right)(III.22)=−2​D​N+∑𝐧∈Λp𝐧+2​∑𝐧∈Λ∑μ=1Dq𝐧,μ.\displaystyle=-2DN+\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\Lambda}p_{\mathbf{n}}+2\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\Lambda}\sum_{\mu=1}^{D}q_{\mathbf{n},\mu}.(III.23)

But note that not all constraints are independent.
According to (III.17), all representatives of any link adjacent to a site𝐧∈Λ\mathbf{n}\in\Lambdaare set equal to each other by the vertex constraints at that site.
When collecting together this set of constraints for every lattice site, we double count those link constraints—once for each endpoint of the link.
This is the only double counting present, as a single representative of Gauss’s law is independent at every lattice site, and the constraints equating all copies of a given site variable are also independent at different sites.
Therefore, the amount of over counting is precisely given by the number of constraints that equate the copies of each link variable, i.e.,#​over counted\displaystyle\#\text{ over counted}=∑𝐧∈Λ∑μ=1D(q𝐧,μ−1)\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\Lambda}\sum_{\mu=1}^{D}\left(q_{\mathbf{n},\mu}-1\right)(III.24)=−D​N+∑n∈Λ∑μ=1Dq𝐧,μ,\displaystyle=-DN+\sum_{n\in\Lambda}\sum_{\mu=1}^{D}q_{\mathbf{n},\mu},(III.25)

when summed over all lattice sites.

The number of independent parity checks is thereforer\displaystyle r=rnaïve−#​over counted\displaystyle=r_{\text{na\"{i}ve}}-\#\text{ over counted}(III.26)=−D​N+∑𝐧∈Λp𝐧+∑𝐧∈Λ∑μ=1Dq𝐧,μ.\displaystyle=-DN+\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\Lambda}p_{\mathbf{n}}+\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\Lambda}\sum_{\mu=1}^{D}q_{\mathbf{n},\mu}.(III.27)

Finally, note thatn=∑𝐧∈Λp𝐧+∑𝐧∈Λ∑μ=1Dq𝐧,μn=\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\Lambda}p_{\mathbf{n}}+\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\Lambda}\sum_{\mu=1}^{D}q_{\mathbf{n},\mu}(III.28)

is simply the total number of physical bits used by this code, since it adds up the number of duplicates used by every link and site variable.
The number of independent logical DOFs used by this code is then exactlyk\displaystyle k=n−r\displaystyle=n-r(III.29)=D​N,\displaystyle=DN,(III.30)

which indeed agrees with our expectation that the independent kinematical DOFs are in bijective correspondence with the originalD​NDNlink variables in the standard formulation of theℤ2\mathbb{Z}_{2}LGT.

Having performed this consistency check, we can now summarize the classical construction of this code with definitionIII.1and lemmaIII.2.

## Definition III.1(Classical Gauss’s Law Code).

Assume we are given positive integersp𝐧,q𝐧,μ∈ℕp_{\mathbf{n}},q_{\mathbf{n},\mu}\in\mathbb{N}for every𝐧∈Λ\mathbf{n}\in\Lambdaandμ∈{1,…,D}\mu\in\{1,\dots,D\}.
Letnndenote the total number of physical bits, defined byn=∑𝐧∈Λp𝐧+∑𝐧∈Λ∑μ=1Dq𝐧,μ.n=\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\Lambda}p_{\mathbf{n}}+\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\Lambda}\sum_{\mu=1}^{D}q_{\mathbf{n},\mu}.(III.31)

Suppose that we identify the binary vector space𝔽2n\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}with assignments of bits to duplicated lattice variablesS𝐧(i),L𝐧,μ(j)∈𝔽2,S^{(i)}_{\mathbf{n}},L^{(j)}_{\mathbf{n},\mu}\in\mathbb{F}_{2},(III.32)

where1≤i≤p𝐧1\leq i\leq p_{\mathbf{n}}and1≤j≤q𝐧,μ1\leq j\leq q_{\mathbf{n},\mu}.
Then theclassical Gauss’s law codebuilt from the integersp𝐧p_{\mathbf{n}}andq𝐧,μq_{\mathbf{n},\mu}is the linear subspaceC⊆𝔽2nC\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}satisfying the constraintsS𝐧(a)+∑μ=1D(L𝐧,μ(bμ)+L𝐧−μ^,μ(cμ))=0,S^{(a)}_{\mathbf{n}}+\sum_{\mu=1}^{D}\left(L^{(b_{\mu})}_{\mathbf{n},\mu}+L^{(c_{\mu})}_{\mathbf{n}-\hat{\mu},\mu}\right)=0,(III.33)

for every integer1≤a≤p𝐧1\leq a\leq p_{\mathbf{n}}, and every pair of integer sequencesbμb_{\mu}andcμc_{\mu}whose values satisfy1≤bμ≤q𝐧,μ1\leq b_{\mu}\leq q_{\mathbf{n},\mu}and1≤cμ≤q𝐧−μ^,μ1\leq c_{\mu}\leq q_{\mathbf{n}-\hat{\mu},\mu}.

## Lemma III.2(Alternative Characterization).

Assume we are given positive integersp𝐧,q𝐧,μ∈ℕp_{\mathbf{n}},q_{\mathbf{n},\mu}\in\mathbb{N}for every𝐧∈Λ\mathbf{n}\in\Lambdaandμ∈{1,…,D}\mu\in\{1,\dots,D\}.
Letnndenote the total number of physical bits, defined byn=∑𝐧∈Λp𝐧+∑𝐧∈Λ∑μ=1Dq𝐧,μ.n=\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\Lambda}p_{\mathbf{n}}+\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\Lambda}\sum_{\mu=1}^{D}q_{\mathbf{n},\mu}.(III.34)

Suppose that we identify the binary vector space𝔽2n\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}with assignments of bits to duplicated lattice variablesS𝐧(i),L𝐧,μ(j)∈𝔽2,S^{(i)}_{\mathbf{n}},L^{(j)}_{\mathbf{n},\mu}\in\mathbb{F}_{2},(III.35)

where1≤i≤p𝐧1\leq i\leq p_{\mathbf{n}}and1≤j≤q𝐧,μ1\leq j\leq q_{\mathbf{n},\mu}.
LetC⊆𝔽2nC\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}denote the linear subspace satisfying the constraintsS𝐧(1)+∑μ=1D(L𝐧,μ(1)+L𝐧−μ^,μ(1))\displaystyle S^{(1)}_{\mathbf{n}}+\sum_{\mu=1}^{D}\left(L^{(1)}_{\mathbf{n},\mu}+L^{(1)}_{\mathbf{n}-\hat{\mu},\mu}\right)=0,\displaystyle=0,(III.36)S𝐧(a)+S𝐧(a′)\displaystyle S^{(a)}_{\mathbf{n}}+S^{(a^{\prime})}_{\mathbf{n}}=0,\displaystyle=0,(III.37)L𝐧,μ(b)+L𝐧,μ(b′)\displaystyle L^{(b)}_{\mathbf{n},\mu}+L^{(b^{\prime})}_{\mathbf{n},\mu}=0,\displaystyle=0,(III.38)L𝐧−μ^,μ(c)+L𝐧−μ^,μ(c′)\displaystyle L^{(c)}_{\mathbf{n}-\hat{\mu},\mu}+L^{(c^{\prime})}_{\mathbf{n}-\hat{\mu},\mu}=0,\displaystyle=0,(III.39)

for every pair of integers1≤a,a′≤p𝐧1\leq a,a^{\prime}\leq p_{\mathbf{n}}, every pair of integers1≤b,b′≤q𝐧,μ1\leq b,b^{\prime}\leq q_{\mathbf{n},\mu}, and every pair of integers1≤c,c′≤q𝐧−μ^,μ1\leq c,c^{\prime}\leq q_{\mathbf{n}-\hat{\mu},\mu}.
ThenCCis exactly the classical Gauss’s law code defined by the collection of integersp𝐧p_{\mathbf{n}}andq𝐧,μq_{\mathbf{n},\mu}.

We currently know the number of physical and logical bits used by the classical Gauss’s law code defined from a collection of integersp𝐧p_{\mathbf{n}}andq𝐧,μq_{\mathbf{n},\mu}.
But we have not yet discussed the code distance in this general framework, apart from the initial motivation that led us to introduce duplications of lattice variables in the first place.

As we saw inFig.6, the distance is related to gauge-invariant excitations that correspond to flux strings on the lattice.
To create any such excitation, all the bits of every participating lattice variable must be flipped.
The specific lattice variables involved will depend on whether we have an open string or closed string, and the specific shape of that string.

An open stringα\alphacorresponds to a continuous, non-intersecting path of links between two lattice sites𝐧,𝐧′∈Λ\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}^{\prime}\in\Lambdawith𝐧≠𝐧′\mathbf{n}\neq\mathbf{n^{\prime}}.
For a path spanningℓ\elllinks, we can writeL𝐧𝟏,μ1,L𝐧𝟐,μ2,…​L𝐧ℓ,μℓL_{\mathbf{n_{1}},\mu_{1}},L_{\mathbf{n_{2}},\mu_{2}},\dots L_{\mathbf{n_{\ell}},\mu_{\ell}}for the links on this path.
In this case, the weight of the logical error corresponding to exciting this open string is given bywt(α)=p𝐧+q𝐧𝟏,μ1+q𝐧𝟐,μ2+⋯+q𝐧ℓ,μℓ+p𝐧′.\operatorname*{wt}(\alpha)=p_{\mathbf{n}}+q_{\mathbf{n_{1}},\mu_{1}}+q_{\mathbf{n_{2}},\mu_{2}}+\cdots+q_{\mathbf{n_{\ell}},\mu_{\ell}}+p_{\mathbf{n^{\prime}}}.(III.40)

This is the number of bit-flips needed to flip every copy of each lattice variable involved in the stringα\alpha; namely, the two lattice sites at the endpoints, and all links along the path.

A closed stringβ\betacorresponds to a continuous, non-intersecting closed path of links.
For a closed path spanningℓ\elllinks, we can again writeL𝐧𝟏,μ1,L𝐧𝟐,μ2,…​L𝐧ℓ,μℓL_{\mathbf{n_{1}},\mu_{1}},L_{\mathbf{n_{2}},\mu_{2}},\dots L_{\mathbf{n_{\ell}},\mu_{\ell}}for the links along this path.
The weight in this case is given bywt(β)=q𝐧𝟏,μ1+q𝐧𝟐,μ2+⋯+q𝐧ℓ,μℓ,\operatorname*{wt}(\beta)=q_{\mathbf{n_{1}},\mu_{1}}+q_{\mathbf{n_{2}},\mu_{2}}+\cdots+q_{\mathbf{n_{\ell}},\mu_{\ell}},(III.41)

which reflects the fact that there are no matter excitations on a closed string.

The classical code distance is simply the minimum weight of any flux string, taken over all open strings and closed strings on the lattice.
This is summarized by LemmaIII.3.

## Lemma III.3(Classical Gauss’s Law Code Distance).

LetCCbe the classical Gauss’s law code defined by the collection of integersp𝐧,q𝐧,μ∈ℕp_{\mathbf{n}},q_{\mathbf{n},\mu}\in\mathbb{N}.
Letwo≡minα​wt(α)w_{o}\equiv\min_{\alpha}\operatorname*{wt}(\alpha)(III.42)

denote the minimum weight of any open stringα\alpha, and letwc≡minβ​wt(β)w_{c}\equiv\min_{\beta}\operatorname*{wt}(\beta)(III.43)

denote the minimum weight of any closed stringβ\beta.
Then the code distance ofCCis exactlyd=min⁡(wo,wc).d=\min(w_{o},w_{c}).(III.44)

Note that for the case where allp𝐧=q𝐧,μ=pp_{\mathbf{n}}=q_{\mathbf{n},\mu}=pequal, one findswo=3​pw_{o}=3p,wc=4​pw_{c}=4p, givingd=3​pd=3p.
For other choices ofp𝐧p_{\mathbf{n}}andq𝐧,μq_{\mathbf{n},\mu}, however, other values ofddcan be achieved.
For example, usingq𝐧,μ=1q_{\mathbf{n},\mu}=1, whilepeven=1p_{\rm even}=1,podd=3p_{\rm odd}=3giveswo=wc=d=4w_{o}=w_{c}=d=4, as can be seen inFig.8.
From this example, one also notices the important fact that the minimum must be taken over flux strings ofeverypossible length on the lattice.
The smallest string measured according to lattice units will not necessarily have the smallest weight.Sn−1S_{n-1}Ln−1L_{n-1}SnS_{n}LnL_{n}Sn+1S_{n+1}Figure 8:Example where a longer string has lower weight. The number of copies of each lattice variable are given by(pn−1,qn−1,pn,qn,pn+1)=(1,1,3,1,1)(p_{n-1},q_{n-1},p_{n},q_{n},p_{n+1})=(1,1,3,1,1). The length-22string shown in blue has weight44, while the length-11strings that can be made here would both have weight55.

Understanding how to choose the valuesp𝐧p_{\mathbf{n}}andq𝐧,μq_{\mathbf{n},\mu}to obtain a given valueddin the most efficient way will be discussed inSectionIII.3.

Having discussed our construction of classical Gauss’s law codes to arbitrary distance, all that remains is to form a full quantum code by concatenating with an appropriately chosen inner code.
InSectionII, we opted for a basic definition of concatenation (see DefinitionII.19) which functions purely as an operation between two classical codes, where one code handles bit-flips and the other handles phase-flips.
Therefore, to keep the discussion both accessible and concise, we write DefinitionIII.4under the assumption that the concatenation is performed using another classical code.

## Definition III.4(Quantum Gauss’s Law Code).

LetCout=[nout,kout,dout]C_{\mathrm{out}}=[n_{\mathrm{out}},k_{\mathrm{out}},d_{\mathrm{out}}]be a classical Gauss’s law code, and letCin=[nin,1,din]C_{\mathrm{in}}=[n_{\mathrm{in}},1,d_{\mathrm{in}}]be an arbitrary classical code that encodes a single logical bit.
Then the concatenationCout∘Cin=⟦nout​nin,kout,min⁡(dout,din)⟧C_{\mathrm{out}}\circ C_{\mathrm{in}}=\llbracket n_{\mathrm{out}}n_{\mathrm{in}},k_{\mathrm{out}},\min(d_{\mathrm{out}},d_{\mathrm{in}})\rrbracket(III.45)

is called thequantum Gauss’s law codebuilt fromCoutC_{\mathrm{out}}andCinC_{\mathrm{in}}.

It is worth noting that the construction of the full quantum code works with any general concatenation strategy.
This does lead to a few notable differences between quantum Gauss’s law codes defined by the basic concatenation strategy (DefinitionIII.4) and those obtained from other choices of concatenation.
For instance, one may choose a quantum codeCin=⟦nin,kin=nout,din⟧C_{\mathrm{in}}=\llbracket n_{\mathrm{in}},k_{\mathrm{in}}=n_{\mathrm{out}},d_{\mathrm{in}}\rrbracketas an inner code, identify its logical qubits with the physical qubits ofCoutC_{\mathrm{out}}, and replace the Pauli operators in the stabilizers ofCoutC_{\mathrm{out}}with logical Pauli operators ofCinC_{\mathrm{in}}. This can lead to a more compact encoding, i.e., with a higher encoding ratek/nk/n. The construction for the code distance three was given in Ref.[73].
However, the stabilizers after concatenation may have weights that grow with the lattice sizeNN, making their measurement more costly.
On the other hand, DefinitionIII.4alwaysproduces a quantumlow-density parity check(LDPC) code, meaning that the weight of stabilizer generators and the number of stabilizers acting on each qubit are bounded independently ofNN.

Therefore, the class of quantum Gauss’s law codes defined by concatenation in the form of DefinitionIII.4is a reasonable target for further investigation.
Our optimality theorems inSectionIII.3for the encoding rate—specifically TheoremsIII.5andIII.6—depend mostly on our construction of the classical Gauss’s law code, as elaborated further inAppendixA.
Therefore, these results suffice to provide an insight into what we can expect from any concatenated construction for a quantum Gauss’s law code.

## III.3Optimal Encoding Rate

Now we come to the question of the optimal encoding ratek/nk/nfor quantum Gauss’s law codes defined by our previous class of constructions (DefinitionsIII.1andIII.4).
Equivalently, we are interested in determining which specific choice of multiplicitiesp𝐧p_{\mathbf{n}}andq𝐧,μq_{\mathbf{n},\mu}is necessary to minimize the total number of physical qubitsnn, subject to a desired code distanceddand fixed lattice sizeNN.
The answer can get quite messy to write down, due to edge cases that depend on the parity of lattice sites, and so on—but the underlying logic is always the same.
In order to keep our discussion clean and concise, and to ensure that the logic is easy to see, we will make a few simplifying assumptions:
- (i)

We assume that the number of lattice sitesNμN_{\mu}is even along every axisμ∈{1,2,…,D}\mu\in\{1,2,\dots,D\}.
- (ii)

We assume that the number of lattice sitesNμN_{\mu}is larger than the code distanceddalong every axisμ\mu.

Assumption (i) is a standard requirement for theℤ2\mathbb{Z}_{2}LGT coupled to staggered fermions; and while it is not strictly necessary for a more generic theory, it will help to write a relatively simple expression for the optimal choice ofp𝐧p_{\mathbf{n}}andq𝐧,μq_{\mathbf{n},\mu}, without getting into distracting technicalities.
Assumption (ii) is useful just for the sake of ensuring that a non-contractible string doesn’t contribute to the optimization procedure—as long as everyNμ≥dN_{\mu}\geq d, it is guaranteed that every non-contractible string already has weight≥d\geq d, and therefore there are no constraints placed onp𝐧p_{\mathbf{n}}andq𝐧,μq_{\mathbf{n},\mu}by such strings.
For typical lattices that are used to extract interesting physics, this constraint is always satisfied.

We discuss only the11D and22D cases in explicit detail.
The11D case is special because there are no plaquettes, and therefore all logical bit-flip errors arise from open strings.
The22D case introduces the full complexity of the problem, requiring consideration of both open strings and closed strings.
The extension to higher dimensions follows straightforwardly once the22D case is understood, and we do not write it explicitly here.
For the technical proofs of our optimality theorems, seeAppendixA.

## III.3.111D Case

The intuition for the optimally efficient code construction in11D is as follows: the smallest possible gauge-invariant excitation is formed from exciting a pair of matter sites separated by11link.
This excitation requirestwolattice sites to be flipped, but onlyonelink to be flipped.
Therefore, there is a sense in which the code distance is affected more by the difficulty of flipping lattice sites, in comparison to that of flipping links.
Of course, the situation is slightly complicated due to the fact that longer strings also need to be considered, as inFig.8; these technical details are taken into account in TheoremIII.5.
In fact, we find that to obtain the code that minimizes the number of physical qubits needed, we must indeed delegate the entirety of all duplication to just the lattice sites, prior to concatenation.

## Theorem III.5(Optimal Gauss’s Law Code in11D).

Letd≥3d\geq 3andN≥dN\geq dbe given, withNNeven.
Suppose thatCout∘Cin=⟦n,k,d⟧C_{\mathrm{out}}\circ C_{\mathrm{in}}=\llbracket n,k,d\rrbracketis a quantum Gauss’s law code (see Def.III.4) with distanceddfor the11Dℤ2\mathbb{Z}_{2}LGT defined by Hamiltonian (II.2.1) onNNlattice sites.
Then the following two results hold:
- (i)

The encoding rate is bounded bykn≤2d​(d+1)\frac{k}{n}\leq\frac{2}{d(d+1)}(III.46)
- (ii)

There exists a quantum Gauss’s law codeCout∘CinC_{\mathrm{out}}\circ C_{\mathrm{in}}(i.e., a specific choice of duplication variables(p𝐧,q𝐧)(p_{\mathbf{n}},q_{\mathbf{n}})and a classical codeCinC_{\mathrm{in}})
that saturates the bound (III.46).

In other words, this theorem states that the optimal Gauss’s law code has⟦N​d​(d+1)/2,N,d⟧\llbracket Nd(d+1)/2,N,d\rrbracket.
The proof of theoremIII.5can be found inAppendixA.
In this section, we provide just the optimal code construction.
The concatenation step is straightforward—takeCoutC_{\mathrm{out}}to be a classical distance-ddGauss’s law code for bit-flips using as few physical bits as possible, and takeCinC_{\mathrm{in}}to be a classical repetition codeCin=[d,1,d]C_{\mathrm{in}}=[d,1,d]for phase-flips.

The interesting part of the theorem comes from considering how to chooseCoutC_{\mathrm{out}}with the minimum possible number of physical bits.
Recall that the classical Gauss’s law codeCoutC_{\mathrm{out}}for a11D lattice is defined entirely from a sequence of integers,pnp_{n}andqnq_{n}.
Therefore, specifyingpnp_{n}andqnq_{n}for each lattice site indexn∈Λ={1,2,…,N}n\in\Lambda=\{1,2,\dots,N\}is enough to define the classical code.
An optimal code is obtained by the following selection:pn\displaystyle p_{n}={⌈d−12⌉,n≡0(mod2),⌈d2⌉−1,n≡1(mod2),\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\lceil\frac{d-1}{2}\rceil,&n\equiv 0\pmod{2},\\
\lceil\frac{d}{2}\rceil-1,&n\equiv 1\pmod{2},\end{cases}(III.47)qn\displaystyle q_{n}=1.\displaystyle=1.(III.48)

The effect of this choice is to condition the duplication of lattice sites by the parity ofdd.
Ifddis odd, then all sites are uniformly duplicated by the same amount.
Ifddis even, then the odd-numbered sites are duplicated with one less copy than the even-numbered sites.
These two cases are shown inFig.9.
It’s easy to see that the classical code distanceddis recovered in both cases.
Upon concatenation withCin=[d,1,d]C_{\mathrm{in}}=[d,1,d], it’s also easy to verify that the bound from theoremIII.5is saturated, regardless of the parity ofdd.S2​i−1S_{2i-1}L2​i−1L_{2i-1}S2​iS_{2i}L2​iL_{2i}S2​i+1S_{2i+1}S2​i−1S_{2i-1}L2​i−1L_{2i-1}S2​iS_{2i}L2​iL_{2i}S2​i+1S_{2i+1}Figure 9:Duplicating lattice variables for an optimal code. Each link or site (and any duplicate) corresponds to a single bit in the outer bit-flip code. The minimum-weight logical error is shown in both cases (blue), which corresponds to a flux string excitation on the lattice. Top: distanced=4d=4(even case). Bottom: distanced=5d=5(odd case).

Roughly speaking, it works to choosepnp_{n}with the split cases in (III.47) due to the fact that a length-11flux string always uses exactly one endpoint from both the even and odd sub-lattices; and therefore, the code distance increases by11upon incrementing the site multiplicity on just one of the sub-lattices.

## III.3.222D Case

Deducing the optimal code construction in the22D case is substantially more complicated than the11D case, due to the presence of closed strings.
Nevertheless, there is a generalization of the methodology used in the11D case that applies directly to the22D case and higher dimensions.
The idea is to separately keep track of the minimum weightswow_{o}andwcw_{c}for open strings and closed strings, respectively.
By partitioning the lattice variables based on parity, we can controlwow_{o}andwcw_{c}, and therefore also the code distance.

For instance, we partition the set of lattice sites𝒮\mathcal{S}as𝒮\displaystyle\mathcal{S}=𝒮1⊔𝒮2,\displaystyle=\mathcal{S}_{1}\sqcup\mathcal{S}_{2},(III.49)𝒮1\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{1}≡{S𝐧∣n1+n2≡0(mod2)},\displaystyle\equiv\{S_{\mathbf{n}}\mid n_{1}+n_{2}\equiv 0\pmod{2}\},(III.50)𝒮2\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{2}≡{S𝐧∣n1+n2≡1(mod2)}.\displaystyle\equiv\{S_{\mathbf{n}}\mid n_{1}+n_{2}\equiv 1\pmod{2}\}.(III.51)

This is the standard staggering into the even and odd sub-lattices.
The point is that when all multiplicities on a sub-lattice of staggered sites is incremented,wow_{o}is expected to increase by11(exactly as in the11D case), whilewcw_{c}remains unchanged.

A similar property can be engineered for minimum-weight closed strings, by duplicating links.
Specifically, one can partition the set of linksℒ\mathcal{L}into44groups:ℒ\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=ℒ1⊔ℒ2⊔ℒ3⊔ℒ4,\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{1}\sqcup\mathcal{L}_{2}\sqcup\mathcal{L}_{3}\sqcup\mathcal{L}_{4},(III.52)ℒ1\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1}≡{L𝐧,μ∣n2≡0(mod2),μ=x},\displaystyle\equiv\{L_{\mathbf{n},\mu}\mid n_{2}\equiv 0\pmod{2},\quad\mu=x\},(III.53)ℒ2\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{2}≡{L𝐧,μ∣n2≡1(mod2),μ=x},\displaystyle\equiv\{L_{\mathbf{n},\mu}\mid n_{2}\equiv 1\pmod{2},\quad\mu=x\},(III.54)ℒ3\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{3}≡{L𝐧,μ∣n1≡0(mod2),μ=y},\displaystyle\equiv\{L_{\mathbf{n},\mu}\mid n_{1}\equiv 0\pmod{2},\quad\mu=y\},(III.55)ℒ4\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{4}≡{L𝐧,μ∣n1≡1(mod2),μ=y}.\displaystyle\equiv\{L_{\mathbf{n},\mu}\mid n_{1}\equiv 1\pmod{2},\quad\mu=y\}.(III.56)

To put it succinctly, the links are categorized by: (i) whether they are horizontal or vertical; and (ii) the parity of the transverse coordinate.
This partition has the property that every plaquette on the lattice uses exactly one link from each set (ℒ1\mathcal{L}_{1},ℒ2\mathcal{L}_{2},ℒ3\mathcal{L}_{3},andℒ4\mathcal{L}_{4}) of this partition.
This ultimately has the effect that incrementing the multiplicities of every link in a fixed setℒi\mathcal{L}_{i}increaseswcw_{c}by11, while leavingwow_{o}unchanged.
SeeFig.10for an illustration that summarizes the partitioning.Figure 10:Simultaneous partitioning of lattice sites and links. The two sub-lattices are colored blue (𝒮1\mathcal{S}_{1}) and orange (𝒮2\mathcal{S}_{2}). The partitioned links are colored as green (ℒ1\mathcal{L}_{1}), red (ℒ2\mathcal{L}_{2}), yellow (ℒ3\mathcal{L}_{3}), and purple (ℒ4\mathcal{L}_{4}). Note that every length-11open string touches both sub-lattices (blue and orange), and every plaquette uses all the link colors (green, red, yellow, and purple).

As in the11D case, there are subtleties regarding open strings and closed strings with larger size, and these technicalities are taken into account to yield TheoremIII.6.
We find that the optimal code can be built from this incremental procedure, performed in parallel on both lattice sites and links, with minor modifications designed to achieve exact optimality.

## Theorem III.6(Optimal Gauss’s Law Code in22D).

Letd≥3d\geq 3andNx,Ny≥dN_{x},N_{y}\geq dbe given, withNx,NyN_{x},N_{y}even.
Suppose thatCout∘Cin=⟦n,k,d⟧C_{\mathrm{out}}\circ C_{\mathrm{in}}=\llbracket n,k,d\rrbracketis a quantum Gauss’s law code (see Def.III.4) with distanceddfor the22Dℤ2\mathbb{Z}_{2}LGT defined by Hamiltonian (II.2.1) on anNx×NyN_{x}\times N_{y}lattice.
Then the following two results hold:
- (i)

The encoding rate is bounded bykn≤{29,d=3,167​d2,d>3,d:even,16(7​d+1)​dd>3,d:odd.\frac{k}{n}\leq\begin{cases}\frac{2}{9},&d=3,\\
\frac{16}{7d^{2}},&d>3,\quad d:{\rm even},\\
\frac{16}{(7d+1)d}&d>3,\quad d:{\rm odd}.\end{cases}(III.57)
- (ii)

There exists a quantum Gauss’s law codeCout∘CinC_{\mathrm{out}}\circ C_{\mathrm{in}}that saturates the bound (III.57).

In other words, the optimal Gauss’s law code has a parameter{⟦9​N,2​N,3⟧d=3,⟦7​d28​N,2​N,d⟧d:even,⟦(7​d+1)​d8​N,2​N,d⟧d:odd.\displaystyle\begin{cases}\llbracket 9N,2N,3\rrbracket&d=3\,,\\
\llbracket\frac{7d^{2}}{8}N,2N,d\rrbracket&d:\text{even}\,,\\
\llbracket\frac{(7d+1)d}{8}N,2N,d\rrbracket&d:\text{odd}\,.\end{cases}(III.58)

The proof of this theorem is again provided inAppendixA.
For now, we just provide the optimal code construction.
As in the11D case, it suffices to specify the values of allp𝐧p_{\mathbf{n}}andq𝐧,μq_{\mathbf{n},\mu}.
It is easiest to provide these multiplicities in four separate cases, depending on the remainder ofddmodulo44.
An explicit example for the classical Gauss’s law code withd=5d=5is shown inFig.11.Figure 11:Optimal code construction for distanced=5d=5. Each link or site (and any duplicate) corresponds to a single bit in the outer bit-flip code. Blue: logical error with weight55corresponding to an open string. Green: logical error with weight55corresponding to a closed string.

In what follows, we will assumed>3d>3, since thed=3d=3case is trivial.
There are four separate cases to consider.

a+1,&L_{\mathbf{n},\mu}\in\mathcal{L}_{3}.\end{cases}(III.62)

2a+1,&n_{1}\equiv n_{2}\equiv 1\pmod{2},\\
a+1,&S_{\mathbf{n}}\in\mathcal{S}_{2},\end{cases}(III.63)q𝐧,μ\displaystyle q_{\mathbf{n},\mu}={a+1,L𝐧,μ∈ℒ1∪ℒ3,a,L𝐧,μ∈ℒ2∪ℒ4.\displaystyle=\begin{cases}a+1,&L_{\mathbf{n},\mu}\in\mathcal{L}_{1}\cup\mathcal{L}_{3},\\
a,&L_{\mathbf{n},\mu}\in\mathcal{L}_{2}\cup\mathcal{L}_{4}.\end{cases}(III.64)

2a,&n_{1}\equiv n_{2}\equiv 1\pmod{2},\\
a+2,&S_{\mathbf{n}}\in\mathcal{S}_{2},\end{cases}(III.65)q𝐧,μ\displaystyle q_{\mathbf{n},\mu}={a+1,L𝐧,μ∈ℒ1∪ℒ2∪ℒ4,a,L𝐧,μ∈ℒ3.\displaystyle=\begin{cases}a+1,&L_{\mathbf{n},\mu}\in\mathcal{L}_{1}\cup\mathcal{L}_{2}\cup\mathcal{L}_{4},\\
a,&L_{\mathbf{n},\mu}\in\mathcal{L}_{3}.\end{cases}(III.66)

Note that thed≡2(mod4)d\equiv 2\pmod{4}andd≡3(mod4)d\equiv 3\pmod{4}cases involve further splitting the sub-lattices𝒮1\mathcal{S}_{1}and𝒮2\mathcal{S}_{2}based on the exact parity of each coordinate.
The reason for doing this is a slight technicality that enables hitting exactly the optimal encoding rate.
That being said, a simple algebraic calculation leads to, after concatenating withCin=[d,1,d]C_{\mathrm{in}}=[d,1,d], that all of the above cases hit exactlyn={78​d2​Nd:even,78​d​(d+17)​Nd:odd.\displaystyle n=\begin{cases}\frac{7}{8}d^{2}N&d:\text{even}\,,\\
\frac{7}{8}d(d+\frac{1}{7})N&d:\text{odd}\,.\end{cases}(III.67)

Since there are exactlyk=2​Nk=2Nlogical DOFs in the22Dℤ2\mathbb{Z}_{2}LGT, this immediately implies that the bound from TheoremIII.6is saturated.

## IVPerformance Comparison for Gauss’s Law Codes

In this section, we provide in-depth comparisons for the codes constructed in this paper.
This includes comparing the performance of Gauss’s law codes as a function of code distance, as well as comparing Gauss’s law codes with the basic alternatives.
To keep the discussion concise, we consider only theoptimalGauss’s law codes derived inSectionIII, i.e., codes that saturate our optimality bounds for encoding rate fromTheoremsIII.5andIII.6.

SectionIV.1showcases a high-level comparison of code properties such as the number of physical qubits used and the locality of the encoded lattice Hamiltonian for Gauss’s law codes against baselines.SectionIV.2performs code capacity demonstrations with two selected noise models: the depolarizing channel (simulated numerically) and the uncorrelatedX/ZX/Znoise model (analytically tractable).

## IV.1Code Properties

In this section, we discuss and compare the basic properties of Gauss’s law codes with distanced≥3d\geq 3on anNN-site lattice (in both11D and22D).
We will restrict to oddd=2​t+1d=2t+1, which is necessary to enable correction of arbitrarytt-qubit errors.
A useful comparison to an alternative code is obtained by encoding all sites and links with an⟦n,1,d=2​t+1⟧\llbracket n,1,d=2t+1\rrbracketQECC, which can correct bothXXandZZerrors.
For a comparison, we consider the minimalnnfor a givend=2​t+1d=2t+1, which is known to satisfy Rains’ bound[66].
Fork=1k=1, the bound ist≤⌊n+16⌋.t\leq\left\lfloor\frac{n+1}{6}\right\rfloor\,.(IV.1)

Whent=1,2,3t=1,2,3, QECCs saturating this bound are exactly known[54,12,28,29].
We refer to these codes asminimal-length codesand denote the number of physical qubits asnmin=6​t−1.\displaystyle n_{\text{min}}=6t-1\,.(IV.2)

In addition to counting the total number of physical qubits used by each code, it is also worth comparing the locality of the encoded lattice Hamiltonian, since this is an indication of the complexity of performing quantum simulations using the encoding.
In11D, we will consider both the hard-core boson Hamiltonian (II.2.1) and the staggered fermion case (II.2.1) for illustrative purposes; in22D, we will only consider the hard-core boson case to keep the discussion concise.
The encoded Hamiltonian(s) are obtained simply by replacing each Pauli operatorXX,YY, andZZin the original Hamiltonian(s) (II.2.1) (and (II.2.1) as applicable) with a logical representative in the chosen code.
The hard-core boson case (II.2.1) consists of single-bodyZSZ_{S}andZLZ_{L}terms on every site and link, respectively; three-bodyXS​XL​XSX_{S}X_{L}X_{S}terms on every site-link-site triple; and four-bodyXL​XL​XL​XLX_{L}X_{L}X_{L}X_{L}terms on every plaquette (not applicable in11D).
The staggered fermion case (II.2.1) contains additionalYS​XL​YSY_{S}X_{L}Y_{S}terms on every site-link-site triple.
For a fair comparison, we choose the logical representatives to have the least possible weight in order to reduce the overhead to perform quantum simulation.
We leave as a future work to evaluate the effect of weight and locality on the cost under concrete fault-tolerant quantum computing architectures.

## IV.1.11D Comparison

In11D, the optimal Gauss’s law code at distanced=2​t+1≥3d=2t+1\geq 3for anNN-site lattice uses(2​t+1)​(t+1)​N(2t+1)(t+1)Nphysical qubits (seeTheoremIII.5).
Encoding the same lattice with the minimal-length codes uses2​N​nmin2Nn_{\text{min}}physical qubits.Table3shows the comparison between these values using Rains’ bound fort=1,2,3t=1,2,3(d=3,5,7d=3,5,7).
One can see that the number of physical qubits used by the optimal Gauss’s law code is less than that of the minimal-length encoding ford≤7d\leq 7.
However, the former grows like𝒪​(d2)\mathcal{O}(d^{2})while the lower boundnminn_{\text{min}}grows like𝒪​(d)\mathcal{O}(d), which suggests that at higher code distances, the optimal Gauss’s law code will eventually require more resources than the minimal-length code, if similar bounds turn out to be saturated asymptotically.
Indeed, the Gauss’s-law code uses fewer physical qubits up tod=9d=9, while the minimal-length code that saturates the bound (if it exists) uses fewer physical qubits ford≥11d\geq 11.Table 3:Physical qubits used by the optimal Gauss’s law code (GLC) and the minimal-length code to achieve distanced=2​t+1d=2t+1on anNN-site lattice in11D. Now we come to the Hamiltonian locality.
We first study the logical encoding in the Gauss’s law (GL) code.

which requires weightd=2​t+1d=2t+1sinceZ¯(PF)∼Z⊗d\bar{Z}^{(\text{PF})}\sim Z^{\otimes d}at the physical level.

## IV.1.22D Comparison

In22D, the optimal Gauss’s law code at distanced=2​t+1d=2t+1for anNN-site lattice uses14​(7​t+4)​(2​t+1)​N\frac{1}{4}(7t+4)(2t+1)Nphysical qubits ford>3d>3, and9​N9Nphysical qubits ford=3d=3.

For the Hamiltonian locality comparison in22D, we can parallel the discussion from before.

By similar reasoning to the11D case, the electric energy term, mass term, and hopping term from (II.2.1) all have weight exactly2​t+12t+1after encoding.
It turns out that theXL​XL​XL​XLX_{L}X_{L}X_{L}X_{L}plaquette terms fort>1t>1alsorequire weight exactly2​t+12t+1, matching the code distance. Fort=1t=1, this gives weight44.
This can be shown by casework on the remainder ofddmodulo44, using the exact expressions forp𝐧p_{\mathbf{n}}andq𝐧,μq_{\mathbf{n},\mu}fromSectionIII.3.
The key point is that the optimization procedure used to minimize the logical encoding rate for the Gauss’s law code simultaneously makes the weight of all terms in the Hamiltonian (II.2.1) essentially identical.

For the minimal-length code, the plaquette operatorXL​XL​XL​XLX_{L}X_{L}X_{L}X_{L}clearly has the highest weight, requiring an interaction of4​d4dqubits at once.
The weight is then4​d=8​t+44d=8t+4.
Therefore, the improvement in locality by the Gauss’s law code is a factor of44(d≥5d\geq 5) in the22D case.

## IV.2Code Capacity and Logical Error Rate

In this section, code capacity demonstrations are performed by exposing code states to selected noise models, followed by attempting recovery via the usual stabilizer error correction procedure, equipped with a chosen syndrome decoder.
A code capacity study assumes that errors only happen before syndrome extraction and only on data qubits, and all other parts (circuits and measurements for syndromes, initial state preparation, ancilla qubits) are done perfectly.
Although such a code capacity study is less realistic than fullcircuit-levelsimulation, where all circuits undergo errors, it can assess how much logical information can be recovered from ideal syndrome outcomes, as was done in e.g.[25].
Many technical details regarding the syndrome decoding strategy and analytical calculations are given inAppendicesBandC.
The following features are in common between all of our demonstrations:
- •

Spatial dimensionality of underlying lattice:11D.
- •

Noise model characteristic: a single-qubit error channel applied independently with identical distribution (i.i.d.) to every physical qubit.
- •

Syndrome decoder used for Gauss’s law codes: maximum-likelihood decoder (MLD) (along with posterior evaluation using concatenated structure[63]); seeAppendixBfor details.
- •

Syndrome decoder used for minimal-length codes: maximum-likelihood decoder (MLD).

A convenient performance metric for code capacity demonstrations is the logical error rate (LER), which measures the likelihood that the initial noise is disruptive enough to cause a logical failure even under the assumption of perfectly executed recovery.
Additional details are provided under the heading for each demonstration.

## IV.2.1Depolarizing Noise Demonstration

For our first demonstration, consider the11D spatial lattice withNNsites, and equip the optimal Gauss’s law codeCout∘CinC_{\mathrm{out}}\circ C_{\mathrm{in}}for odd distanced≥3d\geq 3.
In addition to the Gauss’s law code, consider also the minimal-length code with the same distanceddand on the sameNN-site lattice.
In the numerical simulation, we impose open boundary conditions for both boundary links (settingL0=LN=1L_{0}=L_{N}=1), resulting in an open lattice with(N−1)(N-1)links.

We use depolarizing noise, which acts on theithi^{\mathrm{th}}qubit byℰi​(ρ)=(1−p)​ρ+p3​(Xi​ρ​Xi+Yi​ρ​Yi+Zi​ρ​Zi),\mathcal{E}_{i}(\rho)=(1-p)\rho+\frac{p}{3}\left(X_{i}\rho X_{i}+Y_{i}\rho Y_{i}+Z_{i}\rho Z_{i}\right),(IV.9)

whereρ\rhodenotes the state of the entirenn-qubit physical system, and0≤p≤10\leq p\leq 1is the physical error rate666It is different fromp𝐧p_{\mathbf{n}}, which is the duplication parameter..
Applying this as i.i.d. noise to every qubit results in the error channelℰ=ℰ1∘ℰ2∘⋯∘ℰn\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}_{1}\circ\mathcal{E}_{2}\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{E}_{n}.

The recovery operationℛ\mathcal{R}depends on whether the code used is the Gauss’s law code or the minimal-length code.
In both cases, begin by measuring the full stabilizer syndrome.
For the Gauss’s law code, decode the syndrome with MLD (seeAppendixB), where the posterior probability is evaluated making use of the concatenated structure, thereby obtaining a candidate Pauli error pattern.
For the minimal-length code, decode the syndrome with MLD (seeAppendixB).
In both cases, to conclude the recovery operation, apply the decoded pattern directly to the physical system.

As a metric, we define the logical error rate by using the individual logical operators.
Specifically, for Gauss’s law code (GL), we define the logical operators asZ¯n(GL)\displaystyle\bar{Z}^{(\text{GL})}_{n}:=Z¯Ln(PF),\displaystyle:=\bar{Z}^{(\text{PF})}_{L_{n}}\,,(IV.10)X¯n(GL)\displaystyle\bar{X}^{(\text{GL})}_{n}:=X¯Sn(PF)​X¯Ln(PF)​X¯Sn+1(PF),\displaystyle:=\bar{X}^{(\text{PF})}_{S_{n}}\bar{X}^{(\text{PF})}_{L_{n}}\bar{X}^{(\text{PF})}_{S_{n+1}}\,,(IV.11)

whereX¯(PF),Z¯(PF)\bar{X}^{(\text{PF})},\bar{Z}^{(\text{PF})}represent the logical operators in the encoding by the inner phase-flip code.
For the minimal-length codes, the logical operators are defined byZ¯2​m−1(min)\displaystyle\bar{Z}^{(\text{min})}_{2m-1}:=Z¯Sm(min),\displaystyle:=\bar{Z}^{(\text{min})}_{S_{m}}\,,Z¯2​m(min)\displaystyle\bar{Z}^{(\text{min})}_{2m}:=Z¯Lm(min),\displaystyle:=\bar{Z}^{(\text{min})}_{L_{m}}\,,(IV.12)X¯2​m−1(min)\displaystyle\bar{X}^{(\text{min})}_{2m-1}:=X¯Sm(min),\displaystyle:=\bar{X}^{(\text{min})}_{S_{m}}\,,X¯2​m(min)\displaystyle\bar{X}^{(\text{min})}_{2m}:=X¯Lm(min).\displaystyle:=\bar{X}^{(\text{min})}_{L_{m}}\,.(IV.13)

Letx(code),z(code)∈𝔽2k(code)x^{(\texttt{code})},z^{(\texttt{code})}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k^{(\texttt{code})}}denote the binary vectors specifying the residual logical Pauli error after recovery. That is to say, the residual error is written as∏n=1k(code)(X¯n(code))xn(code)​(Z¯n(code))zn(code),\prod_{n=1}^{k^{(\texttt{code})}}\left(\bar{X}_{n}^{(\texttt{code})}\right)^{x_{n}^{(\texttt{code})}}\left(\bar{Z}_{n}^{(\texttt{code})}\right)^{z_{n}^{(\texttt{code})}}\,,(IV.14)

up to a stabilizer and an overall phase.
Then two types of LER can be defined by777The different metrics (LERs) are used for the respective codes, since the logical dimensions are different.
We can instead evaluateLER(GL)\mathrm{LER}^{(\texttt{GL})}for both codes, but that for the minimal-length code does not measure failure of its full logicalX/ZX/Zeigenvalues. On the other hand, imposing Gauss’s law to match the logical dimension would increase the relevantXX-distance.LERZ(code)\displaystyle\mathrm{LER}^{(\texttt{code})}_{Z}=Pr⁡[(x1(code),⋯,xk(code)(code))≠𝟎],\displaystyle=\Pr\left[\left(x^{(\texttt{code})}_{1},\cdots,x^{(\texttt{code})}_{k^{(\texttt{code})}}\right)\neq\bm{0}\right]\,,(IV.15)LERX(code)\displaystyle\mathrm{LER}^{(\texttt{code})}_{X}=Pr⁡[(z1(code),⋯,zk(code)(code))≠𝟎],\displaystyle=\Pr\left[\left(z^{(\texttt{code})}_{1},\cdots,z^{(\texttt{code})}_{k^{(\texttt{code})}}\right)\neq\bm{0}\right]\,,(IV.16)

wherek(code)k^{(\texttt{code})}represents the number of logical qubits in each code, given ask(GL)=N−1,k(min)=2​N−1,\displaystyle k^{(\text{GL})}=N-1\,,\quad k^{(\text{min})}=2N-1\,,(IV.17)

under the assumption that the lattice hasNNsites and(N−1)(N-1)links.
The value ofLERZ(code)\mathrm{LER}^{(\texttt{code})}_{Z}(LERX(code)\mathrm{LER}^{(\texttt{code})}_{X}) evaluates the ability of this setup to protect a simultaneous eigenstate of the logicalZ¯\bar{Z}(X¯\bar{X}) operators against errors.Figure12showsLERZ(code)\mathrm{LER}^{(\texttt{code})}_{Z}(a) andLERX(code)\mathrm{LER}^{(\texttt{code})}_{X}(b) against the physical error ratepp.
The former (latter) setting is called theZZmemory (XXmemory) simulation.
We see that, for both code families, LER decreases with increasingdd, for the value ofppbelow the points where severalddlines cross each other (called thethreshold).
For theZZ-memory simulation, the Gauss’s-law code has a lower LER than the minimal-length code ford=3d=3, but a higher LER ford=7d=7. In contrast, in theXX-memory experiment, the Gauss’s-law code has a lower LER than the minimal-length code for all three distances,d=3,5,7d=3,5,7. Furthermore, the crossing point for the Gauss’s-law code occurs at a higher value ofppthan that for the minimal-length code in theXX-memory experiment, whereas the opposite ordering is observed in theZZ-memory experiment.
Evaluating threshold values in thecircuit-levelsimulation is important to quantify the performance in a more practical setting, which we leave as a future direction.

For theZZmemory experiment, the single-site flip (product overiiis only taken for Gauss’s law code)|0⟩Ln​∏i(|0⟩Sn+1(i))​|0⟩Ln+1→|0⟩Ln​∏i(|1⟩Sn+1(i))​|0⟩Ln+1,\ket{0}_{L_{n}}\prod_{i}(\ket{0}_{S^{(i)}_{n+1}})\ket{0}_{L_{n+1}}\to\ket{0}_{L_{n}}\prod_{i}(\ket{1}_{S^{(i)}_{n+1}})\ket{0}_{L_{n+1}}\,,(IV.18)

can be detected and corrected for Gauss’s law code, while it gives a logical error for minimal-length codes.
Gauss’s law codes can correct such gauge-violating errors, which arealsoideally avoided in LGT simulations.
However, when the site flip is accompanied by a flip of either neighboring link, the resulting error can cause a logical failure of the Gauss’s-law code. The simulation results are consistent with the interpretation that, asddincreases, the multiplicity of such failure patterns comes to outweigh the advantage of correcting a simultaneous flip of all duplicated copies of one site variable (which is not gauge invariant), leading to the reversal in the relative performance of the two codes.
Besides, the asymmetry inXXandZZmemory behavior that is observed only for the Gauss’s law code may come from the concatenation structure (phase-flip and bit-flip errors are treated separately in inner and outer codes) and the weight of logical operatorsEq.IV.11.Figure 12:Memory experiment for a 1DN=4N=4lattice with the open boundary condition. Thexx-axis shows the physical error rate, and theyy-axis is the logical error rate.
Blue and red curves correspond to the Gauss’s-law code (GLC) and the minimal-length code (min-length), respectively. Each legend entry is labeled by(Nq,d)(N_{q},d), whereNqN_{q}is the total number of physical qubits andddis the code distance. The left (right) panel focuses on theZZ(XX) memory experiment, if they can keep theZZ(XX) eigenstates. Monte-Carlo simulation is performed at each data point withNshot=2×105N_{\text{shot}}=2\times 10^{5}shots, and LER is estimated asNfail/NshotN_{\text{fail}}/N_{\text{shot}}. The error bar shows binomial likelihood-factor intervals with a maximum likelihood factor10310^{3}.

## IV.2.2Uncorrelated X/Z Noise Demonstration

For our second demonstration, consider again the11D spatial lattice withNNsites, and equip the optimal Gauss’s law codeCout∘CinC_{\mathrm{out}}\circ C_{\mathrm{in}}for odd distanced≥3d\geq 3.

Define uncorrelatedX/ZX/Znoise on theithi^{\mathrm{th}}qubit as the error channelℰi(ρ)=(1\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{i}(\rho)=(1−p)(1−p)ρ+p(1−p)XiρXi\displaystyle-p)(1-p)\rho+p(1-p)X_{i}\rho X_{i}(IV.19)+p2​Yi​ρ​Yi+p​(1−p)​Zi​ρ​Zi,\displaystyle+p^{2}Y_{i}\rho Y_{i}+p(1-p)Z_{i}\rho Z_{i},(IV.20)

whereρ\rhodenotes the state of the entirenn-qubit physical system, and0<p<10<p<1is used as the independent rate for physicalXX-errors andZZ-errors.888Technically, we will need to assumep<1/2p<1/2in order for the results to be exactly correct. This technicality is discussed inAppendixB.When acting i.i.d. on every qubit, this results in the error channelℰ=ℰ1∘ℰ2∘⋯∘ℰn\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}_{1}\circ\mathcal{E}_{2}\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{E}_{n}.

For the recovery operationℛ\mathcal{R}, measure all inner and outer stabilizer generators, and decode the resulting syndrome with MLD, thereby obtaining a candidate Pauli error pattern.
The explicit decoding method is discussed inAppendixB; importantly, many details are simplified due to the fact that uncorrelatedX/ZX/Znoise is used.
To conclude the recovery operation, apply the decoded pattern directly onto the physical system.Figure 13:Logical error rates on a11D periodic lattice withNs=10N_{s}=10sites, on the worst-case (adversarial) initial code state. The uncorrelatedX/ZX/Zchannel is used with the same physicalXXandZZerror rates. Blue and red curves correspond to the Gauss’s law code (GLC) and the minimal-length code (min-length), respectively. Each legend entry is labeled by(Nq,d)(N_{q},d)whereNqN_{q}is the total number of physical qubits andddis the code distance. These curves are obtained from the syndrome decoding and analytical calculations that are detailed inAppendicesBandC.

InFig.13, we plot the (strict)worst-caseLER, defined asLER≡max|ψ⟩∈ℋC⁡Pr⁡[|⟨ψr|ψ⟩|2≠1],\mathrm{LER}\equiv\max_{\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{H}_{C}}\Pr\left[|\langle\psi_{r}|\psi\rangle|^{2}\neq 1\right],(IV.21)

where|ψr⟩≡ℛ​(ℰ​(|ψ⟩))\ket{\psi_{r}}\equiv\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{E}(\ket{\psi}))denotes the (random) state obtained after recovery, andℋC\mathcal{H}_{C}denotes the code space of the Gauss’s law code.
The LER defined by (IV.21) maximizes over all possible initial states to find the worst-case probability that the initial state is not recovered exactly.
For Gauss’s law codes, which are handled by MLD, this is identical to the LER defined byLER(GL)=Pr⁡[x(GL)≠𝟎∨z(GL)≠𝟎],\mathrm{LER}^{(\texttt{GL})}=\Pr\left[x^{(\texttt{GL})}\neq\bm{0}\lor z^{(\texttt{GL})}\neq\bm{0}\right]\,,(IV.22)

where we use the same notation as inSectionIV.2.1.

As it turns out, there is a very high degree of analytical control over this question, and we can compute (IV.21) exactly.
This analytical calculation is carried out inAppendixC, andFig.13contains the results.
One can see a similar trend as in the depolarizing noise simulation.
The logical error rate of the Gauss’s law code is lower than that of the minimal-length code ford=3d=3, but this ordering is reversed ford=5d=5andd=7d=7.

## VSummary and Conclusion

In this paper, we considered a quantum error-correction protocol incorporating Gauss’s law constraint.
It allowed us to correct violations of Gauss’s law caused by hardware noise within a unified QEC framework.
Specifically, we extended the code construction with code distanced=3d=3[67]to an arbitrary distance.
The sites and links are duplicated, and the classical Gauss’s law code is built from Gauss’s law and parity checks between pairs of duplicated links/sites, which is further concatenated with a phase-flip repetition code to be a full QEC code.
We have shown that our construction with a specific duplication pattern achieves an arbitrary distance and has the optimal encoding rate among the considered constructions.

To quantify the performance of the resulting codes,
the number of physical qubits was compared with baseline minimal-length code constructions, and at low code distances (d≤7)d\leq 7), Gauss’s law code is smaller than that of the minimal-length code, generalizing similar findings ford=3d=3in RRW[67].
We also performed the memory simulation in the code capacity setting for the depolarizing noise, and observed that the Gauss’s-law code exhibits lowerXX-memory logical error rates than the minimal-length code at all distances considered, whereas theZZ-memory comparison depends on the distance. The exact analysis under uncorrelatedX/ZX/Znoise exhibits the same qualitative distance dependence as the depolarizing-noiseZZ-memory comparison.

There are many important future directions. First, it would be important to consider the fault-tolerant syndrome extraction circuit.
It can be implemented using general ancilla-based or flag-based constructions[72,26,77,18,17]or a logical-to-physical CNOT gate as proposed in RRW[67].
Evaluating their performance using circuit-level simulation will allow us to compare the performance of Gauss’s law code with that of other possible codes, in particular in terms of the required error threshold.
How to perform gate operations in a fault-tolerant way is another important direction toward fault-tolerant LGT simulation, as was discussed in[73]for thed=3d=3case.
Finally, it would be interesting to extend Gauss’s law code construction to non-Abelian gauge theory.
