## Appendix AProofs for Optimality Theorems

Here, we provide the proofs ofTheoremsIII.5andIII.6.

## Proof ofTheoremIII.5.

It suffices to prove the boundkn≤2d​(d+1),\frac{k}{n}\leq\frac{2}{d(d+1)},(A.1)

since it’s easy to check that the code defined explicitly by the multiplicities (III.47) saturates the bound.

WriteCout∘CinC_{\mathrm{out}}\circ C_{\mathrm{in}}for the Gauss’s law code, whereCout=[nout,kout,dout]C_{\mathrm{out}}=[n_{\mathrm{out}},k_{\mathrm{out}},d_{\mathrm{out}}]is the classical Gauss’s law code andCin=[nin,1,din]C_{\mathrm{in}}=[n_{\mathrm{in}},1,d_{\mathrm{in}}]is an arbitrary classical code encoding a single logical bit.

Recall that the number of logical qubits is alwaysk=kout=Nk=k_{\mathrm{out}}=Nfor any11D Gauss’s law code, and therefore it suffices to find a tight lower bound onn=nout​ninn=n_{\mathrm{out}}n_{\mathrm{in}}.
Clearly,nin≥din≥dn_{\mathrm{in}}\geq d_{\mathrm{in}}\geq d, with equality whennin=din=dn_{\mathrm{in}}=d_{\mathrm{in}}=d, e.g., by takingCin=[d,1,d]C_{\mathrm{in}}=[d,1,d]to be the classical repetition code.

It remains to find the tight lower bound fornoutn_{\mathrm{out}}.
To this end, supposep1,…,pN∈ℕp_{1},\dots,p_{N}\in\mathbb{N}andq1,…,qN∈ℕq_{1},\dots,q_{N}\in\mathbb{N}are the multiplicities defining the classical Gauss’s law codeCoutC_{\mathrm{out}}.
DefineP\displaystyle P≡∑i=1Npi,\displaystyle\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i},(A.2)Q\displaystyle Q≡∑i=1Nqi,\displaystyle\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{N}q_{i},(A.3)

for brevity.

The classical distance satisfiesdout≥dd_{\mathrm{out}}\geq d, which implies that every open string has weight at leastdd.
In particular, for an open string between sitesjjandj+1j+1, we havepj+qj+pj+1≥dout≥d.p_{j}+q_{j}+p_{j+1}\geq d_{\mathrm{out}}\geq d.(A.4)

Summing overjjand using periodicity yields2​P+Q≥d​N.2P+Q\geq dN.(A.5)

Moreover, since everyqjq_{j}is a positive integer, we haveQ≥NQ\geq N.
Therefore,nout=P+Q=(2​P+Q)+Q2≥d​N+N2=d+12​N,n_{\mathrm{out}}=P+Q=\frac{(2P+Q)+Q}{2}\geq\frac{dN+N}{2}=\frac{d+1}{2}N,(A.6)

which must be satisfied for everydd, regardless of parity.

Finally, using the fact that the number of logical qubits isk=Nk=Nfor the concatenated code, we have that the encoding rate satisfieskn=Nnout​nin≤N(d+12​N)​d=2d​(d+1),\frac{k}{n}=\frac{N}{n_{\mathrm{out}}n_{\mathrm{in}}}\leq\frac{N}{\left(\frac{d+1}{2}N\right)d}=\frac{2}{d(d+1)},(A.7)

as desired.
∎

## Proof ofTheoremIII.6.

As with the11D case, it suffices to prove the upper bound on the encoding ratek/nk/n, since it’s easy to check that the code defined explicitly by the multiplicities inSectionIII.3saturates the bound.

As in the11D case, writeCout∘CinC_{\mathrm{out}}\circ C_{\mathrm{in}}for the Gauss’s law code, whereCout=[nout,kout,dout]C_{\mathrm{out}}=[n_{\mathrm{out}},k_{\mathrm{out}},d_{\mathrm{out}}]is the classical Gauss’s law code andCin=[nin,1,din]C_{\mathrm{in}}=[n_{\mathrm{in}},1,d_{\mathrm{in}}]is an arbitrary classical code encoding a single logical bit.

For the22D case, the number of logical qubits is alwaysk=kout=2​Nk=k_{\mathrm{out}}=2N; therefore, again, it suffices to find a tight lower bound onn=nout​ninn=n_{\mathrm{out}}n_{\mathrm{in}}.
ForCinC_{\mathrm{in}}, the same reasoning holds as in the11D case:nin≥din≥dn_{\mathrm{in}}\geq d_{\mathrm{in}}\geq d, with equality whennin=din=dn_{\mathrm{in}}=d_{\mathrm{in}}=d, e.g., by takingCin=[d,1,d]C_{\mathrm{in}}=[d,1,d]to be the classical repetition code.

The interesting step again reduces to finding the tight lower bound fornoutn_{\mathrm{out}}.
This time, we denotep𝐧∈ℕp_{\mathbf{n}}\in\mathbb{N}andq𝐧,μ∈ℕq_{\mathbf{n},\mu}\in\mathbb{N}as the multiplicities defining the classical Gauss’s law codeCoutC_{\mathrm{out}}.
DefineP\displaystyle P≡∑𝐧∈Λp𝐧,\displaystyle\equiv\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\Lambda}p_{\mathbf{n}},(A.8)Q\displaystyle Q≡∑𝐧∈Λ∑μ=12q𝐧,μ.\displaystyle\equiv\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\Lambda}\sum_{\mu=1}^{2}q_{\mathbf{n},\mu}.(A.9)

The classical distance satisfiesdout≥dd_{\mathrm{out}}\geq d, which implies that every open string has weight at leastdd,andevery closed loop has weight at leastdd.
We proceed by casework ondd.
Ifd=3d=3, then the result is straightforward, becausek=2​Nk=2Nandnout≥3​Nn_{\mathrm{out}}\geq 3N, so thatkn=2​Nnout​nin≤2​N3​N⋅3=29,\frac{k}{n}=\frac{2N}{n_{\mathrm{out}}n_{\mathrm{in}}}\leq\frac{2N}{3N\cdot 3}=\frac{2}{9},(A.10)

as desired.

Now supposed>3d>3, and consider the weight of the open string spanning just a single linkL𝐧,μL_{\mathbf{n},\mu}and its endpointsS𝐧S_{\mathbf{n}}andS𝐧+μ^S_{\mathbf{n}+\hat{\mu}}.
This weight is given byp𝐧+q𝐧,μ+p𝐧+μ^≥dout≥d.p_{\mathbf{n}}+q_{\mathbf{n},\mu}+p_{\mathbf{n}+\hat{\mu}}\geq d_{\mathrm{out}}\geq d.(A.11)

By summing over all𝐧∈Λ\mathbf{n}\in\Lambdaandμ∈{1,2}\mu\in\{1,2\}, we obtain4​P+Q≥2​d​N.4P+Q\geq 2dN.(A.12)

Similarly, consider the weight of the plaquette with linksL𝐧,xL_{\mathbf{n},x},L𝐧+x^,yL_{\mathbf{n}+\hat{x},y},L𝐧+y^,xL_{\mathbf{n}+\hat{y},x}, andL𝐧,yL_{\mathbf{n},y}.
This weight is given byq𝐧,x+q𝐧+x^,y+q𝐧+y^,x+q𝐧,y≥dout≥d.q_{\mathbf{n},x}+q_{\mathbf{n}+\hat{x},y}+q_{\mathbf{n}+\hat{y},x}+q_{\mathbf{n},y}\geq d_{\mathrm{out}}\geq d.(A.13)

After summing over all𝐧∈Λ\mathbf{n}\in\Lambda, we obtain2​Q≥d​N.2Q\geq dN.(A.14)

Combining the inequalities together, we findnout\displaystyle n_{\mathrm{out}}=P+Q=14​(4​P+Q)+38​(2​Q)\displaystyle=P+Q=\frac{1}{4}(4P+Q)+\frac{3}{8}(2Q)(A.15)≥14​(2​d​N)+38​(d​N)\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{4}(2dN)+\frac{3}{8}(dN)(A.16)=78​d​N.\displaystyle=\frac{7}{8}dN.(A.17)

This immediately gives the bound on the encoding ratekn=2​Nnout​nin≤2​N(78​d​N)​d=167​d2,\frac{k}{n}=\frac{2N}{n_{\mathrm{out}}n_{\mathrm{in}}}\leq\frac{2N}{\left(\frac{7}{8}dN\right)d}=\frac{16}{7d^{2}},(A.18)

which is the desired bound for evendd(but also holds for odddd).

For odddd, there is a slight improvement.
For any𝐧∈Λ\mathbf{n}\in\Lambda, consider the sums of the site and link variables around a plaquette with corner at𝐧\mathbf{n}:v𝐧\displaystyle v_{\mathbf{n}}≡p𝐧+p𝐧+x^+p𝐧+y^+p𝐧+x^+y^,\displaystyle\equiv p_{\mathbf{n}}+p_{\mathbf{n}+\hat{x}}+p_{\mathbf{n}+\hat{y}}+p_{\mathbf{n}+\hat{x}+\hat{y}},(A.19)ℓ𝐧\displaystyle\ell_{\mathbf{n}}≡q𝐧,x+q𝐧,y+q𝐧+x^,y+q𝐧+y^,x.\displaystyle\equiv q_{\mathbf{n},x}+q_{\mathbf{n},y}+q_{\mathbf{n}+\hat{x},y}+q_{\mathbf{n}+\hat{y},x}.(A.20)

Consider the44open strings that can be formed from any single link and its endpoints around this plaquette.
The weights of these strings satisfyp𝐧+q𝐧,x+p𝐧+x^\displaystyle p_{\mathbf{n}}+q_{\mathbf{n},x}+p_{\mathbf{n}+\hat{x}}≥d,\displaystyle\geq d,(A.21)p𝐧+q𝐧,y+p𝐧+y^\displaystyle p_{\mathbf{n}}+q_{\mathbf{n},y}+p_{\mathbf{n}+\hat{y}}≥d,\displaystyle\geq d,(A.22)p𝐧+x^+q𝐧+x^,y+p𝐧+x^+y^\displaystyle p_{\mathbf{n}+\hat{x}}+q_{\mathbf{n}+\hat{x},y}+p_{\mathbf{n}+\hat{x}+\hat{y}}≥d,\displaystyle\geq d,(A.23)p𝐧+y^+q𝐧+y^,x+p𝐧+x^+y^\displaystyle p_{\mathbf{n}+\hat{y}}+q_{\mathbf{n}+\hat{y},x}+p_{\mathbf{n}+\hat{x}+\hat{y}}≥d.\displaystyle\geq d.(A.24)

By adding together all these inequalities, we find2​v𝐧+ℓ𝐧≥4​d.2v_{\mathbf{n}}+\ell_{\mathbf{n}}\geq 4d.(A.25)

Furthermore, the weight of the plaquette itself satisfiesℓ𝐧≥d.\ell_{\mathbf{n}}\geq d.(A.26)

By adding thrice of the latter inequality to the former inequality, we find2​v𝐧+4​ℓ𝐧≥7​d.2v_{\mathbf{n}}+4\ell_{\mathbf{n}}\geq 7d.(A.27)

The LHS is always an even integer, but ifddis odd then the RHS is an odd integer.
Therefore, in the case of odddd, we have the stronger inequality2​v𝐧+4​ℓ𝐧≥7​d+1.2v_{\mathbf{n}}+4\ell_{\mathbf{n}}\geq 7d+1.(A.28)

By summing over all𝐧∈Λ\mathbf{n}\in\Lambda, we find8​P+8​Q≥(7​d+1)​N.8P+8Q\geq(7d+1)N.(A.29)

Equivalently,nout≥7​d+18​N.n_{\mathrm{out}}\geq\frac{7d+1}{8}N.(A.30)

This gives a stronger encoding rate bound for odddd, namely,kn=2​Nnout​nin≤2​N(7​d+18​N)​d=16(7​d+1)​d,\frac{k}{n}=\frac{2N}{n_{\mathrm{out}}n_{\mathrm{in}}}\leq\frac{2N}{\left(\frac{7d+1}{8}N\right)d}=\frac{16}{(7d+1)d},(A.31)

as desired.
∎

## Appendix BSyndrome Decoding

In this appendix, we discuss the concept of error syndromes and how they can be used to diagnose and correct errors that occur on physical qubits.
We begin with the definitions of error syndromes and error cosets in the classical and quantum setting, as well as stabilizer cosets and the related notion of “logical equivalence classes,” which are features specific to the quantum case, without a classical analogue.
After defining these general concepts, we discuss the most popular types of syndromedecoders, which aim to deduce a candidate error pattern from knowledge only of its error syndrome.
Finally, we provide explicit syndrome decoding algorithms that are used to obtain the results inSectionIV.2in the main text.

We will assume that all noise models used in the quantum setting are Pauli noise models, since that is the case relevant for this work.

## B.1Error Syndromes, Cosets, and Logical Equivalence Classes

For a classical codeC=[n,k,d]C=[n,k,d]with parity-check matrixHH, theerror syndromeof any bit stringv∈𝔽2nv\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}is defined asσ​(v)≡H​v∈𝔽2n−k.\sigma(v)\equiv Hv\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-k}.(B.1)

The syndrome ofvvis zero if and only ifvvis a codeword, and therefore, the syndrome can be used for the purpose of detecting errors.
If a codewordc∈Cc\in Cis transmitted, and the received wordc′∈𝔽2nc^{\prime}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}has nonzero syndromes≡σ​(c′)≠0s\equiv\sigma(c^{\prime})\neq 0, then we can deduce that an error must have occurred during transmission.

Every binary error is a bit-flip, and therefore we can summarize the error onccas a bit stringe∈𝔽2ne\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}, called theerror pattern, withc′=c+ec^{\prime}=c+e.
The syndrome function can be applied to the error pattern itself, and the result will always match the syndrome of the received word,σ​(e)=σ​(c′)=s\sigma(e)=\sigma(c^{\prime})=s.
For any fixed error syndromes∈𝔽2n−ks\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-k}, there are always exactly2k2^{k}distinct patternse∈𝔽2ne\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}that would lead to the same syndrome,σ​(e)=s\sigma(e)=s.
In classical error correction, this collection is sometimes called the “error coset” corresponding to the syndromess.
The reason for this terminology is that the entire set of all possible error patterns having the same syndromess,Σs≡{e∈𝔽2n∣σ​(e)=s},\Sigma_{s}\equiv\{e\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}\mid\sigma(e)=s\},(B.2)

can be generated from a single representativee~∈𝔽2n\widetilde{e}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}with the same syndromeσ​(e~)=s\sigma(\widetilde{e})=sby arbitraryCC-translations.
In mathematical terms,Σs=e~+C\Sigma_{s}=\tilde{e}+C, wheree~+C≡{e~+c∣c∈C}.\tilde{e}+C\equiv\{\widetilde{e}+c\mid c\in C\}.(B.3)

This is the easiest way to see that|Σs|=2k|\Sigma_{s}|=2^{k}, because clearly|e~+C|=|C|=2k|\tilde{e}+C|=|C|=2^{k}.

At the quantum level, the situation is analogous to the classical case, but there are important changes due to the fact that, in addition to bit-flip errors (XXerrors), there can also be phase-flip errors (ZZerrors), or even simultaneous bit-flip and phase-flip errors (YYerrors).
For starters, it is not possible to assign an error syndrome directly to an arbitrary quantum state of physical qubits.
Instead, one mustmeasureall stabilizer generators to obtain the error syndrome, implying that syndromes are non-deterministic functions of the physical state.

For instance, consider an⟦n,k,d⟧\llbracket n,k,d\rrbracketstabilizer code with stabilizer group𝒮⊆𝒫n\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{n}and code spaceℋC\mathcal{H}_{C}.
An initial (pure) code stateρ≡|ψ⟩​⟨ψ|\rho\equiv\ket{\psi}\bra{\psi}for|ψ⟩∈ℋC\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{H}_{C}becomes a (possibly mixed) received stateρ′\rho^{\prime}after errors are incurred.
The error syndrome forρ′\rho^{\prime}is then given by measuring the stabilizer generatorsS1,…,Sn−k∈𝒮S_{1},\dots,S_{n-k}\in\mathcal{S}, yielding respective outcomesm1,…,mn−k∈{±1}m_{1},\dots,m_{n-k}\in\{\pm 1\}drawn from the probability distribution induced byρ′\rho^{\prime}.

To sidestep this non-deterministic behavior, it is possible to associate the notion of error syndrome directly to the error pattern, instead of the physical quantum state, analogous to the classical case.
Given that our noise model is assumed to be Pauli, it makes sense to define the syndrome functionσ:𝒫n→{±1}n−k\sigma:\mathcal{P}_{n}\to\{\pm 1\}^{n-k}as follows:σ​(E)≡(m1,…,mn−k)⇔∀Si∈𝒮:E​Si=mi​Si​E,\sigma(E)\equiv(m_{1},\dots,m_{n-k})\iff\forall S_{i}\in\mathcal{S}:ES_{i}=m_{i}S_{i}E,(B.4)

where the target space{±1}n−k\{\pm 1\}^{n-k}is due to the fact that every pair of Pauli operators either commutes or anti-commutes.
This always matches exactly the syndrome that would have been measured on the received stateρ′\rho^{\prime}if the Pauli errorEEwas incurred, i.e., ifρ′=E​ρ​E†\rho^{\prime}=E\rho E^{\dagger}.
This pushes the randomness of the syndrome measurement entirely to the noise model, as in the classical case.999In other words, we can assume that the syndrome is randomly decided at the moment the error is applied, instead of when the state is measured.

One can now also define a quantum analogue of error cosets by considering the set of all Pauli operators with a given error syndromem∈{±1}n−km\in\{\pm 1\}^{n-k},Σm≡{E∈𝒫n∣σ​(E)=m}.\Sigma_{m}\equiv\{E\in\mathcal{P}_{n}\mid\sigma(E)=m\}.(B.5)

Similarly to the classical case, ifE~∈𝒫n\widetilde{E}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}has syndromeσ​(E~)=m\sigma(\widetilde{E})=m, thenΣm\Sigma_{m}exactly coincides with a genuine coset[E~]C𝒫n​(𝒮)[\widetilde{E}]_{C_{\mathcal{P}_{n}}(\mathcal{S})}in the group theory sense.
Importantly, this coset is built from thecentralizerC𝒫n​(𝒮)C_{\mathcal{P}_{n}}(\mathcal{S})of the stabilizer𝒮\mathcal{S}within the Pauli group𝒫n\mathcal{P}_{n}.
The coset is given by[E~]C𝒫n​(𝒮)≡E~​C𝒫n​(𝒮)={E~​P∣P∈C𝒫n​(𝒮)}.[\widetilde{E}]_{C_{\mathcal{P}_{n}}(\mathcal{S})}\equiv\widetilde{E}C_{\mathcal{P}_{n}}(\mathcal{S})=\{\widetilde{E}P\mid P\in C_{\mathcal{P}_{n}}(\mathcal{S})\}.(B.6)

This holds becauseE~\widetilde{E}andE~​P\widetilde{E}Phave the same syndrome if and only ifPPcommutes with the entire stabilizer.

However, there is a crucial subtlety present here: unlike in the classical setting, the Pauli error patternEEthat connects an initial code state|ψ⟩∈ℋC\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{H}_{C}to the received state|ψ′⟩\ket{\psi^{\prime}}isnotunique.
This is true for two separate reasons.
First, there is a technicality involving global phases: ifE,E′∈𝒫nE,E^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}differ only by a global phase, then there is no physical difference betweenE​|ψ⟩E\ket{\psi}andE′​|ψ⟩E^{\prime}\ket{\psi}.
This is resolved by replacing the Pauli group𝒫n\mathcal{P}_{n}with the Pauli group modulo phases𝒫¯n\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}, which is defined as the quotient space𝒫n/{I,i​I,−I,−i​I}\mathcal{P}_{n}/\{I,iI,-I,-iI\}.
After passing to this quotient, we identify𝒮\mathcal{S}with its image under the quotient map whenever no confusion arises.
Consequently, all previous definitions (e.g., stabilizer group, error cosets, etc.) are updated in the obvious way.

However, even when working with the Pauli group modulo phases, there can still existE,E′∈𝒫¯nE,E^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}withE≠E′E\neq E^{\prime}such thatE​|ψ⟩=E′​|ψ⟩=|ψ′⟩E\ket{\psi}=E^{\prime}\ket{\psi}=\ket{\psi^{\prime}}holds for every|ψ⟩∈ℋC\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{H}_{C}.
The point is that ifE′=E​SE^{\prime}=ESwhereS∈𝒮S\in\mathcal{S}, thenE′​|ψ⟩=E​S​|ψ⟩=E​|ψ⟩E^{\prime}\ket{\psi}=ES\ket{\psi}=E\ket{\psi}, because|ψ⟩∈ℋC\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{H}_{C}is held fixed by stabilizer group elements.
This observation motivates defining a different coset called thestabilizer cosetof an error patternE∈𝒫¯nE\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n},[E]𝒮≡E​𝒮={E​S∣S∈𝒮}.[E]_{\mathcal{S}}\equiv E\mathcal{S}=\{ES\mid S\in\mathcal{S}\}.(B.7)

Every Pauli error pattern inE​𝒮E\mathcal{S}not only has the same error syndrome, but cannot be distinguished by any physical measurement—including even the final results of a quantum computation.
The converse is also true, but requires a technicality involving the global phase.
As long asEEitself is an element of𝒫¯n\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}, thenEErepresents an equivalence class of Pauli strings that are identical up to global phase.
In that case, each elementE​SESof the stabilizer coset is also regarded as an element of the Pauli group modulo phases, and the entire collection of all possible stabilizer cosets is mathematically identical to the quotient space𝒫¯n/𝒮\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}/\mathcal{S}, where the Pauli group modulo phases𝒫¯n\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}is used, rather than the proper Pauli group𝒫n\mathcal{P}_{n}.
It is this quotient space,𝒫¯n/𝒮\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}/\mathcal{S}, which represents physically distinguishable Pauli errors on the code space.

The4n4^{n}Pauli operators (modulo global phase) therefore naturally split into2n−k2^{n-k}error cosets{Σm}\{\Sigma_{m}\}based on the2n−k2^{n-k}possible error syndromesm∈{±1}n−km\in\{\pm 1\}^{n-k}.
Each error cosetΣm\Sigma_{m}contains4n/2n−k=2n+k4^{n}/2^{n-k}=2^{n+k}distinct Pauli strings, which form stabilizer cosets containing|𝒮|=2n−k|\mathcal{S}|=2^{n-k}error patterns each.101010The fact that the size of each stabilizer coset matches the total number of error cosets is merely a mathematical coincidence at this level.Once a syndrome is fixed, the number of stabilizer cosets consistent with that syndrome is therefore2n+k/2n−k=4k2^{n+k}/2^{n-k}=4^{k}.
These stabilizer cosets are calledlogical equivalence classes.
They are physically distinguishable, not at the level of theencoding, but rather at the level ofnumerical resultsfor logical computations.

In summary, the4n4^{n}elements of the Pauli group modulo phases𝒫¯n\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}can be organized as4n=2n−k⏟syndromes×4k⏟logical classes×2n−k⏟equivalent errors.4^{n}=\underbrace{2^{n-k}}_{\text{syndromes}}\times\underbrace{4^{k}}_{\text{logical classes}}\times\underbrace{2^{n-k}}_{\text{equivalent errors}}.(B.8)

It is useful to compare this to the situation in the classical setting.
For a classical[n,k,d][n,k,d]code, there are2n−k2^{n-k}distinct error cosets (one per syndrome pattern), and each syndrome pattern is consistent with2k2^{k}different logical “classes.”
Each “class” in the classical setting contains only one error pattern, so that every error pattern is uniquely specified by its syndrome and logical class.
This can be summarized as follows:2n=2n−k⏟syndromes×2k⏟logical “classes”×1⏟no equivalence classically.2^{n}=\underbrace{2^{n-k}}_{\text{syndromes}}\times\underbrace{2^{k}}_{\text{logical ``classes"}}\times\underbrace{1}_{\text{no equivalence classically}}.(B.9)

## B.1.1Binary vector representation

Pauli errors, stabilizer cosets, and logical equivalence classes all admit a natural coordinate description in the setting of stabilizer codes.
Such a description is useful for specifying probability measures on stabilizer cosets, expressing syndrome decoding algorithms, and analyzing the performance of quantum error correction.

This coordinate description is based on the fact that the Pauli group (up to phase) itself has the structure of a vector space over𝔽2\mathbb{F}_{2}.
Specifically, ifttis a positive integer, then the multiplication on𝒫¯t\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{t}is group-isomorphic to the addition on𝔽22​t\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2t}.
Explicitly, ifP∈𝒫¯tP\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{t}is an arbitrary Pauli string, then we define itsbinary sympectic vector(andXXandZZcomponentsof the binary vector),(x1,…,xt,z1,…,zt)∈𝔽22​t,(x_{1},\dots,x_{t},z_{1},\dots,z_{t})\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2t},(B.10)

by selecting each pair(xi,zi)(x_{i},z_{i})based on theithi^{\mathrm{th}}tensor factor ofPP, via the correspondence provided inTable7.
The equivalence between multiplication on𝒫¯t\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{t}and addition on𝔽22​t\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2t}is then straightforward to check.
These coordinates are called the binary symplectic representation in the literature.Table 7:Identification of Pauli matrices with𝔽2\mathbb{F}_{2}-valued binary vector.Pauli MatrixBinary VectorII(0,0)(0,0)XX(1,0)(1,0)YY(1,1)(1,1)ZZ(0,1)(0,1)

For an⟦n,k,d⟧\llbracket n,k,d\rrbracketstabilizer code with stabilizer𝒮\mathcal{S}, one can conversely specify a Pauli errorE∈𝒫¯nE\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}by explicitly providing a list ofXXandZZcomponents for that error.
Importantly, however, Pauli errors with different coordinates can belong to the same stabilizer coset.
This is because the physically distinguishable errors are elements of the quotient space𝒫¯n/𝒮\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}/\mathcal{S},notsimply the Pauli group modulo phases𝒫¯n\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}.

It turns out that a very natural representation of the quotient space𝒫¯n/𝒮\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}/\mathcal{S}exists, thereby enabling easy access to defining and analyzing physically distinct errors.
This representation is based on the previously discussed structure of the Pauli group modulo phases, whose physically inequivalent elements can first be classified by their error syndrome, and then by their logical equivalence class.
The key point is that, after fixing the syndromem∈{±1}n−km\in\{\pm 1\}^{n-k}, the4k4^{k}different logical equivalence classes are in bijective correspondence with thelogicalPauli group modulo phases,𝒫¯k\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{k}.

The bijection is not unique, but it can be specified by two pieces of information: (i) a selection of Pauli operators{Em}⊆𝒫¯n\{E_{m}\}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}, one for each syndromem∈{±1}n−km\in\{\pm 1\}^{n-k}, such thatσ​(Em)=m\sigma(E_{m})=m, and (ii) a representation of logical operators within𝒫¯n\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}.
The latter is obtained by specifying physical representativesX¯i,Z¯i∈𝒫¯n\overline{X}_{i},\overline{Z}_{i}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}for the logical Pauli basis, withi∈{1,…,k}i\in\{1,\dots,k\}.
For(xL,zL)∈𝔽22​k(x_{\mathrm{L}},z_{\mathrm{L}})\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2k}, define the abstract logical PauliP​(xL,zL)≡∏j=1kXj(xL)j​Zj(zL)j∈𝒫¯k,P(x_{\mathrm{L}},z_{\mathrm{L}})\equiv\prod_{j=1}^{k}X_{j}^{(x_{\mathrm{L}})_{j}}Z_{j}^{(z_{\mathrm{L}})_{j}}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{k},(B.11)

and its chosen physical representativeP¯​(xL,zL)≡∏j=1kX¯j(xL)j​Z¯j(zL)j∈𝒫¯n.\overline{P}(x_{\mathrm{L}},z_{\mathrm{L}})\equiv\prod_{j=1}^{k}\overline{X}_{j}^{(x_{\mathrm{L}})_{j}}\overline{Z}_{j}^{(z_{\mathrm{L}})_{j}}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}.(B.12)

These two pieces of information together allow us to uniquely write every stabilizer coset[E]𝒮∈𝒫¯n/𝒮[E]_{\mathcal{S}}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}/\mathcal{S}in the form[E]𝒮=Em​P¯​(xL,zL)​𝒮,m=σ​([E]𝒮)∈{±1}n−k.[E]_{\mathcal{S}}=E_{m}\overline{P}(x_{\mathrm{L}},z_{\mathrm{L}})\mathcal{S},\qquad m=\sigma([E]_{\mathcal{S}})\in\{\pm 1\}^{n-k}.(B.13)

Here, we writeσ​([E]𝒮)\sigma([E]_{\mathcal{S}})to denote the syndrome of any representative ofE​𝒮E\mathcal{S}; this is well defined because all representatives in a stabilizer coset have the same syndrome.
We can then define a2n−k2^{n-k}-to-11mapπ:𝒫¯n/𝒮→𝒫¯k\pi:\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}/\mathcal{S}\to\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{k}byπ​(Em​P¯​(xL,zL)​𝒮)=P​(xL,zL).\pi\left(E_{m}\overline{P}(x_{\mathrm{L}},z_{\mathrm{L}})\mathcal{S}\right)=P(x_{\mathrm{L}},z_{\mathrm{L}}).(B.14)

It’s not difficult to see that this map is a bijection when restricted to stabilizer cosets having a fixed syndromem∈{±1}n−km\in\{\pm 1\}^{n-k}.

Therefore, the equivalence class of physical errors that anyE∈𝒫¯nE\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}belongs to is exactly specified bym=σ​(E)m=\sigma(E)and the abstract logical Pauliπ​(E​𝒮)=P​(xL,zL)\pi(E\mathcal{S})=P(x_{\mathrm{L}},z_{\mathrm{L}}).
It follows that this equivalence class can be uniquely represented by a set of syndrome and logical labels,(s1,…,sn−k,xL,1,…,xL,k,zL,1,…,zL,k)∈𝔽2n+k,\left(s_{1},\dots,s_{n-k},x_{\mathrm{L},1},\dots,x_{\mathrm{L},k},z_{\mathrm{L},1},\dots,z_{\mathrm{L},k}\right)\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n+k},(B.15)

where thes1,…,sn−k∈𝔽2s_{1},\dots,s_{n-k}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}are called thesyndrome vector, related to the syndromem=σ​(E)m=\sigma(E)bymi=(−1)sim_{i}=(-1)^{s_{i}}, and thexL,1,…,xL,k,zL,1,…,zL,k∈𝔽2x_{\mathrm{L},1},\dots,x_{\mathrm{L},k},z_{\mathrm{L},1},\dots,z_{\mathrm{L},k}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}are calledlogical labels, defined to be the binary symplectic representation ofπ​(E​𝒮)∈𝒫¯k\pi(E\mathcal{S})\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{k}.
It’s easy to see that the binary representations of two different Pauli stringsE,E′∈𝒫¯nE,E^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}are identical if and only ifEEandE′E^{\prime}are in the same equivalence class of physically distinguishable errors.
Thus we have a well-defined coordinatization of the quotient space𝒫¯n/𝒮\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}/\mathcal{S}, whose elements are exactly those equivalence classes.
This labeling with the basis choice is explained in[63,43]

## B.2General Syndrome Decoding

Here, we discuss general decoding strategies that apply to every classical and quantum code.
We begin by defining the general concept of a syndrome decoder for both the classical and quantum case, and then proceed to discuss the two main types of syndrome decoders that are relevant for this work:minimum-weight(MW) decoders andmaximum-likelihood(ML) decoders.

## B.2.1Classical and Quantum Decoders

Consider the usual setup for the classical case, whereC=[n,k,d]C=[n,k,d]is a classical code, andc∈Cc\in Cis a transmitted codeword.
A classical error channel applies an errore∈𝔽2ne\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}to produce the received wordc′=c+ec^{\prime}=c+e.
The question we now ask is how the syndromes≡σ​(c′)=σ​(e)∈𝔽2n−ks\equiv\sigma(c^{\prime})=\sigma(e)\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-k}can be used to reconstruct the erroree, and therefore also the original codewordcc.

This question is answered by asyndrome decoderforCC, which takes a syndromes∈𝔽2n−ks\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-k}as input, and returns a plausible error pattern from the error cosetΣs\Sigma_{s}, called thecandidate.
In mathematical terms, the syndrome decoder is a function𝒟:𝔽2n−k→𝔽2n\mathscr{D}:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-k}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}which satisfies the property that𝒟​(s)∈Σs\mathscr{D}(s)\in\Sigma_{s}(or equivalently, thatσ​(𝒟​(s))=s\sigma(\mathscr{D}(s))=s) for everys∈𝔽2n−ks\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-k}.
Once a candidatee′≡𝒟​(s)e^{\prime}\equiv\mathscr{D}(s)has been determined by the decoder, a “recovered word”rrcan be defined by adding this pattern to the received word,r=e′+c′r=e^{\prime}+c^{\prime}.
If the candidatee′e^{\prime}matches the actual erroreethat occurred, then the correct initial codeword is exactly recovered,r=e+c′=e+(e+c)=cr=e+c^{\prime}=e+(e+c)=c.
Conversely, ife′≠ee^{\prime}\neq e, thenr=e′+c′r=e^{\prime}+c^{\prime}will still be a codeword, butnotthe initial codeword transmitted.

Now consider the usual setup for the quantum case, where we have an⟦n,k,d⟧\llbracket n,k,d\rrbracketstabilizer code, with stabilizer group𝒮\mathcal{S}and code spaceℋC\mathcal{H}_{C}.
We subject this code to a quantum error channel, which we assume to be Pauli noise.
This noise applies a Pauli error stringE∈𝒫¯nE\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}, which modifies a transmitted code state|ψ⟩∈ℋC\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{H}_{C}to some received state|ψ′⟩≡E​|ψ⟩\ket{\psi^{\prime}}\equiv E\ket{\psi}.
By measuring the stabilizer generators on|ψ′⟩\ket{\psi^{\prime}}, we obtain measurement outcomesm≡σ​(E)m\equiv\sigma(E), which tell us the error syndrome,σ​(E)=m\sigma(E)=m.
The question we now ask is how the syndromem∈{±1}n−km\in\{\pm 1\}^{n-k}can be used to reconstruct the errorEE, and therefore also the original code state|ψ⟩\ket{\psi}.

The key starting point is the observation that, actually,EEcannotbe determined uniquely, because there are many equivalent Pauli errors that would have had the exact same effect asEEon the code state|ψ⟩\ket{\psi}.
Therefore, the candidate error returned by a syndrome decoder in the quantum setting should really correspond to astabilizer coset, rather than any specific Pauli error pattern.
In mathematical terms, we therefore define a stabilizer syndrome decoder as a function𝒟:{±1}n−k→𝒫¯n/𝒮\mathscr{D}:\{\pm 1\}^{n-k}\to\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}/\mathcal{S}, where𝒟​(m)\mathscr{D}(m)always returns a stabilizer coset[E]𝒮∈𝒫¯n/𝒮[E]_{\mathcal{S}}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}/\mathcal{S}such thatσ​([E]𝒮)=m\sigma([E]_{\mathcal{S}})=m.

Despite this observation that stabilizer syndrome decoders should return stabilizer cosets rather than specific error patterns, it is sometimes useful, as an abuse of notation, to directly write𝒟​(m)=E\mathscr{D}(m)=E, whereE∈𝒫¯nE\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}.
In such cases, the hidden assumption is that𝒟​(m)\mathscr{D}(m)actually returns the entire stabilizer cosetE​𝒮E\mathcal{S}, andEEjust serves as a representative shorthand for that coset.
This becomes relevant downstream (at the level of implementation on a quantum computer), because applying a recovery requires choosing a single representative recovery operator from the returned stabilizer coset so that it can be enacted with physical quantum gates.

When working with syndrome and logical coordinates, there is another useful way to describe syndrome decoders for stabilizer codes.
Recall that a stabilizer coset can be specified by the binary tuple inEq.B.15.
The job of a syndrome decoder is then to provide candidate logical coordinatesxL,zL∈𝔽2kx_{\mathrm{L}},z_{\mathrm{L}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}from the syndrome coordinatess∈𝔽2n−ks\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-k}.
Therefore, we may also write the coordinate shorthand𝒟​(s)=(xL,zL)\mathscr{D}(s)=(x_{\mathrm{L}},z_{\mathrm{L}})to mean that the decoder returns the stabilizer coset with labels(s,xL,zL)(s,x_{\mathrm{L}},z_{\mathrm{L}})on the input syndromem∈{±1}n−km\in\{\pm 1\}^{n-k}defined bymi=(−1)sim_{i}=(-1)^{s_{i}}.

## B.2.2Minimum-Weight Decoding

The representative-level idea underlying MW decoding, both in the classical and quantum setting, is to select an error pattern with the smallest possible weight among all patterns consistent with a given error syndrome.

For a classical codeC=[n,k,d]C=[n,k,d]with parity-check matrixHH, the MW decoder𝒟MW\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{MW}}is defined as𝒟MW​(s)∈arg​mine∈𝔽2nσ​(e)=s⁡wt(e).\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{MW}}(s)\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}\\
\sigma(e)=s\end{subarray}}\operatorname*{wt}(e).(B.16)

If there are ties for the error pattern with least weight, the decoder only needs to select one of them to qualify as an MW decoder.

For a quantum⟦n,k,d⟧\llbracket n,k,d\rrbracketcode with stabilizer𝒮\mathcal{S}, the MW decoder is defined asE^MW​(m)\displaystyle\widehat{E}^{\mathrm{MW}}(m)∈arg​minE∈𝒫¯nσ​(E)=m⁡wt(E),\displaystyle\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\begin{subarray}{c}E\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}\\
\sigma(E)=m\end{subarray}}\operatorname*{wt}(E),(B.17)𝒟MW​(m)\displaystyle\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{MW}}(m)≡E^MW​(m)​𝒮.\displaystyle\equiv\widehat{E}^{\mathrm{MW}}(m)\mathcal{S}.(B.18)

This is a representative-level MW rule: it minimizes the weight of an individual Pauli representative and returns its stabilizer coset, rather than optimizing a degeneracy-aware coset weight.
Similarly to the classical case, if there are ties for the Pauli error pattern with least weight, then the MW decoder can select any of them.

## B.2.3Maximum-Likelihood Decoding

While the MW decoder is a useful baseline, it is not always the best possible decoder available, in the sense of maximizing the probability of successful recovery.
In the presence of a specific error channel, the most ideal syndrome decoder is called themaximum-likelihood(ML) decoder.
For a classical code, the candidate returned by an ML decoder is the most likely error pattern consistent with the observed syndrome, and therefore depends strongly on the distribution of errors generated by the error channel.

For a classical[n,k,d][n,k,d]code with a prescribed parity-check matrix, if a syndromes∈𝔽2n−ks\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-k}is observed, then the ML decoder𝒟ML\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{ML}}returns the most likely error patterne∈𝔽2ne\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}with syndromeσ​(e)=s\sigma(e)=s.
In mathematical terms, if𝐞\mathbf{e}denotes the (random) bit-flip error drawn from the classical error channel, then𝒟ML​(s)∈arg​maxe∈𝔽2nσ​(e)=s⁡Pr𝐞∼P⁡[𝐞=e∣σ​(𝐞)=s],\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{ML}}(s)\in\operatorname*{arg\,max}_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}\\
\sigma(e)=s\end{subarray}}\Pr_{\mathbf{e}\sim P}\left[\mathbf{e}=e\mid\sigma(\mathbf{e})=s\right],(B.19)

wherePPdenotes the distribution of bit-flip errors generated by the error channel from which𝐞\mathbf{e}is drawn.
If there are ties for the most likely error pattern, the decoder is still classified as ML as long as it picks one of the equally likely maximum-likelihood options.

A useful special case of classical ML decoding is when the error channel is a binary symmetric channel (BSC) with physical error rate0≤p≤10\leq p\leq 1.
In this channel, the error pattern𝐞\mathbf{e}is generated by sampling every bit i.i.d. from the distributionBernoulli​(p)\mathrm{Bernoulli}(p)that assigns probabilityppto the outcome11and probability1−p1-pto the outcome0.
For0<p<10<p<1, ifs∈𝔽2n−ks\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-k}is a syndrome with positive probability, then a simple consequence of Bayes’ law is the relationPr⁡[𝐞=e∣σ​(𝐞)=s]=Pr⁡[𝐞=e]​δ​[σ​(e)=s]Pr⁡[σ​(𝐞)=s],\Pr\left[\mathbf{e}=e\mid\sigma(\mathbf{e})=s\right]=\frac{\Pr[\mathbf{e}=e]\delta[\sigma(e)=s]}{\Pr[\sigma(\mathbf{e})=s]},(B.20)

where theδ​[condition]\delta[\text{condition}]evaluates to the boolean value of the condition, i.e.,0if the condition is not satisfied, or11if the condition is satisfied.
Note that the numerator can be expressed asPr⁡[𝐞=e]​δ​[σ​(e)=s]\displaystyle\Pr[\mathbf{e}=e]\delta[\sigma(e)=s]=pwt(e)​(1−p)n−wt(e)​δ​[σ​(e)=s]\displaystyle=p^{\operatorname*{wt}(e)}(1-p)^{n-\operatorname*{wt}(e)}\delta[\sigma(e)=s](B.21)∝(p1−p)wt(e)​δ​[σ​(e)=s],\displaystyle\propto\left(\frac{p}{1-p}\right)^{\operatorname*{wt}(e)}\delta[\sigma(e)=s],(B.22)

and it provides the onlyee-dependence in (B.20).
Therefore, the ML decoder returns𝒟ML(s)∈arg​maxe∈𝔽2nσ​(e)=s(p1−p)wt(e).\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{ML}}(s)\in\operatorname*{arg\,max}_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}\\
\sigma(e)=s\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{p}{1-p}\right)^{\operatorname*{wt}(e)}.(B.23)

Ifp<1/2p<1/2, then this is a strictly decreasing function inwt(e)\operatorname*{wt}(e), and𝒟ML​(s)∈arg​mine∈𝔽2nσ​(e)=s⁡wt(e).\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{ML}}(s)\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}\\
\sigma(e)=s\end{subarray}}\operatorname*{wt}(e).(B.24)

This implies that classical ML decoding on the BSC with error ratep<1/2p<1/2is equivalent to classical MW decoding.

For a quantum⟦n,k,d⟧\llbracket n,k,d\rrbracketcode with stabilizer𝒮\mathcal{S}, the ML decoder must return the most likelystabilizer cosetof Pauli error patterns consistent with the observed syndrome.
Write𝐄\mathbf{E}for the random Pauli error generated by a given Pauli noise model, and writeσ​(𝐄)\sigma(\mathbf{E})for the stabilizer syndrome obtained by the random Paulis.
With these notations, we introduce the following probabilities:Pr⁡[E∣m]\displaystyle\Pr[E\mid m]≡Pr⁡[𝐄=E∣σ​(𝐄)=m],\displaystyle\equiv\Pr[\mathbf{E}=E\mid\sigma(\mathbf{E})=m]\,,(B.25)Pr⁡[[E]𝒮∣m]\displaystyle\Pr[[E]_{\mathcal{S}}\mid m]≡∑E′∈[E]𝒮Pr⁡[𝐄=E′∣σ​(𝐄)=m].\displaystyle\equiv\sum_{E^{\prime}\in[E]_{\mathcal{S}}}\Pr[\mathbf{E}=E^{\prime}\mid\sigma(\mathbf{E})=m]\,.(B.26)

Then the ML decoder returns𝒟ML​(m)∈arg​max[E]𝒮∈𝒫¯n/𝒮σ​(E)=m⁡Pr⁡[[E]𝒮∣m].\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{ML}}(m)\in\operatorname*{arg\,max}_{\begin{subarray}{c}[E]_{\mathcal{S}}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}/\mathcal{S}\\
\sigma(E)=m\end{subarray}}\Pr[[E]_{\mathcal{S}}\mid m]\,.(B.27)

That is, the decoder returns the stabilizer coset most likely to be hit by a random Pauli error generated by the noise distribution, among all stabilizer cosets with the correct syndrome.
Importantly, this coset doesnotnecessarily contain the most likely individual Pauli string consistent with the observed syndrome.
Additionally, if there are ties, the ML decoder must select one stabilizer coset among all those tied for the highest likelihood.
Therefore, as a shorthand, we may use the convention that equality “==” denotes the same thing as set membership “∈\in” forarg​max\operatorname*{arg\,max}andarg​min\operatorname*{arg\,min}.

Furthermore, using the fact that the coset[E]𝒮∈𝒫¯n/𝒮[E]_{\mathcal{S}}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}/\mathcal{S}withσ​(E)=m\sigma(E)=mcan be expressed asEm​P¯​(xL,zL)​𝒮E_{m}\overline{P}(x_{\rm L},z_{\rm L})\mathcal{S},
the probabilityPr⁡[[E]𝒮∣m]\Pr[[E]_{\mathcal{S}}\mid m](B.26) is specified by the syndrome and logical labels,Pr⁡[xL,zL∣s]\displaystyle\Pr[x_{\rm L},z_{\rm L}\mid s]≡Pr⁡[[Em​P¯​(xL,zL)]𝒮∣m]\displaystyle\equiv\Pr[[E_{m}\overline{P}(x_{\rm L},z_{\rm L})]_{\mathcal{S}}\mid m](B.28)=∑S∈𝒮Pr⁡[Em​P¯​(xL,zL)​S∣m],\displaystyle=\sum_{S\in\mathcal{S}}\Pr[E_{m}\overline{P}(x_{\rm L},z_{\rm L})S\mid m]\,,(B.29)

wheremi=(−1)sim_{i}=(-1)^{s_{i}}.
Then the ML decoding can be written using the logical label as(xL∗,zL∗)=arg​maxxL,zL∈𝔽2k⁡Pr⁡[xL,zL∣s].\left(x_{\mathrm{L}}^{*},z_{\mathrm{L}}^{*}\right)=\operatorname*{arg\,max}_{x_{\mathrm{L}},z_{\mathrm{L}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}}\Pr[x_{\rm L},z_{\rm L}\mid s]\,.(B.30)

## B.2.4Marginalized ML Decoding

In a memory experiment, one may use the eigenstate of theZ¯\overline{Z}(X¯\overline{X}) operator|0⟩L\ket{0}_{\mathrm{L}}(|+⟩L\ket{+}_{\mathrm{L}}) as an initial state, and observe if the QEC protocol keeps the initial information.
We call it aZZmemory (XXmemory) experiment/task.
For these specific tasks, only theXX(ZZ) components of a logical label are required, and thus the other components can be marginalized.
More specifically, instead of usingjointML decoding (B.30), it suffices to usemarginalizedML decoding, defined byxL∗=arg​maxxL∈𝔽2k​∑zL∈𝔽2kPr⁡[xL,zL∣s],\displaystyle x_{\mathrm{L}}^{*}=\operatorname*{arg\,max}_{x_{\mathrm{L}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}}\sum_{z_{\mathrm{L}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}}\Pr\left[x_{\mathrm{L}},z_{\mathrm{L}}\mid s\right]\,,(B.31)

for theZZmemory task, andzL∗=arg​maxzL∈𝔽2k​∑xL∈𝔽2kPr⁡[xL,zL∣s],\displaystyle z_{\mathrm{L}}^{*}=\operatorname*{arg\,max}_{z_{\mathrm{L}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}}\sum_{x_{\mathrm{L}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}}\Pr\left[x_{\mathrm{L}},z_{\mathrm{L}}\mid s\right]\,,(B.32)

for theXXmemory task.

## B.3Maximum-Likelihood Decoding for Concatenated Codes

Maximum-likelihood decoding for the concatenated code means the ordinary joint logical-class optimization defined above.
Although evaluating this posterior is hard in general, Ref.[63]showed that concatenation permits an efficient message-passing evaluation of the same posterior.
We will focus on the special case used in the main text, wherekin=1k_{\mathrm{in}}=1so thatCout∘Cin=⟦n,k,d⟧C_{\mathrm{out}}\circ C_{\mathrm{in}}=\llbracket n,k,d\rrbracketwithn=nout​ninn=n_{\mathrm{out}}n_{\mathrm{in}},k=koutk=k_{\mathrm{out}}, andd=min⁡(dout,din)d=\min(d_{\mathrm{out}},d_{\mathrm{in}}).
To keep the discussion as simple as possible, we will assume the case of i.i.d. single-qubit Pauli noise, since that is all we need for the main text.

First, we will introduce the binary vector representation for concatenated codes.
Namely, the concatenated structureCout∘CinC_{\mathrm{out}}\circ C_{\mathrm{in}}admits a hierarchical understanding of how theXXandZZcomponents of a Pauli errorE∈𝒫nE\in\mathcal{P}_{n}relate to the syndrome vector and logical labels, defined inSectionB.1.1.

## B.3.1Binary vector representation with concatenated structure

LetE∈𝒫¯nE\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}be an arbitrary Pauli error, and write(x,z)∈𝔽22​n(x,z)\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2n}for the corresponding binary vector ofEE, wherex,z∈𝔽2nx,z\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}respectively denote theXXandZZcomponents.
Letxin(b),zin(b)∈𝔽2ninx_{\mathrm{in}}^{(b)},z_{\mathrm{in}}^{(b)}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n_{\mathrm{in}}}respectively denote the segments of theXXandZZcomponents supported on thebthb^{\mathrm{th}}block, where1≤b≤nout1\leq b\leq n_{\mathrm{out}}.
Recall that the errorEEcan be specified by a syndrome vector and logical labels as(s,xL,zL)(s,x_{\mathrm{L}},z_{\mathrm{L}}), where the bit strings∈𝔽2n−ks\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-k}corresponds to the syndrome vector, andxL,zL∈𝔽2kx_{\mathrm{L}},z_{\mathrm{L}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}respectively correspond to theXXandZZcomponents of the label of the logical equivalence class.

For the syndrome vector, writes=(sout,sin(1),…,sin(nout)),s=\left(s_{\mathrm{out}},s_{\mathrm{in}}^{(1)},\dots,s_{\mathrm{in}}^{(n_{\mathrm{out}})}\right),(B.33)

wheresout∈𝔽2nout−kouts_{\mathrm{out}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n_{\mathrm{out}}-k_{\mathrm{out}}}corresponds to the outer syndrome, and eachsin(b)∈𝔽2nin−kins_{\mathrm{in}}^{(b)}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n_{\mathrm{in}}-k_{\mathrm{in}}}corresponds to the inner syndrome on thebthb^{\mathrm{th}}block with1≤b≤nout1\leq b\leq n_{\mathrm{out}}.
The inner syndrome vector can then be understood assin(b)=Hin​zin(b),s_{\mathrm{in}}^{(b)}=H_{\mathrm{in}}z_{\mathrm{in}}^{(b)},(B.34)

whereHinH_{\mathrm{in}}is the parity-check matrix forCinC_{\mathrm{in}}.
The outer syndrome can be similarly understood assout=(Hout⊗[1nin])​x,s_{\mathrm{out}}=\left(H_{\mathrm{out}}\otimes[1^{n_{\mathrm{in}}}]\right)x,(B.35)

whereHout⊗[1nin]H_{\mathrm{out}}\otimes[1^{n_{\mathrm{in}}}]is the Kronecker product ofHoutH_{\mathrm{out}}and[1nin][1^{n_{\mathrm{in}}}].
Here,HoutH_{\mathrm{out}}denotes the parity-check matrix forCoutC_{\mathrm{out}}, and[1nin][1^{n_{\mathrm{in}}}]denotes the1×nin1\times n_{\mathrm{in}}binary matrix containing only11s.111111Technically, this assumes that the inner codeCinC_{\mathrm{in}}is a repetition code, but we can assume this without loss of generality because every classical[nin,kin=1,din][n_{\mathrm{in}},k_{\mathrm{in}}=1,d_{\mathrm{in}}]code is equivalent to a repetition code.

For thexLx_{\mathrm{L}}andzLz_{\mathrm{L}}coordinates ofEE, recall that we must first choose a representation of𝒫¯k\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{k}within𝒫¯n\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}, along with a collection of syndrome representativesEm∈𝒫¯nE_{m}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}withσ​(Em)=m\sigma(E_{m})=mfor every syndromem∈{±1}n−km\in\{\pm 1\}^{n-k}.
These specifics cannot be provided explicitly without knowledge of the specific codesCinC_{\mathrm{in}}andCoutC_{\mathrm{out}}.

However, some specifics relevant to our discussion can be determined from the following observation: the concatenated codeCout∘CinC_{\mathrm{out}}\circ C_{\mathrm{in}}has the special property that every stabilizer generator is either a purelyXX-type or purelyZZ-type Pauli string.
Stabilizer codes with this defining property are calledCSS codes[13,76], and an important fact about CSS codes is that logical Pauli operators can be chosen so that every logicalXX(resp. logicalZZ) operator is represented by a purelyXX-type (resp.ZZ-type) physical operator.

A mathematical consequence of the CSS nature of the code, together with the known concatenated structure, is the guaranteed existence of functionsπinX:𝔽2nin→𝔽2kin,\displaystyle\pi^{X}_{\mathrm{in}}:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n_{\mathrm{in}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k_{\mathrm{in}}},(B.36)πinZ:𝔽2nin→𝔽2kin,\displaystyle\pi^{Z}_{\mathrm{in}}:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n_{\mathrm{in}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k_{\mathrm{in}}},(B.37)πoutX:𝔽2nout→𝔽2kout,\displaystyle\pi^{X}_{\mathrm{out}}:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n_{\mathrm{out}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k_{\mathrm{out}}},(B.38)πoutZ:𝔽2nout→𝔽2kout,\displaystyle\pi^{Z}_{\mathrm{out}}:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n_{\mathrm{out}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k_{\mathrm{out}}},(B.39)

such that ifEEhas Pauli coordinatesx1,…,xnx_{1},\dots,x_{n}andz1,…,znz_{1},\dots,z_{n}, then itsxLx_{\mathrm{L}}andzLz_{\mathrm{L}}coordinates can be reconstructed asxL\displaystyle x_{\mathrm{L}}=πoutX​(πinX​(xin(1)),…,πinX​(xin(nout))),\displaystyle=\pi^{X}_{\mathrm{out}}\left(\pi^{X}_{\mathrm{in}}\left(x^{(1)}_{\mathrm{in}}\right),\dots,\pi^{X}_{\mathrm{in}}\left(x^{(n_{\mathrm{out}})}_{\mathrm{in}}\right)\right),(B.40)zL\displaystyle z_{\mathrm{L}}=πoutZ​(πinZ​(zin(1)),…,πinZ​(zin(nout))),\displaystyle=\pi^{Z}_{\mathrm{out}}\left(\pi^{Z}_{\mathrm{in}}\left(z^{(1)}_{\mathrm{in}}\right),\dots,\pi^{Z}_{\mathrm{in}}\left(z^{(n_{\mathrm{out}})}_{\mathrm{in}}\right)\right),(B.41)

where againxin(b)x^{(b)}_{\mathrm{in}}andzin(b)z^{(b)}_{\mathrm{in}}respectively denotebthb^{\mathrm{th}}block ofxxandzz.

While the choice of functions (B.36) is not unique, they have a useful interpretation in the context of coordinatizing the logical class for a Pauli error, which can be made precise when specific codesCinC_{\mathrm{in}}andCoutC_{\mathrm{out}}are given.
First, separate an arbitrary Pauli errorE∈𝒫nE\in\mathcal{P}_{n}into independent actions on each inner block of qubits.
The action on thebthb^{\mathrm{th}}block has labelsxin(b)x^{(b)}_{\mathrm{in}}andzin(b)z^{(b)}_{\mathrm{in}}.
Next, for1≤b≤nout1\leq b\leq n_{\mathrm{out}}, we define(xout,zout)(x_{\mathrm{out}},z_{\mathrm{out}})with their elements given as(xout)b≡πinX​(xin(b))(x_{\mathrm{out}})_{b}\equiv\pi^{X}_{\mathrm{in}}(x^{(b)}_{\mathrm{in}})and(zout)b≡πinZ​(zin(b))(z_{\mathrm{out}})_{b}\equiv\pi^{Z}_{\mathrm{in}}(z^{(b)}_{\mathrm{in}})respectively.
Then, one can interpret(xout,zout)(x_{\mathrm{out}},z_{\mathrm{out}})as theXXandZZcomponents of the “inner” logical equivalence class for the piece ofEEsupported on thebthb^{\mathrm{th}}inner block.
Normally, this would requireπinX\pi^{X}_{\mathrm{in}}andπinZ\pi^{Z}_{\mathrm{in}}to both depend on the full binary Pauli vector for the portion ofEEsupported on thebthb^{\mathrm{th}}block,(xin(b),zin(b))(x^{(b)}_{\mathrm{in}},z^{(b)}_{\mathrm{in}}); the existence of an interpretation whereπinX\pi^{X}_{\mathrm{in}}only depends on theXXcomponents andπinZ\pi^{Z}_{\mathrm{in}}only depends on theZZcomponents is a direct consequence of the fact that our inner stabilizer generators are purelyXX-type.
Now, theXXandZZcomponents on each inner block can themselves be viewed as the binary vector for a single-qubit Pauli operator (sincekin=1k_{\mathrm{in}}=1) associated with that block.
By concatenating these binary vectors over allnoutn_{\mathrm{out}}blocks, we obtain an effective binary vector(xout,zout)∈𝔽22​nout(x_{\mathrm{out}},z_{\mathrm{out}})\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2n_{\mathrm{out}}}for a Pauli string onnoutn_{\mathrm{out}}qubits.
By construction, (B.35) can be re-expressed assout=Hout​xouts_{\mathrm{out}}=H_{\mathrm{out}}x_{\mathrm{out}}.
Therefore, similarly to the inner case, we can interpretπoutX​(xout)∈𝔽2kout\pi^{X}_{\mathrm{out}}(x_{\mathrm{out}})\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k_{\mathrm{out}}}andπoutZ​(zout)∈𝔽2kout\pi^{Z}_{\mathrm{out}}(z_{\mathrm{out}})\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k_{\mathrm{out}}}respectively as theXXandZZcomponents of an “outer” logical class corresponding to the Pauli stringE^∈𝒫¯nout\hat{E}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n_{\mathrm{out}}}whose binary representation is(xout,zout)(x_{\mathrm{out}},z_{\mathrm{out}}).
As before, this interpretation is possible because the outer stabilizer generators are purelyZZ-type.
Finally, by recognizing that the overallXXandZZcomponents for the logical class ofEEarexL,zL∈𝔽2kx_{\mathrm{L}},z_{\mathrm{L}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}andk=koutk=k_{\mathrm{out}}, it follows that the outer logical classes are in bijective correspondence with theactuallogical classes for the concatenated code.
The simplest way to associate them is by simply equating theXXandZZcomponents together, i.e.,xL\displaystyle x_{\mathrm{L}}≡πoutX​(xout),\displaystyle\equiv\pi^{X}_{\mathrm{out}}(x_{\mathrm{out}}),(B.42)zL\displaystyle z_{\mathrm{L}}≡πoutZ​(zout).\displaystyle\equiv\pi^{Z}_{\mathrm{out}}(z_{\mathrm{out}}).(B.43)

When taken together with the syndrome vectors=(sout,sin(1),…,sin(nout))s=(s_{\mathrm{out}},s^{(1)}_{\mathrm{in}},\dots,s^{(n_{\mathrm{out}})}_{\mathrm{in}}), this concludes the construction of the syndrome and logical labels ofEE.

When the classical codesCinC_{\mathrm{in}}andCoutC_{\mathrm{out}}are specified, this “hierarchical coordinatization” of physically distinguishable errors as described above can be made precise by providing all of the following information:
- •

The parity-check matrices,HinH_{\mathrm{in}}andHoutH_{\mathrm{out}};
- •

The coordinatization of inner logical equivalence classes byπinX\pi^{X}_{\mathrm{in}}andπinZ\pi^{Z}_{\mathrm{in}};
- •

The coordinatization of outer logical equivalence classes byπoutX\pi^{X}_{\mathrm{out}}andπoutZ\pi^{Z}_{\mathrm{out}}.

Importantly, there is a compatibility requirement between these maps—namely, we need to make sure that the resultingxLx_{\mathrm{L}}andzLz_{\mathrm{L}}do indeed attribute a unique list of coordinates to each logical class (this will not be satisfied by any arbitrary set of maps).

Note that by providing the coordinate maps, we sidestep the need to provide any syndrome representatives{Em}\{E_{m}\}, since the conclusion of our preceding discussion is that the inner and outer maps are themselves sufficient to determine the logical class of every Pauli operator.
Therefore, the{Em}\{E_{m}\}are only needed when it comes time to actually implement quantum gates, since a representative of the equivalence class must be chosen at that point.

## B.3.2Decoding Strategy

Having introduced the binary coordinates for concatenated codes, we can now describe maximum-likelihood decoding for the concatenated code.

The starting point, as introduced in the case of ML decoding, is a random stabilizer coset, which takes values in the quotient space𝒫¯n/𝒮\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}/\mathcal{S}and is induced by a random physical Pauli error.
This random stabilizer coset carries a random syndrome vector and logical labels,(𝐬out,𝐬in(1),…,𝐬in(nout),𝐱L,1,…,𝐱L,k,𝐳L,1,…,𝐳L,k).(\mathbf{s}_{\mathrm{out}},\mathbf{s}_{\mathrm{in}}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{s}_{\mathrm{in}}^{(n_{\mathrm{out}})},\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{L},1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{L},k},\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{L},1},\dots,\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{L},k}).(B.44)

In our setting, the measure on these random syndromes and logical labels is induced by a Pauli noise channel, which generates random Pauli errors𝐄\mathbf{E}taking values in𝒫¯n\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}, with the corresponding random binary symplectic vector,(𝐱1,…,𝐱n,𝐳1,…,𝐳n).(\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{n},\mathbf{z}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{z}_{n}).(B.45)

As a useful notation, we write𝐱in(b)\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}}^{(b)}and𝐳in(b)\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{in}}^{(b)}for thebthb^{\mathrm{th}}block of the random variables𝐱\mathbf{x}and𝐳\mathbf{z}, respectively.

The goal of any syndrome decoder is to use syndrome data to produce a candidate stabilizer coset with that same syndrome.
Therefore, at the level of syndrome and logical labels, it suffices for a decoder to compute candidatexLx_{\mathrm{L}}andzLz_{\mathrm{L}}coordinates, given just the syndrome vectorss.

The ordinary joint logical-class ML decoder achieves this by maximizing the conditional probability distribution (B.30).
The likelihood function can be expanded asPr⁡[xL,zL∣s]\displaystyle\Pr\left[x_{\mathrm{L}},z_{\mathrm{L}}\mid s\right]=1Pr⁡[𝐬out=sout∣sin(1),…,sin(nout)]\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Pr[\mathbf{s}_{\mathrm{out}}=s_{\mathrm{out}}\mid s_{\mathrm{in}}^{(1)},\dots,s_{\mathrm{in}}^{(n_{\mathrm{out}})}]}×∑xout,zout∈𝔽2noutδ[xL=πoutX(xout)]δ[zL=πoutZ(zout)]δ[sout=Houtxout]\displaystyle\times\sum_{x_{\mathrm{out}},z_{\mathrm{out}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n_{\mathrm{out}}}}\delta[x_{\mathrm{L}}=\pi^{X}_{\mathrm{out}}(x_{\mathrm{out}})]\delta[z_{\mathrm{L}}=\pi^{Z}_{\mathrm{out}}(z_{\mathrm{out}})]\delta[s_{\mathrm{out}}=H_{\mathrm{out}}x_{\mathrm{out}}]×∏b=1noutPr[(𝐱out)b=(xout)b∧(𝐳out)b=(zout)b∣sin(b)],\displaystyle\times\prod_{b=1}^{n_{\mathrm{out}}}\Pr[(\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{out}})_{b}=(x_{\mathrm{out}})_{b}\wedge(\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{out}})_{b}=(z_{\mathrm{out}})_{b}\mid s_{\mathrm{in}}^{(b)}],(B.46)

where we defined random variables(𝐱out)b\displaystyle(\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{out}})_{b}≡πinX​(𝐱in(b)),\displaystyle\equiv\pi^{X}_{\mathrm{in}}\left(\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{in}}^{(b)}\right),(B.47)(𝐳out)b\displaystyle(\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{out}})_{b}≡πinZ​(𝐳in(b)).\displaystyle\equiv\pi^{Z}_{\mathrm{in}}\left(\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{in}}^{(b)}\right).(B.48)

The denominator is just a normalization factor that doesn’t depend onxLx_{\mathrm{L}}orzLz_{\mathrm{L}}, so it suffices to optimize the numerator for joint logical-class ML decoding.
This expansion uses the concatenated structure but remains anexactevaluation of the posterior in (B.30): the inner blocks supply the posterior factors, while the outer sum imposessout=Hout​xouts_{\mathrm{out}}=H_{\mathrm{out}}x_{\mathrm{out}}and the logical label constraints. This efficient evaluation using the concatenated structure was introduced in[63].

For memory-protection tasks, the relevant objective is instead task-specific marginal ML decoding.
TheZZmemory task retainsxLx_{\mathrm{L}}and sums overzLz_{\mathrm{L}}, whereas theXXmemory task retainszLz_{\mathrm{L}}and sums overxLx_{\mathrm{L}}, as explained inSectionB.2.4.
Note that this choice of marginal is determined by the task, not by the concatenated structure.

For the first case, let us imagine that we marginalize overzLz_{\mathrm{L}}.
By using (B.3.2), the marginal can be reduced to the formPr⁡[xL∣s]\displaystyle\Pr\left[x_{\mathrm{L}}\mid s\right]=∑zL∈𝔽2koutPr⁡[xL,zL∣s]\displaystyle=\sum_{z_{\mathrm{L}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k_{\mathrm{out}}}}\Pr\left[x_{\mathrm{L}},z_{\mathrm{L}}\mid s\right](B.49)∝∑zL∈𝔽2kout∑xout,zout∈𝔽2noutδ​[xL=πoutX​(xout)]​δ​[zL=πoutZ​(zout)]×δ​[sout=Hout​xout]\displaystyle\propto\sum_{z_{\mathrm{L}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k_{\mathrm{out}}}}\sum_{x_{\mathrm{out}},z_{\mathrm{out}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n_{\mathrm{out}}}}\delta[x_{\mathrm{L}}=\pi^{X}_{\mathrm{out}}(x_{\mathrm{out}})]\delta[z_{\mathrm{L}}=\pi^{Z}_{\mathrm{out}}(z_{\mathrm{out}})]\times\delta[s_{\mathrm{out}}=H_{\mathrm{out}}x_{\mathrm{out}}](B.50)×∏b=1noutPr[(𝐱out)b=(xout)b∧(𝐳out)b=(zout)b∣sin(b)]\displaystyle\times\prod_{b=1}^{n_{\mathrm{out}}}\Pr[(\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{out}})_{b}=(x_{\mathrm{out}})_{b}\wedge(\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{out}})_{b}=(z_{\mathrm{out}})_{b}\mid s_{\mathrm{in}}^{(b)}](B.51)=∑xout∈𝔽2noutsout=Hout​xoutxL=πoutX​(xout)∏b=1nout∑(zout)b∈𝔽2Pr⁡[(𝐱out)b=(xout)b∧(𝐳out)b=(zout)b∣sin(b)]\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{\mathrm{out}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n_{\mathrm{out}}}\\
s_{\mathrm{out}}=H_{\mathrm{out}}x_{\mathrm{out}}\\
x_{\mathrm{L}}=\pi^{X}_{\mathrm{out}}(x_{\mathrm{out}})\end{subarray}}\prod_{b=1}^{n_{\mathrm{out}}}\sum_{(z_{\mathrm{out}})_{b}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}}\Pr[(\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{out}})_{b}=(x_{\mathrm{out}})_{b}\wedge(\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{out}})_{b}=(z_{\mathrm{out}})_{b}\mid s_{\mathrm{in}}^{(b)}](B.52)=∑xout∈𝔽2noutsout=Hout​xoutxL=πoutX​(xout)∏b=1noutPr⁡[(𝐱out)b=(xout)b∣sin(b)].\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{\mathrm{out}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n_{\mathrm{out}}}\\
s_{\mathrm{out}}=H_{\mathrm{out}}x_{\mathrm{out}}\\
x_{\mathrm{L}}=\pi^{X}_{\mathrm{out}}(x_{\mathrm{out}})\end{subarray}}\prod_{b=1}^{n_{\mathrm{out}}}\Pr[(\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{out}})_{b}=(x_{\mathrm{out}})_{b}\mid s_{\mathrm{in}}^{(b)}].(B.53)

This turns out to be simple enough that it can often be computed explicitly for i.i.d. single-qubit Pauli noise.
TheXXcoordinate returned by task-specific marginal ML decoding forZZmemory is then121212TheZZcoordinate is irrelevant for this task. If a full logical label is desired, it may be supplied by the optional auxiliary completionzL∗\displaystyle z_{\mathrm{L}}^{*}≡πoutZ​(zout∗),\displaystyle\equiv\pi^{Z}_{\mathrm{out}}\left(z_{\mathrm{out}}^{*}\right),(B.54)zout∗\displaystyle z_{\mathrm{out}}^{*}∈arg​maxzout∈𝔽2nout​∏b=1noutPr⁡[𝐳out,b=zout,b∣sin(b)].\displaystyle\in\operatorname*{arg\,max}_{z_{\mathrm{out}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n_{\mathrm{out}}}}\prod_{b=1}^{n_{\mathrm{out}}}\Pr\left[\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{out},b}=z_{\mathrm{out},b}\mid s_{\mathrm{in}}^{(b)}\right].(B.55)For uncorrelatedX/ZX/Znoise, combining this hard decision of inner code with the syndrome-constrained outer decision above yields a solution of the joint logical-class ML problem (B.30).xL∗∈arg​maxxL∈𝔽2kout⁡Pr⁡[xL∣s].x_{\mathrm{L}}^{*}\in\operatorname*{arg\,max}_{x_{\mathrm{L}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k_{\mathrm{out}}}}\Pr\left[x_{\mathrm{L}}\mid s\right].(B.56)

Now we come to the alternative task, where marginalization overxLx_{\mathrm{L}}is performed instead.
The marginal distribution forzLz_{\mathrm{L}}is then given byPr⁡[zL∣s]\displaystyle\Pr\left[z_{\mathrm{L}}\mid s\right]=∑xL∈𝔽2koutPr⁡[xL,zL∣s]\displaystyle=\sum_{x_{\mathrm{L}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k_{\mathrm{out}}}}\Pr\left[x_{\mathrm{L}},z_{\mathrm{L}}\mid s\right](B.57)∝∑xL∈𝔽2kout∑xout,zout∈𝔽2noutδ​[xL=πoutX​(xout)]​δ​[zL=πoutZ​(zout)]×δ​[sout=Hout​xout]\displaystyle\propto\sum_{x_{\mathrm{L}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k_{\mathrm{out}}}}\sum_{x_{\mathrm{out}},z_{\mathrm{out}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n_{\mathrm{out}}}}\delta[x_{\mathrm{L}}=\pi^{X}_{\mathrm{out}}(x_{\mathrm{out}})]\delta[z_{\mathrm{L}}=\pi^{Z}_{\mathrm{out}}(z_{\mathrm{out}})]\times\delta[s_{\mathrm{out}}=H_{\mathrm{out}}x_{\mathrm{out}}](B.58)×∏b=1noutPr[(𝐱out)b=(xout)b∧(𝐳out)b=(zout)b∣sin(b)]\displaystyle\times\prod_{b=1}^{n_{\mathrm{out}}}\Pr[(\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{out}})_{b}=(x_{\mathrm{out}})_{b}\wedge(\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{out}})_{b}=(z_{\mathrm{out}})_{b}\mid s_{\mathrm{in}}^{(b)}](B.59)=∑xout,zout∈𝔽2noutsout=Hout​xoutzL=πoutZ​(zout)∏b=1noutPr⁡[(𝐱out)b=(xout)b∧(𝐳out)b=(zout)b∣sin(b)]\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{\mathrm{out}},z_{\mathrm{out}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n_{\mathrm{out}}}\\
s_{\mathrm{out}}=H_{\mathrm{out}}x_{\mathrm{out}}\\
